    Smoothed exact global optimization beyond convexity



                                            Abstract
    We study exact global optimization of nonconvex quadratic and fixed-degree polynomial
problems with continuous and integer variables after one linear perturbation from a finite rational
law computed before sampling. The algorithms return an exact global optimizer on every draw
and never resample. Their expected bit work is polynomial in the input length and explicit
curvature, width, and noise ratios for fixed structural parameters. For quadratic programs
with few negative directions, aligned noise for continuous problems and Gaussian-like noise for
mixed problems give FPT bounds jointly in negative inertia, integer dimension where applicable,
and numerical ratios; uniform ambient noise gives a dimension-dependent exponent. Sparse
polynomial interactions admit polynomial bounds for each fixed treewidth, including graph,
simplex, and order constraints. A small core with tractable recourse gives FPT bounds jointly in
core size and numerical ratios. Core-only noise suffices for uniformly strongly convex continuous
recourse and structured convex integer recourse, with distinct sampling-precision bounds. Strong
noise gives a separate component algorithm without a width parameter. The proofs combine
curvature-corrected refinement, noise-dependent counting, and exact certificates; most routes
use a rare same-draw fallback, while lattice and component methods finish directly. We prove
limits of the sparse retention rule, a hardness implication for arbitrary sparse affine constraints,
and arithmetic implications for convex quartic residual point evaluation under core-only noise.
Sampled solutions give certified original-objective approximations with the numerical factors
retained. Practical performance is unestablished.

                                                   4
1     Introduction
Global optimization asks for a provably optimal solution of a nonconvex problem, not a locally
optimal or approximately optimal one. For quadratic and polynomial objectives with continuous and
integer variables this is hard in the worst case, even under strong structural restrictions. Minimizing
a quadratic function with a single negative eigenvalue is NP-hard [34], and box-constrained quadratic
programming remains strongly NP-hard when its interaction graph has treewidth two [17]. An exact
method must also handle degenerate configurations: ties between distant solutions, flat optimal
faces, and optimal values very close to a decision threshold. The analysis below treats each of
them explicitly. Spatial branch-and-bound solvers handle many instances well, but they have no
polynomial guarantee.
    Smoothed analysis [42] measures the cost of an algorithm on a worst-case instance after a small
random perturbation of its data. For discrete optimization with a linear objective, polynomial
smoothed complexity is closely tied to pseudo-polynomial solvability [8, 38]. For continuous
problems, a linear tilt removes degeneracy qualitatively: for semialgebraic problems under a
constraint qualification, generic tilts give a unique minimizer with quadratic growth [31]. Such
statements give no probability bounds, and an exact algorithm in the bit model needs more. It
needs quantitative probability bounds, a finite rational law that a Turing machine can sample, and
an exact way to finish the draws that remain degenerate, which under a finite law have positive
probability.
    This paper studies exact global optimization under one random linear perturbation. A rational
base instance with objective F0 and feasible set X is given. A vector γ is drawn once from a finite
rational law that is computed from the base instance before sampling, and the task is an exact
global minimizer of the sampled objective

                                   Fγ (x) = F0 (x) + γ T x   on X.

We ask for algorithms that are correct on every draw, never resample, and have expected bit
complexity polynomial in the input length for fixed structural parameters, with explicit dependence
on numerical ratios between curvature, widths, and the noise scale σ. Section 2 makes this model
precise.

1.1   Main results
The results follow four structural routes. They use different structure, and partly different noise
models. Table 1 lists the main theorems.
    Few negative curvature directions (section 4). For a rational quadratic program on a bounded
polytope whose Hessian has k negative eigenvalues, the nonconvexity lies in a k-dimensional factor.
The algorithm searches k auxiliary coordinates that carry the negative curvature and solves a convex
quadratic program at each query. Under an independent Gaussian-like finite law on every original
coefficient, the expected bit work is f (nz , k, 1 + ν diam(P)/σ) poly(I) with an absolute exponent,
also for bounded mixed-integer programs with nz integer variables; here ν is the largest negative
curvature and P the polyhedral relaxation (theorem 4.19). Noise aligned with the negative directions
gives a similar fixed-parameter bound for continuous problems (theorem 4.21). Under independent
uniform noise on every coefficient the bound is polynomial for each fixed k but contains a power of
the dimension that grows with k (theorem 4.20), and theorem 9.4 shows that the counted quantities
really depend on the dimension. For k ≤ 2 a different argument gives a bound linear in a numerical
ratio (theorem 4.9). Convex piecewise-quadratic unaries with rational breakpoints plus a concave
term with k supplied factor rows allow arbitrarily many integer variables (theorems 4.29 and 4.31);

                                                   5
on integer boxes with separable convex polynomial terms, a lattice of attainable objective values
gives exactness without any fallback (theorem 8.17).
    Sparse interactions (sections 5 and 6). For a fixed-degree polynomial on a mixed box whose
monomials fit the bags of a supplied tree decomposition with at most p coordinates per bag, the
expected bit work is
                                     h     (1 + n/2)Lwmax ip
                                  C0p 4 +                     polyd (I)
                                                  2σ
under independent uniform noise on all coordinates, where L bounds the coordinate second derivatives
and wmax the coordinate widths (theorem 5.2). The integer dimension and the negative inertia are
unrestricted, and no growth or nondegeneracy assumption is made. The bound is polynomial for each
fixed p, but it is not fixed-parameter in p, and theorem 9.1 shows that its factor nΩ(p) is necessary
for the retention rule used. The method extends to equality constraints given by explicit polynomial
maps or monotone implicit equations, to affine state recurrences with polynomial actuators, to
products of simplices, and to order constraints xi ≤ xj with binary variables (theorems 6.4, 6.11,
6.15 and 6.17). For arbitrary sparse affine constraints no such result holds unless NP ⊆ ZPP
(theorem 9.5).
    A small core with tractable recourse (sections 7 and 8). Suppose that fixing k continuous core
coordinates leaves a residual problem that an oracle solves exactly, or with certified error, on
every residual subbox: a convex quadratic or polynomial problem, a quadratic program whose
residual interaction graph is a forest, or a native-integer problem with an exact oracle under bound
tightening. Then the expected bit work of the core search is at most 8k [3 + (1 + k)L/(2σ)]k times
a polynomial in the input length that includes the oracle cost, where L bounds the curvature of
the core coordinates only; integer recourse adds an exact solve of a small polynomial problem of
cost ckd times a polynomial (theorems 7.9, 7.13 and 8.4). The number of residual variables, their
coupling, and their curvature enter only through the input length. Under noise on all coordinates,
residual convexity suffices. Under noise on the core alone, our continuous point theorem uses a
certified uniform strong-convexity modulus of continuous recourse (theorem 7.15), or, for separable
convex integer flows and totally unimodular recourse, a certificate that describes all tied optimal
flows at once (theorems 8.11, 8.12 and 8.14). These are sufficient conditions. The companion results
described below also provide point oracles for residual-convex cubics and supplied convexifiers;
general arithmetic obstructions start at degree four (section 9.4).
    Strong noise (theorem 8.16). Without any width or core parameter, if the noise scale dominates
the variation of every coordinate derivative, weighted by the size of the coordinate’s domain and the
degree of the interaction graph, then most coordinates are pinned at a bound by the sign of their
derivative, and the others form small random components of the interaction graph. Each component
is solved exactly by a fixed-degree algebraic procedure (theorem 8.1). Here the noise is large, not
small, and the result describes strongly random linear parts rather than slight perturbations. The
sufficient scale (41) is conservative: for nonquadratic continuous components it contains the large
effective solver base cd , whereas the quadratic case uses the smaller weight ai = 3.
    In every route the output is an exact optimizer of the sampled instance. For quadratic objectives
on rational polyhedral and box domains it is rational; an implicit graph lift can be irrational. For
polynomial objectives the usual output is a compact implicit descriptor: fixed integer labels and
active bounds, and a small box on which the objective is certified strongly convex, so that its unique
minimizer there is the global optimizer. The descriptor supports evaluation of the optimizer and
the optimal value to any requested precision in time polynomial in the precision. Results that finish
with a small exact algebraic solve return all coordinates of the optimizer as functions of one real
algebraic number. The strong-noise route returns one such representation per random component,
with the optimal value as a sum of component values. Bilinear flow coupling and interior optimal

1.2   One mechanism
The first three routes use one mechanism with four steps. Stating it once explains what each theorem
needs from its structure.
     Curvature-corrected refinement preserves global optimizers. Each route has a search space,
namely auxiliary factor coordinates, bag tuples, or core coordinates, and a value function on it
obtained by minimizing over the other variables. Minimizing over a fixed feasible set preserves an
upper bound on coordinate curvature. Hence the minimum of the value function over a grid cell
is at least its least corner value minus a correction proportional to the curvature bound and the
squared mesh. Refining only the cells whose corrected bound does not exceed the incumbent never
discards a global optimizer. This holds on every draw, including draws with ties.
     Independent linear noise bounds the expected number of retained states. A retained cell has a
corner whose value is within a fixed multiple of the sum of the correction and certified evaluation
error of the optimum. Comparing such a corner with its two grid neighbors in one coordinate confines
that coordinate’s noise coefficient to an interval whose length is proportional to the curvature bound
times the mesh. The interval does not depend on the other coefficients of the search space, so when
these coefficients are independent the probabilities multiply. Summing over a deterministic grid gives
an expected count that does not depend on the mesh. The count is taken over a grid fixed before
sampling, so no independence between the noise and the adaptively retained cells is assumed. The
direct product argument applies to sparse box bags, cores, and aligned noise; constrained-domain
variants use their proved directional and transport bounds. Under uniform ambient noise in the
low-rank route, the factor coefficients and residual noise are generally dependent; a conditional
volume estimate (lemma 4.23) replaces independence there and costs a power of the dimension. For
Gaussian-like noise, independence holds in the continuous Gaussian proxy.
     Exact closure finishes the sampled problem. Refinement alone gives approximations. An exact
answer comes from a certificate proving that a small exactly solvable problem contains a global
optimizer: a region on which a parametric convex quadratic program has one formula, a strongly
convex patch after fixing integer labels and active bounds, a gap that excludes every competing
integer label, or a certificate that fixes the face of the core and the set of tied optimal flows. Each
certificate is checked exactly and is sound on every draw.
     A base-selected finite law pays for an exact fallback on the same draw. The certificate passes by
a refinement depth computed from base data unless the draw lies in a rare exceptional set: small
growth, a small gradient at an active bound, a near-tie between integer labels, or noise near one of
finitely many fixed hyperplanes. The finite law is chosen fine enough that this set has probability at
most the reciprocal of the cost factor of an exact fallback algorithm, which then runs on the same
draw. The cost factor is fixed first, then the thresholds and the depth, and the law last, so that no
precision depends on the sampled coefficients it is meant to control.
     The common invariant is a valid curvature-corrected bound together with a noise comparison
whose deterministic part excludes the tested coefficient. Direct recourse obtains it by minimizing
over a feasible set independent of the searched coordinates. Graph charts and order transport
replace that literal premise with their proved curvature and feasible-comparison arguments. The
boundaries in section 9 delimit these mechanisms. Two results finish differently: the integer lattice
theorem (theorem 8.17) stops exactly once a certified gap falls below the spacing of the attainable
objective values, and the strong-noise theorem (theorem 8.16) solves its random components exactly
on every draw. Neither needs a rare fallback.



                                                  8
1.3   What the guarantees mean
Several distinctions matter for reading the theorems.
    Parameters. A fixed-parameter bound has the form f (κ) poly(I) with an exponent independent
of κ; the low-rank results under aligned or Gaussian-like noise and the core results are of this form,
with the numerical ratio included in κ. The sparse results are polynomial for each fixed width
but are not fixed-parameter in the width. A bound that is polynomial in a ratio such as L/σ is
polynomial in its magnitude. For fixed structure, polynomially bounded ratios give polynomial
work, but (1 + L/σ)O(k) poly(I) with L/σ = poly(I) gives I O(k) ; it does not give FPT in k alone.
    Every draw versus expectation. Correctness holds on every draw, including ties and draws with
a continuum of optimizers. Only the work bound is an expectation, over all draws; on rare draws
the work can be exponential.
    The law. The finite law is constructed from the base instance; its grid size or accuracy is chosen
after a fallback cost factor and a stopping depth are known. The theorems do not cover an arbitrary
finite law, a coarse grid chosen independently of the instance, or an arbitrary approximation of
a Gaussian. Most results use polynomially many random bits per coefficient, and for them one
resolution depending only on the input length suffices (proposition 2.3); the nonlinear integer-flow
results with arbitrary core faces use a number of bits that depends on the core size.
    Outputs. Rational outputs are explicit. Implicit outputs are compact descriptors whose global
optimality is established by a separate proof record; the descriptor has polynomial length, the
expected length of the record obeys the corresponding structural and numerical work bound, and
evaluation to q bits costs time polynomial in I + q. Algebraic outputs from a fallback can be
exponentially long on rare draws. No result promises expanded minimal polynomials of small degree
or exact comparisons of sums of unrelated algebraic numbers. No route promises a canonical choice
among tied optimizers on every draw.
    The original objective. All theorems optimize the sampled objective. Proposition 2.6 turns a
sampled solution into a certified interval for the original optimum, of length at most the width of
the realized perturbation over X, maxX γ T x − minX γ T x, plus the evaluation error. Choosing the
noise scale so that the largest possible such width is at most ε/2, which for Gaussian-like laws must
account for their support radius (section 2.6), for routes valid at every positive scale, gives on every
draw an exactly feasible point whose original objective value is certified to be within ε of optimal.
The expected work is polynomial in 1/ε when the structure and the other numerical parameters
are fixed, and polynomial in I and 1/ε only when those parameters are polynomially bounded. On
implicit graphs the feasible point has rational free coordinates and an exact algebraic lift. We do
not claim that this improves on dedicated approximation algorithms, and the strong-noise results
give no such consequence. No result solves the unperturbed problem exactly, which is consistent
with the worst-case hardness cited above.

1.4   Boundaries
Section 9 delimits the method by explicit families. For the sparse retention rule, connected
quartic instances of treewidth p − 1, with fixed curvature, widths, and noise scale, retain more
than (5n/(6p))p/2 cells on every draw, so the rule admits no bound f (p) poly(I) (theorem 9.1);
replacing the global rounding allowance by a bag-local one deletes all optimizers (proposition 9.2).
Certified conditional values give one sound way to remove the ambient dimension from this count
(proposition 5.10 and corollary 9.3). Under uniform ambient noise, perfectly conditioned low-rank
instances have expected counts growing like (N/k)k/4 (theorem 9.4). Objective noise does not make
arbitrary sparse affine constraints of treewidth three tractable unless NP ⊆ ZPP, because every


                                                   9
exact sampled optimizer of a suitable family decides Subset Sum (theorem 9.5). Under core-only
noise with convex quartic residual problems, a constant-accuracy residual point evaluator with
expected polynomial work would give Las Vegas algorithms for Square Root Sum and PosSLP.
The Square Root Sum implication already holds at treewidth two with bounded coefficients; the
PosSLP construction has neither restriction (theorem 9.10). Value accuracy with polynomially
many bits does not localize residual points (proposition 9.9). Single-flow certificates fail at boundary
core optima on an event of probability 1/16 (proposition 9.11). These are limitations of specified
mechanisms or conditional implications, not unconditional lower bounds, and several of the instances
are easy for other methods.

1.5   Relation to prior work
The ingredients of the mechanism are classical, and we use them as such: semiconcavity and corner
rounding on grids, nonserial dynamic programming on tree decompositions [11], exact polynomial-
time convex quadratic programming [30], convex optimization in the bit model [23], exact convex
mixed-integer quadratic programming in fixed integer dimension [15], separable convex optimization
over totally unimodular systems [24], quantifier elimination with block-sensitive bounds [36] and
other effective real-algebraic methods [6, 7], and isolation-type anticoncentration for perturbed
linear objectives [8, 38]. Generic uniqueness and growth under linear tilts of semialgebraic problems
are known qualitatively [31]; section 3 quantifies them for the finite laws used here.
    Among smoothed analyses of continuous global optimization, Kelner and Nikolova [27] give
expected polynomial time for minimizing constant-rank quasi-concave functions over integral
polytopes under a random rotation of the objective subspace, by bounding the vertices of a
projected polytope. Our low-rank route perturbs the linear coefficients instead, allows indefinite
quadratic objectives that are not quasi-concave, and returns exact rational optimizers. For problems
with few negative eigenvalues, deterministic algorithms give approximations whose work grows
polynomially in the inverse accuracy for fixed negative inertia [45]. Rational Jacobi rotations also
yield approximation algorithms for mixed-integer quadratic programs with fixed integer dimension
and negative inertia [16]. For bounded treewidth, linear programming hierarchies give approximations
of polynomial optimization problems in time polynomial for fixed width [12]. The original-objective
consequence of our results (section 1.3) is of the same type and is not claimed to be better.
    Recent deterministic exact results address other structured classes. Khajavirad [28] solves sparse
box polynomial problems under sign, hidden-binary, width, and component-curvature conditions;
the quadratic result is in the rational bit model, whereas its higher-degree result uses a unit-cost
algebraic RAM. Exact integer and mixed-integer quadratic algorithms of Ari and Hildebrand [4] have
dimension- and magnitude-dependent bounds for integer data on polytopes. Ari and Hildebrand
[3] treats bounded pure integer QP, and Wei [46] gives polynomial time for pure integer QP in
each fixed integer dimension. These premises and parameters differ from the arbitrary-dimensional
continuous residual problems and finite-noise expected bit work studied here. Noise alone gives no
general tractability principle: Soltanalian [40] rules out uniform expected polynomial bit time for
exact ReLU verification under rounded, clipped Gaussian parameter noise, assuming NP ⊈ BPP.
That result concerns network verification rather than the structured optimization classes of our
theorems.
    Critical-region descriptions for parametric convex QP are established machinery [44]; their
existence does not bound the number of regions polynomially. Likewise, classical finite-set isolation
and Pareto-count analyses concern discrete feasible alternatives [9, 33]. Our continuous growth
estimates and transfers to a fixed atomic law are separate steps. Algebraic tube bounds control
continuous volume; the finite law requires the additional grid-transfer argument.


                                                  10
    The results of this paper are specific certificates, bounds, and compositions built from these
ingredients. For few negative directions they are closure by regions of a parametric convex program,
with a fixed-hyperplane exceptional set, and the resulting fixed-parameter expected bounds under
aligned and Gaussian-like noise and the dimension-dependent bound under uniform noise (section 4).
For sparse interactions they are exact closure on certified strongly convex patches after a global-error
bag search, with fixed-width expected bounds, and the extensions to graph, recurrence, simplex,
and order constraints (sections 5 and 6). For small cores they are the excluded-region certificate
for continuous recourse, the competing-label certificate for integer recourse, and the optimal-face
certificate for core-only integer flows and totally unimodular systems (sections 7 and 8). Further
contributions are the strong-noise component composition (section 8) and the boundary results of
section 9. Exactness on every draw of a base-chosen finite law is carried through all of these results,
but we do not regard the finite-law model itself, isolation-type anticoncentration, representations of
algebraic points by one root, or connected-set counting as new.
    Three unpublished companion manuscripts have specific overlaps. Exact Arithmetic in Poly-
nomial Optimization: Values, Optimizers, and Certificates [20] supplies all-precision value and
selected-core oracles for core-noise problems with convex residuals, including coupled-polytope
versions and exact rational QP reconstruction. It also treats the jointly convex specialization of
those value and selected-core oracles; with no residual block it returns a canonical lexicographically
least optimizer. It supplies full selected-point oracles for residual-convex cubics without a residual
modulus or convexifier, and under a supplied convexifier when the transformed objective is cubic on
the feasible polytope or is a fixed-degree polynomial convex on all of Rn . Its cubic selector is the
lexicographically least optimal core followed by the least-norm residual optimizer in that fiber. One
base-selected law and one per-draw work factor Ω(γ) apply to all precision queries q for this fixed
point. These law and query properties already appear in the companion. Its outputs are Cauchy
names for the selected point and value; our ordinary outputs are finite patch descriptors with a
separate global proof record, and need not select the same canonical point. This is a representation
distinction. For uniformly strongly convex residuals, the direct recourse count also preserves the
original L/σ, whereas a derived Schur convexifier can require scale M + M 2 /µ, where M bounds
the full Hessian blocks and µ is the residual modulus (section 9.5). The finite-law transfer, section
bounds, projected-growth argument, and lexicographic fallback overlap with our foundations. Its
quartic point reductions, including their core-only-noise consequence, and regularization example
are reproduced with proofs in theorem 9.10 and proposition 9.9. Thus quartic point obstructions do
not exclude its positive cubic result.
    The same companion separates several output requests. Exact recognition of an active coordinate
of a uniformly strongly convex cubic decides Square Root Sum, although arbitrary 2−q point
approximation remains polynomial in the input length and q. An expanded minimal polynomial
can be exponentially large on its path family, while a linear-size recurrence describes the optimizer.
Its specified rational rectangle enclosures can also require large endpoint denominators, including
when an enclosure must certify an active-bound sign. These are limits of the stated output formats;
they do not exclude compact implicit descriptors.
    Decomposition-aware global optimization: certified coordinate grids, conditional recourse, and
structural limits [14] develops deterministic curvature-corrected grids, conditional-recourse filtering,
boundary output, and the star obstruction. For its box quadratic class, let Li = max{0, supXb ∂ii F }
and ∥d∥2L = i Li d2i . Weighted growth F (x) − min F ≥ γ∗ ∥x − x∗ ∥2L gives κ̄ = max{1, 1/γ∗ }; point
              P

growth with modulus g gives κ = max{1, maxi Li /g} and κ̄ ≤ κ. The companion certifies 2−q
accuracy in f (p, κ̄)(I + q + 1)5 bit work, with
                                                      √
                                   f (p, t) = c0 (c1 p t)p t(1 + log2 t)2 ,

                                                  11
                                         C1
       √ exact output in f1 (p, κ)(I + 1) under point growth at a unique optimizer. Graded grids
and gives
use O( κ̄ log(np+ 2)) nodes per coordinate; its sharp example for the filtered common uniform-grid
method needs (n − 2)κ + 1 in the stated stages. Under rETH its lower bound excludes a sublinear
width exponent in the restricted product-time model f (p)(κI)o(p) for unique-minimizer integer box
QP with a supplied decomposition. It is a deterministic approximation boundary, not a smoothed
lower bound or a matching p/2 exponent result. The limits of integrating the growth-dependent
work under noise are discussed in section 5.4. Our sparse theorem bounds expected counts without
a supplied growth modulus and closes exactly on every draw. For its fixed-degree polynomial
factors, the companion also derives a Hessian lower bound on the free coordinates at a point-growth
optimizer and returns a rational face box whose restricted objective is strongly convex. Its runtime
guarantee additionally assumes strict complementarity at the active continuous bounds and depends
polynomially on the bit length of a reciprocal margin. The returned descriptor is implicit; its
optimizer need not have rational coordinates. Our polynomial closure reuses this type of enclosure
and supplies finite-noise margin tails and an exact same-draw fallback.
    Sparse indicator quadratics: exact complexity and smoothed separator messages [41] treats
positive-definite indicator quadratics and perturbs only binary indicator penalties by independent
uniform-grid draws, with at least 2n labels per marginal. It returns exact answers and message
dictionaries on a stated bounded boundary box on every draw, with fixed-width expected work
polynomial in input length and the displayed numerical magnitudes. Removing those numerical
factors would give NP ⊆ ZPP, even at its fixed matrix family and noise scale. These finite-noise,
width, and dictionary ideas overlap. Our sparse route perturbs every original linear coefficient
and treats continuous and native integer coordinates; its affine-feasibility boundary does not
contradict that indicator model. The proofs needed here are included in this paper. The bounds are
mathematical guarantees; practical performance is unestablished.

1.6   Organization
Section 2 fixes the model and output contracts. Section 3 develops the shared counting, tail, and
fallback tools. Section 4 treats few negative directions, sections 5 and 6 sparse interactions, sections 7
and 8 small cores with continuous and integer recourse and the strong-noise route. Section 9 proves
the boundary results, and section 10 discusses uses and open questions. Appendices contain the
longer proofs.


2     Model, noise laws, and output contracts
This section fixes the input model, the perturbation laws, the meaning of an exact smoothed
algorithm, and the output formats. Later sections state their theorems in these terms. The section
ends with two elementary facts that apply to all results: a certified bound for the original objective,
and an example showing why a finite law forces some exact treatment of ties.

2.1   Instances and input length
All data are rational and encoded in binary. A base instance consists of an objective F0 , a feasible
set X ⊆ Rn , a noise scale σ > 0, and the structural data required by the relevant theorem: a
quadratic convexifier, a tree decomposition, a core, curvature bounds, or certificates. Its base length
I is the total binary length of these data. It is measured before any noise is drawn; the bit lengths
of sampled coefficients are accounted for separately.



                                                   12
Objectives. A quadratic objective is F0 (x) = 12 xT Ax + bT x + c with a symmetric rational matrix
A. A polynomial objective of total degree at most d is given by an explicit list of monomials,
each a rational coefficient together with the list of its variables and their positive exponents, or
as a sum of factors, each such a list on a stated set of variables (its scope). This sparse encoding
charges a monomial for the variables it contains, not for the whole dimension. In every polynomial
theorem d is a fixed constant, and polyd (·) denotes a polynomial whose exponent depends on d only.
Objectives are never given as arithmetic circuits. Circuits can be exponentially more compact than
monomial lists, and they change the complexity of evaluation and of exact output; section 9.4 uses
this difference.

Domains. A mixed box is X = ni=1 Xi , where each Xi is either a continuous interval [ℓi , ui ] or a
                                   Q

native integer interval [ℓi , ui ] ∩ Z. Native integer bounds are encoded in binary, so the number of
labels ui − ℓi + 1 can be exponential in I. Integer bounds are rounded inward, empty domains are
rejected, and fixed coordinates are substituted before any algorithm starts. After this preprocessing
every remaining coordinate has width wi = ui − ℓi > 0, and the continuous hull of X is i [ℓi , ui ].
                                                                                             Q

We write nc and nz for the numbers of continuous and native integer coordinates. A mixed polytope
is P ∩ (Znz × Rnc ) for a bounded rational polyhedron P given by linear inequalities. Products
of simplices, order polytopes, explicit and implicit graph constraints, integer flows, and totally
unimodular systems are defined where they are used. Optimization statements assume X =        ̸ ∅, or
explicitly decide feasibility and report an empty set before promising an attaining optimizer.

Structural and numerical parameters. The structural parameters are the negative inertia
k = n− (A) of a quadratic objective, the number k of rows in a supplied concave factor when that
factor is used, the bag size p (treewidth plus one) of a supplied tree decomposition whose bags
contain all factor scopes, the size k of a supplied continuous core, and, in the integer theorems, the
integer dimension. The numerical parameters are curvature and width quantities compared with
σ. Examples are an upper bound L on coordinate second derivatives, ν = max{0, −λmin (A)}, a
convexifier scale α, and coordinate widths wi . Each theorem states on which set a curvature bound
must hold. A numerical parameter enters a bound through its magnitude, not through its encoding
length.

Status of premises. Each premise of a theorem has one of three statuses. It is verified if the
algorithm decides it within the stated work bound; examples are exact definiteness tests of rational
matrices and a curvature bound computed from monomial coefficients. It is certified if the input
contains a proof of it: the proof’s encoding length is part of I, and the work of checking it is part of
the stated work bound. It is promised if the algorithm uses it without checking. A theorem says,
for each promise, whether correctness depends on it or only the complexity analysis does. A false
promise of the first kind can produce a wrong output; a false promise of the second kind leaves
every output correct and can only invalidate the stated work bound.

2.2   Linear perturbations and finite laws
Every result perturbs only the linear part of the objective. The sampled objective is

                                        Fγ (x) = F0 (x) + γ T x,                                    (1)

for a random vector γ ∈ Qn drawn once. The feasible set, the nonlinear coefficients, and all structural
data are not perturbed.


                                                  13
Definition 2.1 (Grid law). For a rational σ > 0 and a power of two M ≥ 2, let
                                     n        2σj                          o
                            Λσ,M = −σ +           : j = 0, 1, . . . , M − 1
                                             M −1
be the M equally spaced points of [−σ, σ], including both endpoints, and let Uσ,M be the uniform
distribution on Λσ,M .

    A draw from Uσ,M uses exactly log2 M fair random bits, and every atom has binary length
O(log M + size(σ)). Because M − 1 is odd, 0 is not an atom. The atoms are equally spaced at
distance 2σ/(M − 1), so a closed interval of length λ contains at most λ(M − 1)/(2σ) + 1 of them.
Hence
                              λ     1
                  Uσ,M (J) ≤     +        for every closed interval J of length λ.                (2)
                             2σ M
The first term is the bound for the continuous uniform law on [−σ, σ]. The second is an atomic
term; it does not decrease with λ, and every finite-law estimate in this paper carries a term of this
kind.

Definition 2.2 (Perturbation models). Let D be a finite rational law on Q. The following models
specify γ in (1).

  (i) Ambient: γ1 , . . . , γn are independent with law D.

 (ii) Core-only: for a supplied core C ⊆ [n], the coefficients γi , i ∈ C, are independent with law D,
      and γi = 0 for i ∈
                       / C.

 (iii) Aligned: for a supplied rational matrix T ∈ Qk×n , γ = T T ξ, where ξ1 , . . . , ξk are independent
       with law D.

    The models coincide in special cases: core-only noise with C = [n], and aligned noise with T = I,
are ambient noise. In general they differ, and a guarantee proved for one model does not transfer
automatically to another. Aligned coefficients are dependent in the original coordinates and lie in
the row space of T . Core-only noise leaves every residual cost unchanged, so ties and flat directions
among residual variables can persist on every draw.
    Two marginal laws are used. The first is the grid law Uσ,M . The second is a Gaussian-like finite
rational law Gb,σ , whose Kolmogorov distance to the normal law N (0, σ 2 ) is at most 2−b and which
is produced by a bounded rejection sampler from polynomially many fair bits (section 3.3). We call
σ the noise scale: it is the half-width of the grid law and the standard deviation of the normal proxy.
The support of Gb,σ is contained in [−(b + 20)σ, (b + 20)σ]. The exact normal law is used only as a
proof device; it is not an input model, because its samples are not finite rational data. Our samplers
have a bound on their worst-case number of random bits, which forces finite support. A Turing
machine without such a bound can also sample a rational law with countably infinite support.

Base-chosen laws. In every theorem except the strong-noise theorem, the law is computed
deterministically from the base instance before sampling: the integer M , or the accuracy b of the
Gaussian-like law, is a function of the base data. As stated, the law therefore depends on the
instance. The strong-noise theorem (theorem 8.16) holds for every grid size M , and its work bound
depends on M explicitly. The theorems assert their bounds for this constructed law. They do not
assert the same bounds for every finite law with noise scale σ, for a coarse grid chosen independently
of the instance, or for an arbitrary approximation of a Gaussian. The reason is the atomic term
in (2): the law must be fine enough that atoms on exceptional sets have small total probability,

                                                   14
and “fine enough” is measured against base-only quantities, such as the cost of an exact fallback
or the spacing of a value lattice. In the results with polynomial sampling precision the logarithms
of these quantities are bounded by polynomials in the input length alone, so the law can also be
chosen from the input length rather than from the individual instance (proposition 2.3).

Sampling precision. Write b = log2 M for the number of random bits per perturbed coordinate
of a grid law. A theorem has polynomial sampling precision if b ≤ poly(I), so that the sampled
instance has length I + n · poly(I). A few results for nonlinear flows and totally unimodular systems
need parameter-dependent sampling precision b ≤ f (k) poly(I); this enlarges the sampled instance,
and the stated work bounds account for it.

Proposition 2.3 (Resolution depending only on the input length). Fix one of the theorems of
sections 4 to 8 with polynomial sampling precision, together with its fixed format constants, such as
the degree and the dependency depth of graph constraints, and its oracle implementation. There are
effective nondecreasing positive integer-valued polynomials P , A, and D, depending only on these
choices, such that the theorem remains valid, with the same output contract and the same structural
and numerical factors in its expected work bound up to a change of its polynomial factor, when its
law is replaced as follows for every valid base instance of length at most I:

 (a) a grid law by Uσ,M (I) with M (I) = 2P (I) ; in the lattice theorem (theorem 8.17) the level cap is
     reset to log2 M (I);

  (b) a Gaussian-like law by Gb(I),σ with b(I) = A(I)+D(I)t(I) and t(I) = ⌈4 log2 (A(I)+D(I)+21)⌉,
      the auxiliary box and the level cap being recomputed for this support.

For the core-only flow and totally unimodular theorems with arbitrary core faces (theorems 8.12
and 8.14) the same holds for instances whose core size is at most a supplied K, with M (I, K) =
2⌈F (K)P (I)⌉ for an effective nondecreasing F , and with F (K) in the sampling, output, and work
bounds. The integer envelope F (K) is chosen with evaluation cost polynomial in 1 + size(K) + F (K),
and this computation is charged before sampling. The strong-noise theorem holds for every M ; its
probabilities qi must be computed for the chosen M , and its sufficient regime is preserved by every
larger power of two.

Proof. Apply corollary 3.21, whose routewise verification is in appendix A.7. The effective bounds
control logarithms of the base-only format and degree multipliers; in particular log(s + 1) ≤ polyd (I)
for an atom universe of size s, even when native integer ranges contribute exponentially many atoms.
Coefficient heights and fallback work retain their separate polynomial dependence on the added
sampled bits. The Gaussian support schedule recomputes the auxiliary box and level cap, and gives
(b(I) + 20)σ ≤ 2t(I) σ. The lattice cap is reset as stated. The arbitrary-face flow and TU cases use
the parameter-dependent part of the corollary with k ≤ K. For strong noise the actual qi and β are
recomputed; the sufficient regime of equation (41) holds for every larger power of two.

    The resolution M (I) standardizes the scalar law, which is still rescaled by the supplied σ and
applied to the coordinates that the theorem perturbs; it is not one probability measure for all
instances. Taking K = I in the flow case gives a law depending only on I, but its sampling cost need
not stay within fd (k) polyd (I) for the actual core size k. The geometric and oracle qualifications
remain part of the proposition. The routewise widths and derivative bounds have polynomial
encoding length; fixed degree alone does not ensure this for arbitrary compact semialgebraic
                                                                             N
domains. For example, 1 ≤ x0 ≤ 2, xi = x2i−1 has last-coordinate width 22 − 1. The proposition
also fixes the oracle implementation; it does not supply one for a broader class.

                                                  15
Small and strong noise. In most results σ is a free parameter, and the bounds degrade as σ
decreases; these are statements about small perturbations of an arbitrary instance. The random-
component results of section 8 instead require noise that dominates the variation of every coordinate
derivative. There σ is large compared with the curvature of the instance, and the results describe
instances whose linear part is strongly random rather than slightly perturbed.

2.3   Exact smoothed algorithms
Computation is measured in bit operations of a deterministic Turing machine. Oracle calls, where a
theorem allows them, are charged at their stated cost.
Definition 2.4 (Exact smoothed algorithm). Fix a class of base instances, an output format, a
perturbation model, and a rule assigning to each base instance Π a finite rational law DΠ for the
perturbation. An exact smoothed algorithm with expected work W is a deterministic algorithm A
with the following properties.
  (i) For every base instance Π and every γ in the support of DΠ , A(Π, γ) halts and returns an
      exact global optimizer and the optimal value of Fγ on X, in the stated format.
 (ii) The perturbation is drawn once. The algorithm does not resample, discard, or condition on
      any event of the draw.
 (iii) The bit work of computing DΠ , sampling, and running A(Π, γ) has expectation at most W (Π)
       over γ ∼ DΠ .
    The randomness defines the instance to be solved; the algorithm itself is deterministic given
the draw. Property (i) includes draws with tied or positive-dimensional optimal sets and draws on
which a fast route fails. Property (ii) excludes the shortcut of redrawing until a draw is generic,
which would change the law of the solved instance and with it the meaning of the expectation.
By Markov’s inequality, an expected bound W implies work at most tW with probability at least
1 − 1/t; it is not a worst-case bound, and on rare draws the work can be exponential in I.

How ties are handled. A finite law gives ties and other degenerate draws positive probability
(section 2.7), so an exact smoothed algorithm needs a way to solve them. Most results use a fast
route that refines a search and closes it with an exact certificate, together with an exact fallback: a
deterministic algorithm that solves every sampled instance with bit work at most B · poly(I + b),
where the factor B depends only on the base instance and log B ≤ poly(I). The fallback runs on the
same draw when the fast route has not finished by a stopping depth computed from base data. If
this happens with probability at most 1/B, the fallback contributes only a polynomial term in I + b
to the expected work, including the stated parameter factor when sampling precision depends on the
core size (lemma 3.18). The quantities are chosen in a fixed order: first B, then the thresholds that
define exceptional events, then the stopping depth, and finally the law. Two results need no rare
fallback. The lattice theorem for low-rank integer problems (theorem 8.17) stops exactly once the
certified gap falls below the spacing of the lattice of attainable objective values. The strong-noise
component theorem (theorem 8.16) solves every random component exactly on every draw.

Expectations of adaptive searches. The fast routes refine cells adaptively, and which cells
are visited depends on the draw. Expected counts are therefore never computed as if the retained
cells were independent of the noise. Every visited cell is charged to a node of a deterministic grid
fixed before sampling, and the expected number of nodes that satisfy a necessary near-optimality
condition is bounded node by node (theorem 3.9).

                                                  16
2.4   Output contracts
Exact output for Fγ takes one of the following forms. Each theorem states which form it returns on
ordinary draws and on fallback draws.

Definition 2.5 (Output formats).

 (a) Rational output: a feasible x∗ ∈ Qn with Fγ (x∗ ) = minX Fγ , and this value.

 (b) Implicit patch output: a set S of coordinates with fixed rational values zS (native integer labels,
     original continuous bounds, or continuous values fixed by a sound singleton-hull certificate),
     a rational box Q in the remaining coordinates such that (zS , y) ∈ X for every y ∈ Q, and a
     rational g0 > 0 with a verified proof that ∇2yy Fγ (zS , y) ⪰ g0 I on Q, together with the claim
     that the unique minimizer ŷ of Fγ (zS , ·) on Q gives a global optimizer x̂ = (zS , ŷ) of Fγ on X.
     Products of simplices and order polytopes use a rational relative polytope in place of Q.

 (c) Charted output: for feasible sets that are graphs of maps from free to dependent coordinates,
     an implicit patch output for the reduced objective in the free coordinates, together with a
     certified chart that determines every dependent coordinate from the free ones. The chart is
     either an explicit polynomial map, or a list of scalar equations, each with a rational bracket
     on which a certified positive derivative bound guarantees a unique root. The optimizer is the
     lift of the unique reduced patch minimizer through the chart.

 (d) Algebraic output: a real algebraic number θ, given by a nonzero integer polynomial and
     an isolating interval with rational endpoints, and rational polynomial maps that give every
     continuous coordinate of one optimizer and the optimal value as functions of θ, with integer
     coordinates listed explicitly.

 (e) Component output: a partition of the unfixed coordinates into components, an algebraic
     output for each component, rational values for the remaining coordinates, and the optimal
     value written as a rational constant plus the sum of the component values.

    Several distinctions are essential.
    Descriptor versus proof. Implicit patch and charted outputs are compact descriptors of polynomial
length. A descriptor denotes a unique point because the restricted objective is strongly convex on
the patch; that point is a global optimizer because of a separate global proof record, which contains
the pruning trace and the certificate checks. The descriptor alone does not prove global optimality.
The proof record can be large on an individual draw; its expected size obeys the same bound as the
expected work.
    Evaluation. Given a descriptor and an integer q ≥ 0, a q-bit evaluation returns a rational
point within Euclidean distance 2−q of the optimizer and a rational interval of width at most 2−q
that contains the optimal value. For patch outputs this costs polyd (I + q) bit operations: convex
optimization in the bit model gives value accuracy, and the verified modulus g0 converts value
accuracy into distance. On box, polytope, and explicit-graph domains the evaluation also returns a
feasible rational point with a certified objective gap. An implicit graph need not contain any rational
feasible point: the equation y 2 = 2 with y ∈ [1, 2] is an example. For charted outputs on implicit
graphs, an exactly feasible point consists of rational free coordinates together with the algebraic
dependent coordinates that the chart assigns to them; rational approximations of all coordinates
need not be feasible. Evaluation does not decide exact comparisons of the optimal value with a
rational threshold, and it does not identify the exact active set. The exact-arithmetic companion
shows why these are different requests: exact active-coordinate recognition for a uniformly strongly

                                                  17
convex cubic can decide Square Root Sum while point approximation remains polynomial [20]. If
the patch has no free coordinate, its unique point is evaluated directly; on an implicit graph this
includes evaluation of its exact dependent lift. No full-dimensional convex optimization routine is
invoked on a point.
    Algebraic sizes. An algebraic output fixes one optimizer: all its coordinates are functions of one
root θ, so coordinates of different optimizers cannot be mixed. Its defining polynomial need not
be minimal. Algebraic outputs from fallback draws have length and q-bit refinement cost at most
B polyd (I + b + q), where B = 2polyd (I) is base-only; their expected contribution is polynomial in the
sampled bit length and requested precision under the stated law, with any stated sampling-parameter
factor retained. A component output does not include a defining polynomial of the total value,
whose degree can grow exponentially with the number of components, and it does not provide exact
sign or equality tests for sums of unrelated algebraic numbers. Rational enclosures of the total value
are obtained by refining each component.
    Selection. Unless a theorem says so, the returned optimizer is the one the algorithm happens to
certify. No canonical selection rule, such as the lexicographically least or minimum-norm optimizer,
is promised.

2.5   Fixed-parameter and fixed-width bounds
A bound is fixed-parameter in a parameter κ if it has the form f (κ) · poly(I), where the exponent of
the polynomial does not depend on κ. The low-negative-inertia, core, and integer-dimension results
are of this form, with numerical ratios such as ν diam(X)/σ or L/σ included in κ. A bound is
fixed-width polynomial if it is polynomial for each fixed value of a parameter p but its degree grows
with p, for instance [nLwmax /σ]O(p) poly(I). The sparse results are of this kind, and they are not
fixed-parameter bounds in the width. The limitation is genuine for the method used: theorem 9.1
exhibits instances on which its retained state count is (Ω(n/p))p/2 on every draw.
    A bound that is polynomial in a numerical ratio is polynomial in its magnitude, which can be
exponential in its encoding length. The phrase expected polynomial time is used only when the relevant
ratios are polynomially bounded in I and the structural parameters are fixed. FPT means joint
parameterization by structure and the displayed numerical ratios, or parameterization by structure
for each fixed bounded ratio. If a ratio R grows polynomially in I, a bound (1 + R)O(k) poly(I) is
I O(k) , rather than FPT in k alone.

2.6   The original objective
The theorems optimize the sampled objective Fγ . An exact optimizer of Fγ need not optimize F0 ,
and nothing is claimed about its distance from the optimal set of F0 . The following elementary
bound states what a sampled solution certifies for the original problem. For compact X and γ ∈ Rn ,
let
                                  ωX (γ) = max γ T x − min γ T x

be the width of X in the direction γ.

Proof. For every x ∈ X, F0 (x) = Fγ (x) − γ T x ≥ a − maxX γ T x, which gives the lower endpoint.
The upper endpoint is the value of the feasible point y. Finally,

     For an exact sampled optimizer x̂ one may take y = x̂, δ = 0, and a = minX Fγ . The endpoints

outputs they are generally irrational. Rational certified endpoints come from a q-bit evaluation: a
feasible point y with a rational upper value, and a rational lower bound a, whose distance from
each other is the evaluation error, which is added to δ. The point y must be exactly feasible. On
implicit graphs it consists of rational free coordinates and their exact algebraic lift, and F0 (y) is
replaced by the upper end of a certified rational enclosure; a coordinatewise rational approximation
of the lift may be infeasible and cannot be used.

certificate of original-objective gap at most ε on every draw. For grid laws take σ = ε/(2Wnoise ).
For Gaussian-like laws the support bound is exactly σ̄ = (b + 20)σ, with b chosen by the effective
base-data schedule. Put R = max{1, Wnoise /ε} and let I0 be the length of the supplied data and
accuracy, excluding the noise scale. There is an effective nondecreasing polynomial P such that the
required accuracy at σ = 2−t satisfies b(t) ≤ P (I0 + t), after absorbing fixed encoding constants.

Exponential growth dominates this fixed polynomial, so t = O(I0 + log2 R + 1). If t ≥ 1, failure at

This preserves polynomial sampling precision and includes the full support factor in every numerical

   Substitute this scale into the theorem’s displayed bound, retaining every other numerical factor.
For theorem 5.2 under grid noise, for example, the bound is C0p [4+(1+n/2)Lwmax Wnoise /ε]p polyd (I).
The work is polynomial in 1/ε for fixed structure and fixed other numerical parameters; it is


                                                      19
polynomial in I and 1/ε only when those numerical factors are polynomially bounded. Binary-
encoded curvature, widths, and coupling-derived bounds may be exponentially large. The strong-
noise results retain the width bound but do not give this prescribed-accuracy consequence: their
lower noise-scale condition need not permit the calibration. None of these guarantees yields exact
optimization of F0 by taking a positive noise scale to zero. This is consistent with the worst-case
hardness of the unperturbed problems discussed in sections 1 and 9.

2.7    Why a finite law forces an exact treatment of ties
Under a continuous noise law, ties among optimizers and other degenerate configurations typically
have probability zero. Under a finite law they can have positive probability that no refinement of
the search removes. The following example is the smallest instance of this phenomenon.

Example 2.7 (A tie with probability 1/M ). Let X = [0, 1]2 , F0 (x) = 2x1 x2 − x1 − x2 , and let
γ1 , γ2 be independent with law Uσ,M , where 0 < σ ≤ 1/4. The event γ1 = γ2 has probability exactly
M · M −2 = 1/M .
     On this event, put s = 1 − γ1 ∈ [3/4, 5/4]. The minimum value of Fγ is −s, and

                                 Fγ (x) + s = s(1 − x1 − x2 ) + 2x1 x2 .

If x1 + x2 ≤ 1, both terms are nonnegative, and they vanish together only at (1, 0) and (0, 1). If
x1 + x2 > 1, then x1 x2 > 0, and (1 − x1 )(1 − x2 ) ≥ 0 gives x1 + x2 − 1 ≤ x1 x2 , so Fγ (x) + s ≥
(2 − s)x1 x2 > 0. Hence Fγ has exactly the two global minimizers (1, 0) and (0, 1) on every draw of
this event.
    Consequently no certificate that exhibits a strongly convex patch containing every global
optimizer can succeed on this event, at any refinement depth: a strictly convex function has at
most one minimizer on a convex set. The point-growth modulus is zero, so any tail bound of the
form Pr{g∗ < ε} ≤ cε + β for the grid law must have β ≥ 1/M for all ε > 0. An algorithm that is
correct on every draw must solve this event by other means. The closure-based results do so with
their same-draw fallback, whose expected contribution on this event is at most M −1 times its cost;
choosing M large compared with the base-only factor B keeps this contribution polynomial. Under
any absolutely continuous law for (γ1 , γ2 ) the tie has probability zero, which is why continuous-noise
analyses can ignore it.

   The example shows why the probability estimates of the later sections have the form “continuous
bound plus atomic term”, and why the grid size M is chosen only after the fallback factor and the
stopping depth are known. Example 3.11 shows the companion effect on counts: a fixed atom can
keep the number of retained cells growing with the refinement level.


3     Local counts, growth tails, and an exact fallback
This section develops the probabilistic and algorithmic tools shared by the quadratic, sparse, and
recourse results. Every statement concerns a fixed base function and an independent random linear
perturbation of it. No statement uses convexity, differentiability, uniqueness of an optimizer, or
quadratic growth of the base problem unless that property is listed among its hypotheses.
    Throughout, I denotes the bit length of the rational base data before any perturbation is sampled.
It includes the noise width σ and every supplied decomposition or certificate. A perturbation is
drawn once, from a law that the algorithm computes from the base data before sampling. No



                                                   20
algorithm in this paper discards, resamples, or conditions on a draw, and every algorithm is correct
on every draw. Probability enters only the bounds on work.
    The section has four parts. Theorem 3.4 bounds the expected number of grid nodes that survive
comparisons with their grid neighbors. Together with curvature-corrected refinement (Theorem 3.9),
it bounds the expected number of cells that a search retains at every refinement level, uniformly in
the level. Section 3.3 transfers such bounds from continuous proxy laws to finite rational laws that
a Turing machine can sample. Theorem 3.16 and Lemma 3.17 bound the probability of small global
quadratic growth and of small active gradients. Theorem 3.20 gives an exact algorithm for every
draw of a fixed-degree polynomial problem. Its cost contains an exponential factor that depends
only on the base data, and Lemma 3.18 shows how a rare exceptional event pays for that factor.
Section 3.6 summarizes how the later sections combine these parts. Proofs are in Appendix A.

3.1    Upper curvature and local comparison events
Let X = ki=1 [ai , bi ] be a box with ai < bi and widths wi = bi − ai . The mesh statements use k ≥ 1.
          Q

Fixed coordinates are held at their values, and a singleton box is evaluated directly before a width
maximum, mesh, or accuracy threshold is formed. For a function f : X → R and a coefficient vector
c ∈ Rk write
                                          Fc (x) = f (x) + cT x.

Definition 3.1 (Upper coordinate curvature). A function f : X → R has upper coordinate curvature
at most L = (L1 , . . . , Lk ) ∈ [0, ∞)k if, for every i and every x ∈ X, the function t 7→ f (x+tei )−Li t2 /2
is concave on the interval {t : x + tei ∈ X}.

    The property holds when f − 21 i Li x2i is concave. It is preserved by pointwise infima: if every
                                          P

fω has upper coordinate curvature at most L and inf ω fω is finite, then inf ω fω has upper coordinate
curvature at most L, because an infimum of concave functions is concave. The auxiliary value
functions of Section 4 are of this kind. Downward kinks, discontinuous derivatives, and arbitrarily
large negative curvature are allowed. Only an upper bound on curvature is used.

Lemma 3.2 (Neighbor comparisons confine one coefficient). Let f : X → R be arbitrary, v ∈ X,

    The bound concerns a deterministic grid. An adaptive search visits a subset of its nodes that
depends on the draw, but every node that the search keeps for a probabilistic reason will be shown
to satisfy a local event. The expectation is therefore taken over a fixed family of events, and no
independence between the draw and the adaptively selected cells is used. The comparisons with
grid neighbors make the other coefficients cancel, which is why independence across coordinates
multiplies the per-coordinate bounds.

    A coordinate that is not yet refined has no interior node. It contributes the factor 2, with no

equal parts. Clipping a uniform step at the end of an interval would create a short final gap g, and
Lemma 3.2 would then produce a term δ/g that the count cannot control. The balance constant
cbal matters only for anisotropic curvature; it is used with cbal < 4 in Section 4.7.

Lemma 3.7 (Corrected corners). Let f have upper coordinate curvature at most L, and let
C = i [li , li + ηi ] ⊆ X be a cell. For every c and every x ∈ C,

Definition 3.8 (Corrected-corner search). Fix balanced nested meshes on X, a level cap J, and
exact evaluation of Fc . Level 0 processes the cell X. Level j ≥ 1 processes the children of the
cells retained at level j − 1. For every processed cell C, evaluate Fc at all corners of C and put
F (C) = mincorners Fc − Ej . Optionally, a closure step computes minC Fc exactly together with a
point attaining it; such a cell is closed. After all cells of level j have been processed, let Uj be the
least value of Fc found so far, at corners or at closure points, stored together with a point attaining
it. Retain exactly the processed cells that are not closed and satisfy F (C) ≤ Uj . Stop when no cell
is retained or when level J has been processed.
Theorem 3.9 (Corrected-corner search). Let f : X → R be lower semicontinuous with upper
coordinate curvature at most L, and put F ∗ = minX Fc . For every coefficient vector c the search of
Definition 3.8 has the following properties.
  (i) F (C) ≤ minC Fc for every processed cell C.
 (ii) If no cell is retained after level j, then Uj = F ∗ and the stored point is a global minimizer.
 (iii) Uj ≤ F ∗ + Ej .
                                                                                                    2E
 (iv) Every cell retained at level j has a corner v ∈ Gj with Fc (v) ≤ F ∗ + 2Ej . Hence c ∈ Ev j ,
                                                                     2E
      and the number of retained cells is at most 2k #{v ∈ Gj : c ∈ Ev j }.
  (v) The number U j = min{Uj , minC retained F (C)} satisfies U j ≤ F ∗ ≤ Uj and Uj − U j ≤ Ej , with
      the minimum over an empty retained set defined as +∞.
Consequently, under the hypotheses of Corollary 3.6, the expected number of cells retained at level j
is at most 2k times the corresponding bound of that corollary. This holds with or without closure
steps.
    Item (iv) is the bridge between the adaptive search and the deterministic count: a cell is retained
for a probabilistic reason only through one of its corners, which is a nearly optimal node of a fixed
grid.
Corollary 3.10 (Approximation with logarithmic accuracy dependence). Use isotropic meshes
with common curvature L > 0, let s = maxi wi , fix ε > 0, and put J = max{0, ⌈ 12 log2 (kLs2 /(8ε))⌉}.
Without closure steps, the search, stopped after level J or earlier if no cell is retained, returns a
point x and a number U with U ≤ F ∗ ≤ Fc (x) and Fc (x) − U ≤ ε, for every c. If the coordinates of
c are independent with densities bounded by ϕi , the expected number of evaluations of Fc is at most
2k + 8k HJ, where H = i [2 + (1 + 2k)Lϕi wi ]. If instead ci ∼ Uσ,M with M ≥ 2J , the same holds
                        Q

with H = i [3 + (1 + 2k)Lwi /(2σ)].
          Q

    For fixed k and numerical bounds on Lϕi wi , the expected number of evaluations is logarithmic
in 1/ε. The bound counts exact evaluations of the sampled objective. Their bit cost must be added,
and the conclusion concerns the sampled objective, not the unperturbed one. The finite law in the
corollary is chosen for the requested accuracy. A fixed finite law does not support the same bound
at arbitrarily fine levels.

                                                      23
Example 3.11 (A fixed atom defeats level-uniform counts). Let k = 1 and X = [0, 1], and let the
law of c have an atom c0 with P(c = c0 ) = p0 > 0. For f (x) = −c0 x, which has upper coordinate
curvature at most L for every L > 0, the draw c = c0 makes Fc ≡ 0. On this draw every node of
every level is a global minimizer, every cell has F (C) = −Ej < 0 = Uj , and all 2j cells of level j
are retained. The expected number of retained cells is therefore at least p0 2j , which grows without
bound.
    The example limits this particular search: under a fixed finite law, the cells retained by indefinitely
continued refinement cannot be counted uniformly in the level. It does not show that every exact
algorithm needs a rare fallback. A finite law supports Corollary 3.6 only up to a level J with
M ≥ miJ (or, for transferred bounds, up to a node budget fixed in advance), so an exact algorithm
built on this search stops refining at a level computed from the base data and finishes the sampled
problem in another way. Most results below use closure steps, which certify the exact minimum
of a whole cell from a structural formula, together with an exact fallback on the same draw for
the draws that are still unresolved at level J. The lattice theorem (Theorem 8.17) instead stops at
a predetermined level at which the certified gap is below the spacing of the attainable objective
values, and the strong-noise theorem (Theorem 8.16) does not use this search; neither needs a rare
fallback. In all cases atoms, ties, and flat optimal faces are handled on every draw; they are never
excluded by a genericity assumption.

3.3    Finite rational laws and continuous proxies
A Turing machine with fair random bits can sample laws with infinitely many rational atoms if
its running time is unbounded; for example, counting the failures before the first success gives a
geometric law on the nonnegative integers. The samplers in this paper are not of this kind: each
uses at most a prescribed number of fair bits and bit operations in the worst case, and a sampler
with this property has a finite law with rational atoms. The analysis often computes a probability
under a continuous proxy law and then transfers it to the finite law that the algorithm actually
samples. Two families of finite laws are used.
Definition 3.12 (Finite laws and Kolmogorov distance). The grid law Uσ,M is that of Definition 2.1.
For an integer b ≥ 1 and rational σ > 0, Gb,σ is the law of σZb , where Zb is the output of the sampler of
Lemma 3.14. The Kolmogorov distance of laws µ, ν on R is dK (µ, ν) = supt∈R |µ(−∞, t] − ν(−∞, t]|.
Lemma 3.13 (Product replacement). (a) The uniform grid law satisfies dK (Uσ,M , Unif[−σ, σ]) ≤
   1/M , and the Gaussian-like law satisfies dK (Gb,σ , N (0, σ 2 )) ≤ 2−b .

 (b) Let µ1 , . . . , µn and ν1 , . . . , νn be laws on R with dK (µi , νi ) ≤ δi , and let E ⊆ Rn be a Borel
     set. Suppose that for every i and every choice of real values of the coordinates other than the
     ith, the section of E in the ith coordinate is a union of at most C intervals (open, closed,
     half-open, degenerate, or unbounded). Then
                                   O               O                n
                                                                        X
                                          µi (E) −        νi (E) ≤ 2C         δi .
                                      i               i                 i=1

   The section bound must hold for every choice of the other coordinates, not only for almost every
choice under the proxy law, because the replacement passes through mixed products of finite and
continuous marginals.
Lemma 3.14 (A bounded rational Gaussian-like sampler). For every integer b ≥ 1 there is a finite
law Gb on Q, determined by b through a fixed deterministic rule, with the following properties.

                                                     24
 (a) Its support lies in [−(b + 20), b + 20], and every atom is a rational number of bit length O(b).

  (b) dK (Gb , N (0, 1)) ≤ 2−b .

  (c) One sample is produced from independent fair bits in poly(b) bit operations in the worst case.

    The law Gb,σ is not a Gaussian law, and no theorem in this paper takes real Gaussian coefficients
as Turing input. The Gaussian law N (0, σ 2 ) serves only as a proxy in proofs: it makes orthogonal
projections of the noise independent and supplies density bounds. Every theorem that uses a
Gaussian-like law specifies the accuracy b as a function of the base data, computed before sampling.
The bounds do not apply to an arbitrary finite approximation of a Gaussian, and in particular not
to a coarse one, because Example 3.11 shows how atoms can create unbounded counts.

3.4    Global growth and active gradients
Definition 3.15 (Point growth). Let X ⊂ Rn be nonempty and compact, let f : X → R be lower
semicontinuous, and let Fc = f + cT x. The point-growth modulus g∗ (c) ∈ [0, ∞] is the supremum of
the numbers g ≥ 0 for which some minimizer x∗ of Fc on X satisfies

                                   Fc (x) − Fc (x∗ ) ≥ g ∥x − x∗ ∥2         (x ∈ X).

If X is a singleton, g∗ = ∞. If Fc has two distinct minimizers, then g∗ = 0. Write wi =
maxX xi − minX xi and S = i wi .
                          P


    Point growth is measured from one minimizer. It differs from growth measured by the distance
to the set of minimizers, which can remain positive in the presence of ties.

Theorem 3.16 (Sharp growth tail). Let X ⊂ Rn be nonempty and compact, let f : X → R be
lower semicontinuous, and let c have independent coordinates with densities bounded by ϕ1 , . . . , ϕn .
Then for every ε > 0
                                                                n
                                                                X
                                           P{g∗ (c) < ε} ≤ 2ε         ϕ i wi .
                                                                i=1

In particular, if ci is uniform on [−σ, σ] for every i, then P{g∗ (c) < ε} ≤ εS/σ. If X is not a
singleton, then for every ρ ∈ (0, 1], with probability at least 1 − ρ the minimizer of Fc is unique and
g∗ (c) ≥ ρ/(2 i ϕi wi ). The constant 2 cannot be decreased: for X = [−w/2, w/2], f = 0, and c
              P

uniform on [−σ, σ], P{g∗ (c) < ε} = 2εϕw with ϕ = 1/(2σ) whenever 0 < ε ≤ σ/w.

    The theorem assumes neither convexity, differentiability, nor definability of f or X. Its proof
works in coefficient space. The function H(a) = maxx∈X {aT x − f (x) + ε ∥x∥2 } is convex, and
wherever H is differentiable at the proximal point of −c, the tilted problem has growth at least ε.
The proximal map is a 1-Lipschitz gradient map, so the area formula shows that its Jacobian is
singular almost everywhere on the bad event. This bounds the indicator of the bad event by the
trace of the Jacobian of the proximal residual, whose integral along each coordinate is at most 2εwi .
    Two comparisons clarify the scope. For a finite binary feasible set and independent continuous
coefficients, Beier and Vöcking bound the density of the gap between the best and second-best
                                 P
values by the same expression 2 i ϕi [8]. That statement compares values, not values relative to
squared distances, and it does not apply to continuous feasible sets. Qualitative genericity, stability,
and growth results are known for semialgebraic programs [31]; they do not give this quantitative
tail on arbitrary compact sets with a lower semicontinuous objective. Theorem 3.16 quantifies the
probability of small growth.

                                                         25
    The theorem concerns continuous laws. It does not by itself give an exact algorithm in the bit
model. For example, minimizing x2 /2 + cx over [−1, 1] with continuous c has the interior minimizer
−c, which is irrational with probability one when it is interior. A finite rational law has atoms, and
ties can occur with positive probability. The next lemma gives finite-law tails when the problem is
semialgebraic.
    For the polynomial statements, let D ⊂ Rn+m be a nonempty compact set described by a
quantifier-free formula with at most s atomic polynomial sign conditions of degree at most d
and rational coefficients. Let F ∈ Q[x, y] have degree at most d. The reduced objective f (x) =
min{F (x, y) : (x, y) ∈ D} is lower semicontinuous on the compact projection X of D. The noise
acts on x only: Fγ (x, y) = F (x, y) + γ T x, and g∗ (γ) is the point-growth modulus of f + γ T x on X.
When m = 0, f = F . A bounded integer coordinate xj ∈ {ℓj , . . . , uj } is described by the disjunction
Wuj
  t=ℓj (xj = t), which adds uj − ℓj + 1 atoms but no variables.
