    The constant aR comes from the block-sensitive quantifier-elimination theorem of Renegar
[36]. Its exponent depends on the product of the two block sizes, not on a tower over the number
of variables; a bound of cylindrical-decomposition type would not give a sampling precision of
polynomial length. For fixed d, if log(s + 1) is polynomial in I, then log Ctail is polynomial in I. This
holds for mixed boxes with binary-encoded integer ranges, since those contribute j (uj − ℓj + 1)
                                                                                         P

atoms. The value of aR is not extracted here; the sampling rules below use one fixed admissible
value. The section bound is used only through the logarithm of Ctail , which determines a number of
sampled bits. For quadratic objectives with m = 0 an elementary argument gives the explicit bound
8(F + 1)2 in place of Ctail , where F counts candidate faces (Lemma 4.8).

Lemma 3.18 (Paying for a rare fallback). (a) (Accounting.) Let an algorithm act on one draw
   from a law fixed before sampling, and let its work on a draw be W0 + 1E W1 . Suppose that

                                                     26
      W1 ≤ B P (Lmax ) on every draw, where B ≥ 1 and the polynomial P are fixed before sampling
      and Lmax bounds the bit length of every sampled instance, and that P(E) ≤ 1/B. Then
      E[W0 + 1E W1 ] ≤ EW0 + P (Lmax ).

 (b) (Budget for polynomial mixed boxes.) In the setting of Lemma 3.17(d), assume S > 0, let
     ρ ∈ (0, 1], and put
                                     ρσ                 ρσ
                                g0 =    ,     τ=                 .
                                      S            max{1, Kact }
      Let Ebad be the event that g∗ < g0 , or that g∗ ≥ g0 and some active continuous coordinate
      has |∂i Fγ (x∗ )| ≤ τ . If γi ∼ Uσ,M with M ≥ (2nCtail + Kact )/ρ, or if dK (γi , N (0, σ 2 )) ≤
      ρ/(2nCtail + 2Kact ), then P(Ebad ) ≤ 3ρ.

   If S = 0, the mixed box is a single point. It is evaluated directly before any threshold is chosen,
and neither the tails nor a fallback are needed; part (b) divides by S and is used only when S > 0.
   Part (a) cancels the combinatorial multiplier B of a fallback, not its whole bit cost: the remaining
polynomial factor must depend only on the bit length of the sampled instance, which is bounded
before sampling. The multiplier B must cover every base-only cost of the fallback, including the
conversion of its output to the required format (Theorem 3.20). This ordering, first B, then the
thresholds and the level cap, and only then the law, avoids a circular choice in which a precision
depends on the sampled coefficients that it is supposed to control.

3.5   An exact fallback for fixed-degree polynomial problems
Definition 3.19 (Explicit mixed semialgebraic instances). Fix a degree bound d ≥ 1. A base
instance consists of variables x ∈ RN with N ≥ 1, a set Z ⊆ {1, . . . , N } of integer variables with
binary-encoded bounds ℓj ≤ uj (j ∈ Z), a quantifier-free formula Φ0 whose atoms are explicitly
listed polynomial sign conditions of degree at most d with rational coefficients, and an objective
F0 ∈ Q[x] of degree at most d, given by its list of monomials with rational coefficients. Its domain is

                    D = {x ∈ RN : Φ0 (x) holds and xj ∈ Z ∩ [ℓj , uj ] for j ∈ Z},

which is assumed nonempty and compact. Its bit length I counts Φ0 , F0 , and the bounds. A tilted
instance adds rational coefficients γi , each of bit length at most b, to any subset of the variables:
                  P
Fγ (x) = F0 (x) + i γi xi . A real algebraic number θ is given in root representation by a nonzero
p ∈ Z[t] and a rational interval, possibly a point, that contains θ and no other real root of p.

    The class contains mixed boxes (Φ0 a conjunction of bounds), bounded polytopes and mixed
polytopes, and polynomial graph constraints y = g(x) with explicit polynomials g. It does not
contain objectives given by arithmetic circuits or by unexpanded compositions; the expanded
monomial list of F0 is part of I. A problem with no remaining variables is evaluated directly before
this N ≥ 1 elimination construction is invoked.

Theorem 3.20 (Exact fallback). Fix d ≥ 1. There are a deterministic algorithm, a base-computable
integer B = 2polyd (I) , and an exponent ed depending only on d such that the following holds for
every base instance of Definition 3.19 and every tilted instance whose added coefficients have bit
length at most b.

  (i) Within B (I +b+1)ed bit operations the algorithm returns an algebraic output (Definition 2.5(d))
      of the lexicographically least global minimizer xlex and of the optimal value f ∗ = minD Fγ : the
      integer coordinates of xlex ; a squarefree polynomial P ∈ Z[t] with a rational interval isolating


                                                  27
      one real root θ of P ; and rational polynomials ri , for the continuous coordinates i ∈
                                                                                            / Z, and rf
             lex               ∗
      with xi = ri (θ) and f = rf (θ). As auxiliary data it also returns a root representation of
      every coordinate of xlex and of f ∗ . The degree of P and of every auxiliary defining polynomial
      is at most B, independently of b, and every coefficient and interval endpoint has bit length at
      most B (I + b + 1)ed .

 (ii) For every integer q ≥ 0 it returns, within B (I + b + q + 1)ed bit operations, a rational point
      within Euclidean distance 2−q of xlex and a rational interval of width at most 2−q that contains
      f ∗.

(iii) If D is a mixed box, it also returns within the bound of (ii) a rational point y ∈ D with
        y − xlex ≤ 2−q and a rational number ℓ ≤ f ∗ with Fγ (y) − ℓ ≤ 2−q .

Neither B nor ed depends on γ, b, or q. The algorithm is correct for every γ, including draws with
ties, with a continuum of minimizers, or with singular stationary sets.

    The output is long on some instances: its length is at most B (I + b + 1)ed , exponential in the
base length and polynomial in the added coefficient length b. No bound independent of b is possible,
since for the domain {1} and F0 = 0 the optimal value is the added coefficient itself. Lemma 3.18(a)
needs exactly this separation of a base-only factor from a polynomial factor in I + b. The proof
characterizes each coordinate of xlex , and the optimal value, as the unique solution of a formula
with two quantified blocks and one free variable, eliminates the quantifiers by Renegar’s theorem
[36], and recovers each singleton by univariate root isolation. The resulting scalar representations
all refer to the same canonical minimizer, but separately isolated coordinates do not yet form an
algebraic output. A final step converts them into one shared root (Lemma A.2): in the tensor
product of the univariate quotient algebras, a linear form with integer coefficients separates all tuples
of roots; the characteristic polynomial P of its multiplication matrix expresses every coordinate as a
polynomial function of its roots; and the scalar isolators select the root θ that corresponds to xlex
and f ∗ . The other roots of P correspond to tuples of scalar roots that need not come from any
optimizer. Only the selected root carries meaning: the value identity Fγ (xlex ) = rf (θ) holds at θ,
but it need not hold as an identity modulo P . The conversion costs a polynomial in the product of
the scalar degrees. Elimination bounds these degrees independently of b (only coefficient lengths
grow with b), so the product is 2polyd (I) and the conversion fits in the factor B. That factor is fixed,
with the conversion included, before any threshold or law is chosen. No minimal polynomial, unique
minimizer, or finite stationary set is required. For quadratic objectives on mixed polytopes, a much
smaller rational fallback suffices (Lemma 4.11).

3.6    How the tools are combined
The closure-based exact results follow one template: those of Section 4 and, with growth, label, or
face certificates in place of critical regions, those of Sections 5–8 other than the two results described
at the end of this subsection.

 (1) Before sampling, the algorithm computes from the base data a fallback multiplier B that
     covers every base-only cost of the fallback, the bad-event thresholds, a level cap J, and a finite
     law (an accuracy M or b and, for Gaussian-like laws, a support radius). All have polynomial
     bit length in I, except in the results with parameter-dependent sampling precision.

 (2) It draws the perturbation once.



                                                   28
  (3) It runs curvature-corrected refinement with exact closure steps through level J. Closure certifies
      the exact minimum of a cell from a structural formula valid on the whole cell. Correctness of
      every retained bound and of every closed cell holds on every draw (Theorem 3.9).
  (4) If unresolved cells remain at level J, it runs an exact fallback on the same draw.
The expected work of step (3) is bounded through Theorem 3.9(iv) by expected local counts of
fixed grids, computed under a continuous proxy and transferred by Lemma 3.13 when necessary.
The probability that step (4) runs is at most 1/B, which pays for the fallback by Lemma 3.18(a).
The structural routes differ in what plays the role of the box X and its function f , in the closure
certificate, and in the event whose small probability bounds step (4): fixed hyperplane tubes and
integer-label gaps for low-rank quadratic problems (Section 4), and growth and active-gradient
events when closure is certified by growth. Two results complete the sampled problem differently
and need no rare fallback. The lattice theorem (Theorem 8.17) stops at a predetermined level at
which the certified gap is below the spacing of the attainable objective values. The strong-noise
theorem (Theorem 8.16) pins coordinates by derivative signs and solves the remaining components
exactly on every draw. In each case the target is the exact optimum of the sampled objective.

Laws that depend only on the input length. Each theorem computes its law from the
individual base instance. In the results with polynomial sampling precision one resolution per input
length suffices, because the base-only quantities that determine the law have logarithms bounded
by a fixed polynomial in the input length and satisfy the schedule conditions below. To state this,
fix a theorem together with its fixed format constants (such as the degree and the dependency
depth of graph constraints) and its oracle implementation, let C be its class of valid base instances,
and write IΠ for the base length of Π ∈ C. A grid schedule assigns to every Π a power of two
MΠ . It is monotone if, for every power of two M ≥ MΠ , the conclusions of the theorem hold for Π
with Uσ,M in place of Uσ,MΠ , all other base-selected quantities of Π (thresholds, level cap, auxiliary
box) unchanged, and the polynomial factor of the work bound replaced by a fixed polynomial in
IΠ + log2 M . A Gaussian support schedule computes from Π, for each trial support radius 2t σ
(t = 0, 1, . . . ), an auxiliary box, a level cap, and a required accuracy bΠ (t), such that for every pair
(t, b) with b ≥ bΠ (t) and b + 20 ≤ 2t the conclusions of the theorem hold with Gb,σ and the box and
cap of trial t, the polynomial factor being a fixed polynomial in IΠ + t + b.
Corollary 3.21 (A resolution depending only on the input length). Fix a theorem as above and an
integer I ≥ 1.
 (a) Suppose its grid schedule is monotone and log2 MΠ ≤ P0 (IΠ ) for all Π ∈ C, with an effective
     nondecreasing polynomial P0 ≥ 1. Then for every Π ∈ C with IΠ ≤ I the theorem holds with
     the law Uσ,M (I) , M (I) = 2⌈P0 (I)⌉ . The perturbation model, the scale σ supplied with Π, the
     other base-selected quantities of Π, correctness on every draw, the output contract, and the
     structural and numerical factors of the expected work bound are unchanged; the polynomial
     factor becomes a polynomial in I whose exponent depends only on the exponents of P0 and of
     the original factor.
  (b) Suppose it has a Gaussian support schedule with bΠ (t) ≤ A(IΠ ) + D(IΠ ) t for all Π ∈ C and
      t ≥ 0, where A, D ≥ 1 are effective nondecreasing integer polynomials, and put
            H(I) = A(I) + D(I) + 21,          t(I) = ⌈4 log2 H(I)⌉,       b(I) = A(I) + D(I) t(I).
      Then for every Π ∈ C with IΠ ≤ I the theorem holds with Gb(I),σ and the box and cap of
      trial t(I), with the same conclusions as in (a). The support radius (b(I) + 20)σ is at most
      2t(I) σ ≤ 2H(I)4 σ.

                                                    29
  (c) Suppose that the hypotheses of (a) hold except that log2 MΠ ≤ F (K) P0 (IΠ ) is known only
      for the instances whose structural parameter is at most a supplied bound K, where F is
      an nondecreasing positive integer-valued function, computable in (1 + size(K) + F (K))cF bit
      operations for a fixed exponent cF . The supplied bound K is included in the base data. Then (a)
      holds for these instances with M (I, K) = 2⌈F (K)P0 (I)⌉ , except that the bounds on the sampled
      coefficient lengths, on the output length, and the polynomial factor of the work bound are
      multiplied by (1 + F (K))c , with c depending only on the exponents of P0 , of the original bounds,
      and cF .

     The hypotheses hold route by route as follows; the proofs are in Appendix A.7. The closure-based
grid theorems of Sections 4–8 with polynomial sampling precision, and Theorem 4.9(a), choose MΠ
as the least power of two above finitely many base-only quantities, such as 2J and inverse failure
budgets, whose logarithms are bounded by a fixed polynomial in IΠ . It uses M only through the
interval masses (2), the replacement errors of Lemma 3.13, and these lower bounds, so its schedule
is monotone and part (a) applies; for the quadratic theorems the verification is in Appendices B.6
and B.8. The lattice theorem (Theorem 8.17) is the one exception to the literal definition, because
its level cap is J = log2 M ; part (a) applies to it once the cap is reset to log2 M (I). Part (b) applies
to the Gaussian-like Theorems 4.9(b), 4.19, and 4.31. Part (c) applies to the core-only flow and
totally unimodular theorems with arbitrary core faces (Theorems 8.12 and 8.14), whose sampling
precision depends on the core size k, with a supplied K ≥ k. Taking K = I gives a law that depends
only on I, but its sampling cost need not stay within the bound for the actual core size; nothing here
gives these two theorems a law of polynomial sampling precision. The strong-noise theorem holds
for every M , but its bound depends on M through probabilities qi that are not monotone in M :
the grids of sizes 2 and 4 give the interval [−σ/2, σ/2] the masses 0 and 1/2. With a common M ,
its qi and β must be computed for that M ; its sufficient regime (41), a lower bound on σ together
with a lower bound on M , is preserved when M grows.
     Three qualifications apply. First, the hypothesis log2 MΠ ≤ P0 (IΠ ) is a property of the domains
of a route, not of fixed degree alone. The fallback factor and the tail constants of Lemma 3.17
have logarithms polynomial in I for every explicit semialgebraic instance, since they depend on the
number s of atoms only through log(s + 1), even when native integer ranges contribute exponentially
many atoms. The level caps also involve widths and curvature bounds, and on general compact
semialgebraic domains these need not have polynomial bit length: the domain 1 ≤ x0 ≤ 2, xi = x2i−1
                                                                             N
(1 ≤ i ≤ N ) has a description of length O(N log N ) and last width 22 − 1. The theorems above
use boxes, rational polytopes, explicit graph constraints of fixed dependency depth, and implicit
graphs with supplied brackets, on which these quantities have polynomial encoding length. Second,
the oracle contract of a theorem must hold for every coefficient in the support of the new law, as
the contracts in this paper do, and oracle costs remain explicit factors. Third, the common law
standardizes only the scalar law: it is rescaled by the supplied σ and applied to the coordinates
that the theorem perturbs, so it is not one probability measure for all instances, and grid and
Gaussian-like laws are not interchangeable. For Gaussian-like laws the support radius (b(I) + 20)σ
is the one that enters the original-objective bound of Proposition 2.6. Arbitrary-precision evaluation
of an output uses the same draw and does not change the law; only Corollary 3.10, whose cap
depends on the requested accuracy, needs a resolution depending on that accuracy. The corollary is
an elementary consequence of the effective bounds used in the proofs. A finite collection of routes
can share an enlarged envelope within each law family when its format constants and oracle cost
models are fixed.




                                                   30
4     Few negative directions: quadratic and mixed-integer quadratic
      problems
This section treats the first structural route, in which the nonconvexity of the objective is confined to
a small number k of directions. For a quadratic objective on a bounded polytope or mixed polytope,
k is the negative inertia of the Hessian. A rational factorization A = P − αT T T with P ⪰ 0 moves
the nonconvex part to an auxiliary space Rk , where the search of Section 3 runs. The remaining
recourse is a convex quadratic program, a convex mixed-integer quadratic program, or a separable
scalar problem, and it is solved exactly at every query.
    The principal result is Theorem 4.19. Under a finite Gaussian-like law on all original linear
coefficients, selected from the base data, it computes an exact optimizer of the sampled quadratic or
mixed-integer quadratic program on every draw. Its expected bit work is f (nz , k, 1+ν diam(P)/σ) (I+
1)C with an absolute exponent C, where nz is the number of integer variables, ν the magnitude of
the most negative eigenvalue of the Hessian, and P the polyhedral relaxation. The other results are
variants that differ in the perturbation law or the recourse structure: noise aligned with the negative
directions (Theorem 4.21), uniform noise on all coefficients, whose bound contains a power of the
dimension that grows with k (Theorem 4.20), at most two negative directions with a bound linear
in a numerical ratio (Theorem 4.9), and separable mixed recourse with arbitrarily many integer
variables (Theorems 4.29 and 4.31). Table 2 summarizes them. Every result concerns the exact
optimum of the sampled objective. Proofs are in Appendix B.

4.1   Instances, exact primitives, and factorizations
Definition 4.1 (Quadratic instances). A base instance consists of a symmetric matrix A ∈ Qn×n ,
a vector b ∈ Qn , a matrix D ∈ Qr×n and a vector e ∈ Qr such that P = {x : Dx ≤ e} is nonempty
and bounded, a number nz ∈ {0, . . . , n} of integer variables, and a rational noise width σ > 0. The
feasible set is X = P ∩ (Znz × Rn−nz ), and the objective is F (x) = 12 xT Ax + bT x. We assume X =
                                                                                                  ̸ ∅.
If this is not promised, one convex-MIQP feasibility solve detects infeasibility before any optimum,
growth threshold, or search is defined. Lower-dimensional polytopes and redundant rows are
allowed. Let n− (A) be the number of negative eigenvalues of A, counted with multiplicity, and put
ν = max{0, −λmin (A)}. A perturbation adds a linear term: ambient noise gives Fγ (x) = F (x) + γ T x
with γ ∈ Rn , and noise aligned with a factor T ∈ Qk×n gives F (x) + ξ T T x with ξ ∈ Rk ; these are
the ambient and aligned models of Definition 2.2. We write f ∗ for the minimum of the sampled
objective over X.
    Aligned noise perturbs the original coefficients by T T ξ. These coefficients are correlated and lie
in a k-dimensional subspace, so aligned noise is not independent noise on the original coefficients.
Both models are treated because they lead to different bounds.
Proposition 4.2 (Exact convex primitives). (a) A convex quadratic program min{ 12 xT P x+pT x :
    Dx ≤ e} with P ⪰ 0 and rational data of bit length Lin is solved exactly in poly(Lin ) bit
    operations: infeasibility and unboundedness are detected, and otherwise the optimal value and
    a rational optimal solution are returned [30].
 (b) A convex quadratic program with nz integer variables and the other data as in (a) is solved
     exactly, with the same outputs, in f (nz )Lcin0 bit operations for a function f and an absolute
     constant c0 [15, arXiv v2, Theorem 3].
 (c) Rational linear programs are solved exactly in polynomial time; in particular a vertex of a
     nonempty rational polytope can be found [23].

                                                   31
   When the algorithm of (b) returns an optimal solution, we fix its integer part, which lies in
the box of linear-programming bounds and therefore has polynomial bit length, and re-solve the
continuous slice by (a). This polishing returns an optimal solution of polynomial bit length, so that
the possibly large factor f (nz ) never enters the size of later instances. The factor f (nz ) is absent
when nz = 0.
Definition 4.3 (Convexifying factorization). A convexifying factorization of A is a rational number
α > 0 and a rational matrix T ∈ Qk×n with P = A + αT T T ⪰ 0. It has a supplied rational frame
constant cfr ∈ (0, 1], counted in I, if cfr Ik ⪯ T T T ⪯ Ik . The number k of rows satisfies k ≥ n− (A),
with equality for the factorization of Lemma 4.4; in theorems with a supplied factorization, k denotes
its number of rows.
    Positive semidefiniteness of P and a supplied frame constant are verified exactly by symmetric
elimination, so a supplied factorization is a checked certificate rather than a promise. No relation
between ker P and ker T is required.
Lemma 4.4 (Intrinsic rational normalization). There is a deterministic algorithm, polynomial
in the bit length of A, that either certifies A ⪰ 0, or computes rational β with ν ≤ β < 2ν and a
rational matrix T ∈ Qk×n , with k = n− (A), such that
                                     A + 2β T T T ⪰ 0,        63         T
                                                              64 Ik ⪯ T T ⪯ Ik ,

and the rows of T lie in the range of A. Thus α = 2β gives a convexifying factorization with
2ν ≤ α < 4ν and frame constant 63/64. It uses no real eigenvectors, no bound on ν, and no lower
bound on nonzero eigenvalues as input.
    The construction combines exact rational Jacobi rotations, which give an orthogonal near-
diagonalization [16], with an exact projection onto the range of A. The projection is essential. For
A = diag(−1, 0) and a unit vector t = (cos θ, sin θ) with sin θ = ̸ 0, the determinant of A + 2ttT is
−2 sin2 θ < 0, so an approximate negative eigenvector with a component in ker A does not give a
convexifier, however small that component is. For the factorization of the lemma, P = A + 2βT T T
is positive definite on the range of A and vanishes on ker A, so ker P = ker A ⊆ ker T . This inclusion
holds automatically, but none of the proofs below uses it.

4.2    The auxiliary value function
Let X ⊂ Rn be nonempty and compact, let F : X → R be continuous, let T ∈ Rk×n , and let
Λ = diag(λ1 , . . . , λk ) be positive definite; in all results except Theorem 4.31, Λ = αI. For a residual
ζ ∈ Rn and a factor coefficient ξ ∈ Rk define
                           h                                           i
           Wζ (a) = min F (x) + ζ T x + 12 (a − T x)T Λ(a − T x) ,           V (a) = Wζ (a) + ξ T a,
                     x∈X

and put γ = T T ξ + ζ. A point attaining the minimum defining Wζ (a) is an attaining witness at a.
For a quadratic F with a convexifying factorization and Λ = αI, the inner problem has the convex
objective 12 xT P x + (b + ζ − αT T a)T x up to a constant, so Wζ (a) is one convex QP or convex MIQP.
Lemma 4.5 (Auxiliary value function). In this setting:
 (a) (Square completion.) For every a,
                                 h                                                   i
                 V (a) = min Fγ (x) + 21 (a − T x + Λ−1 ξ)T Λ(a − T x + Λ−1 ξ) − 12 ξ T Λ−1 ξ.
                           x∈X

      Consequently V ∗ = inf Rk V = f ∗ − 12 ξ T Λ−1 ξ, attained at T x∗ − Λ−1 ξ for every minimizer x∗
      of Fγ , and every attaining witness xa at any a satisfies Fγ (xa ) − f ∗ ≤ V (a) − V ∗ .

                                                         32
 (b) (Upper curvature.) Wζ (a) − 12 aT Λa is concave, so Wζ and V have upper coordinate curvature
     at most (λ1 , . . . , λk ).

 (c) (Every attaining witness gives a small active vector.) For every a and every attaining witness
     xa , the vector g = Λ(a − T xa ) + ξ satisfies

        V (z) ≤ V (a) + g T (z − a) + 12 (z − a)T Λ(z − a)   (z ∈ Rk ),    g T Λ−1 g ≤ 2 V (a) − V ∗ .
                                                                                                        


 (d) (A fixed box.) If ℓi ≤ (T x)i ≤ ui for all x ∈ X and |ξi | ≤ si , then A =   i [ℓi −si /λi , ui +si /λi ]
                                                                                  Q

     contains a global minimizer of V , and minA V = V ∗ .

    Part (c) is the inequality that replaces differentiability of Wζ throughout this section. It holds
for every attaining witness, including witnesses at integer ties, on flat optimal faces, and at points
where Wζ is not differentiable. No derivative of Wζ and no selection of a favorable subgradient is
needed. For ambient noise and a factor of full row rank, the decomposition is

                  Ψ = (T T T )−1 T,     ξ = Ψγ,         ζ = ΠT γ,     ΠT = I − T T Ψ,

so that γ = T T ξ + ζ and T ζ = 0. The coordinates of ξ are in general dependent, and ξ and ζ are
dependent under uniform noise; Section 4.6 shows why this matters.

4.3   The inverse-growth route for at most two negative directions
When the sampled problem has positive point growth, the auxiliary problem inherits growth, and a
search without closure terminates exactly by rational reconstruction. This deterministic result is
combined with the growth tail.

Theorem 4.6 (Growth-conditioned exact algorithm). There is a deterministic algorithm for
continuous instances (nz = 0) with k = n− (A) ≥ 1 and rational data of bit length Lin with the
following properties.

 (a) Every output is an exact rational global minimizer together with the exact optimal value; this
     holds on every instance.

 (b) If the minimizer x∗ is unique and F (x) − F (x∗ ) ≥ g ∥x − x∗ ∥2 on √    X for some g > 0,
     the algorithm halts. With κ = 2 + 4ν/g, it processes at most 4k (2 kκ + 2)k cells per
     refinement level, stops after at most Jg ≤ poly(Lin ) + log2 (1 + κ) levels, and uses at most
            √
     ck (1 + κ)k (Jg + 1) poly(Lin + Jg ) bit operations, where ck depends only on k.

 (c) For k ≤ 2 and Z = max{1, ν/g} the bound in (b) is at most poly(Lin ) Z k/2 (1 + log Z)d0 , where
     d0 and the polynomial are absolute.

The algorithm needs neither g nor ν.

    The algorithm normalizes A by Lemma 4.4, runs the corrected-corner search of Definition 3.8 on
W0 over the box of ranges of T x, and attempts two rational reconstructions at every level: of the
optimal value from the certified interval of Theorem 3.9(v), and of the common auxiliary minimizer
T x∗ from the best corner. It accepts only a feasible witness whose value equals the reconstructed
optimal value, so a premature reconstruction is harmless. Growth enters only the termination
bound: by Lemma 4.5 and the triangle inequality, W0 (a) − f ∗ ≥ gW √    ∥a − T x∗ ∥2 with α/gW ≤ κ.
Every retained cell has a near-optimal corner in the ball of radius hj kκ/2 around T x∗ , and the
whole cell lies in that ball enlarged by its diameter.

                                                   33
Corollary 4.7 (Growth-conditioned mixed-integer algorithm). Theorem 4.6 also holds for nonempty
mixed feasible sets of Definition 4.1, with an additional factor f (nz ) in its bit-work bounds, where
f is the function of Proposition 4.2(b). A feasible rational optimizer and its exact value are
returned whenever the algorithm halts; positive point growth guarantees termination. The polynomial
exponents are independent of nz and k, and the factor ck remains dependent only on k. No growth
modulus is needed as input.

   To use Theorem 4.6 under a finite law, the growth tail must be transferred to that law with an
atom term that does not depend on the threshold.

Lemma 4.8 (Explicit growth sections for quadratics). Let F be quadratic and let X = P ∩ (Znz ×
Rn−nz ) as in Definition 4.1, and let F = Z · 2r , where Z is the number of integer points in the box
of linear-programming bounds of the integer variables (Z = 1 if nz = 0). For every ε > 0, every
coordinate i, and all fixed values of the other coefficients, the set of values of γi for which g∗ (γ) < ε
is a union of at most 8(F + 1)2 intervals. Consequently, with S = i wi the sum of the coordinate

                         Sε 16n(F + 1)2                             q

for independent coordinates with law Uσ,M and Gb,σ , respectively, for every ε > 0.

Theorem 4.9 (Expected exact work for at most two negative directions). Let the base instance be
continuous (nz = 0) with k = n− (A) ≤ 2, let S be the sum of the coordinate widths of P, and put
B = max{2, 2r } and D = 8(2r + 1)2 .

 (a) Let M be the least power of two with M ≥ 2nDB, so log2 M = O(r + log(n + 1)), and let
     γ1 , . . . , γn be independent with law Uσ,M . There is an algorithm that returns an exact rational
     minimizer of Fγ and the exact optimal value on every draw, with expected bit work at most
     (I + 1)C (1 + νS/σ) for an absolute constant C.

 (b) The same holds for independent coordinates with law Gb,σ , where b is the least positive integer
     satisfying 2b ≥ 2nDB, hence b = O(r + log(n + 1)).

Either law may use a larger resolution with log M ≤ poly(I) or b ≤ poly(I), respectively; the bit
polynomial then includes that resolution. This permits a common law with a closure theorem while
charging its sampling and coefficient lengths. The algorithm needs neither ν, S, nor the growth of
the sampled instance. For k = 0 one exact convex QP solve suffices.

   The algorithm interleaves, bit operation by bit operation, the algorithm of Theorem 4.6 and the
exact fallback of Lemma 4.11 on the same draw, and stops when either finishes. The fallback costs at
most B poly(Lin ) on every draw. With p = k/2 and Z = max{1, ν/g∗ } (Z = ∞ when g∗ = 0), the
work is at most poly(Lin ) min{B, Z p (1 + log Z)d0 } ≤ poly(Lin )(1 + p−1 log B)d0 min{B, Z p }. The
growth tail with a residual β ≤ 1/B gives P{Z > t} ≤ r0 /t + β for t ≥ 1, where r0 = νS/σ, and
therefore                                                    (
                                         Z B
                                                               2 + r0 ,        k = 1,
                 E min{B, Z p } ≤ 1 + r0     t−1/p dt + βB ≤
                                          1                    2 + r0 log B, k = 2.
All atoms and all draws with g∗ = 0 are included in the term βB.
Remark 4.10 (Why the moment argument stops at two directions). For k ≥ 3 the same integral is
r0 (B 1−2/k − 1)/(1 − 2/k), which is exponential in r. This is a limitation of the argument, not a


                                                     34
lower bound for any algorithm. It cannot be repaired by a sharper tail. Let X = [−w0 /2, w0 /2] × Y
and F (x0 , y) = G(y), where G is a quadratic with k negative eigenvalues of magnitude at most ν on
a polytope Y . For a continuous uniform perturbation, moving x0 to the other endpoint Rshows g∗ ≤
|γ0 |/w0 . Hence P{Z > t} ≥ νw0 /(σt) for t ≥ max{1, νw0 /σ}, and E min{B, Z k/2 } ≥ a0 tB0 s−2/k ds
                                                          k/2
when B > t0 , with a0 = νw0 /σ and t0 = max{1, a0 }. For fixed a0 > 0 and sufficiently large B,
this is of order (νw0 /σ)B 1−2/k for k ≥ 3 and large B. The inverse-growth power Z k/2 is therefore
not integrable to a polynomial bound beyond two negative directions, even though the tail of
Theorem 3.16 is sharp. The closure method below does not integrate an inverse growth power
and has no such restriction. The two results are not comparable: Theorem 4.9 is linear in νS/σ,
while the closure bounds are of degree k in ν diam(P)/σ. They can be applied to the same sampled
objective: part (b) of Theorem 4.9 holds for the law of Theorem 4.19 once its accuracy also satisfies
2b ≥ 2nDB, a requirement of polynomial bit length that does not depend on the support.

4.4   Exact closure of cells
Lemma 4.11 (Smallest-face candidates and the quadratic fallback). Let G(x) = 12 xT Hx + pT x be
a rational quadratic, with H symmetric of any inertia, and let Q = {x : Dx ≤ e} be a nonempty
bounded rational polytope.
 (a) There are a global minimizer x̂ of G on Q and a set S of linearly independent rows active at x̂
                            T 
     such that KS = DH D0S is nonsingular and x̂ is the x-part of KS−1 (−p; eS ).
                           S

 (b) Enumerating all row subsets, solving the nonsingular systems, keeping the feasible solutions,
     and returning one of least value finds an exact rational global minimizer in 2r poly(Lin ) bit
     operations, on every instance. Multiplier signs and definiteness tests are unnecessary.

 (c) For a mixed polytope, enumerating the Z integer points of the box of linear-programming
     bounds and applying (b) to every nonempty slice is exact in B poly(Lin ) bit operations with
     B = max{2, Z2r }.

 (d) For a box in Rk , the 3k faces (each coordinate at its lower bound, at its upper bound, or free)
     suffice in (b).
In particular, every rational QP and MIQP on a nonempty bounded mixed polytope has a rational
global minimizer, and an optimal value, of polynomial bit length.
    The proof chooses a global minimizer on a face of smallest dimension. Its tangent Hessian is
positive semidefinite; a null tangent direction would keep the objective constant up to the boundary
of the face and produce a minimizer on a smaller face. This argument handles singular Hessians,
continua of minimizers, and lower-dimensional polytopes, but it is specific to quadratics.
    Let a base instance and a convexifying factorization be fixed, with Λ = αI. A label is the integer
part z of a point of X, and the slice of z is the set of continuous parts xc with (z, xc ) ∈ X. Write
Wζz for the function Wζ with X replaced by the points of label z, so that Wζ = minz Wζz .
Lemma 4.12 (Critical pieces of the convex recourse). For every feasible label z and every rational
query (a, ζ), polynomial-time rational computations after one exact solve of the slice problem return
a set S of linearly independent rows of the slice constraints, active at an optimal slice point, such
that the following hold.
 (a) The stationarity system of the slice with active rows S is nonsingular. Its solution is affine in
     (a′ , ζ ′ ): xz,S (a′ , ζ ′ ) = ΞS a′ + YS ζ ′ + x0z,S , with multipliers λz,S (a′ , ζ ′ ) affine as well.

                                                     35
  (b) The region Rz,S (ζ ′ ) = {a′ : xz,S (a′ , ζ ′ ) feasible, λz,S (a′ , ζ ′ ) ≥ 0} contains a when ζ ′ = ζ. It
      is defined by at most r + n affine inequalities whose normals do not depend on ζ ′ and whose
      offsets are affine in ζ ′ . For every a′ ∈ Rz,S (ζ ′ ), the point xz,S (a′ , ζ ′ ) attains Wζz′ (a′ ).

  (c) The quadratic qz,S (a′ , ζ ′ ) = F (xz,S ) + ζ ′T xz,S + α2 ∥a′ − T xz,S ∥2 equals Wζz′ on the region
      and satisfies the identity ∇a′ qz,S (a′ , ζ ′ ) = α a′ − T xz,S (a′ , ζ ′ ) for all (a′ , ζ ′ ). Its Hessian
                                                                                 

      HS = α(I − T ΞS ) does not depend on ζ ′ , z, b, or e, and ∥HS ∥ ≤ Θ, where Θ = α(1 + knτT U0 ),
      τT = maxil |Til |, and U0 = (2n)! C02n with C0 the largest absolute value of the integers obtained
      by clearing a common denominator of the entries of P , D, and αT T .

There are at most R = Z · 2r possible pairs (z, S). For every feasible fixed label these regions cover
all real parameter pairs (a, ζ); only extraction at rational queries is computational. The region
description does not require full dimension, an irredundant description, or a relation between ker P
and ker T .

    The gradient identity in (c) is algebraic. It holds on all of Rk , even when the region has empty
interior; it is not obtained by differentiating Wζ along the region. At the query, xz,S (a, ζ) is an
attaining witness of Wζz (a), so Lemma 4.5(c) applies to the vector ∇qz,S (a, ζ) + ξ whenever z is a
winning label. The bound Θ uses only the matrices of the response to a′ . The sampled coefficients
enter only offsets, so Θ is fixed before sampling. Its numerical size is large, but it enters the algorithm
only through its logarithm.
    Under the usual uniqueness and regularity assumptions, affine optimizer formulas on polyhe-
dral critical regions, with quadratic value formulas, are standard in multiparametric quadratic
programming [10, 35, 44], and the reduction of structured indefinite quadratic programs to the
global minimization of a parametric convex value function, with recovery of optimizers from the
minimizing parameter, appears in Ding’s thesis [18]. Lemma 4.12 is the form of this machinery
used here: one region per query, extracted in polynomial time without enumerating regions, with a
Hessian bound that is independent of the sampled coefficients. What the expected-work theorems
add is the analysis of the cells that a region does not close.
    For a continuous instance a region certifies the value of Wζ on a whole cell. For integer variables
it certifies only the value of one label, and a second certificate is needed.

Lemma 4.13 (Best-other-label gaps). Let [ℓi , ui ] be the range of (T x)i over P, and put w̄ =
max{1, i (ui − ℓi )}. At a query v, let z∗ be a winning label and put ∆(v) = minz̸=z∗ Wζz (v) − Wζ (v),
       P

with ∆(v) = +∞ if no other label is feasible.

 (a) ∆(v) and a witness of the best other label are computed by 2nz additional exact convex MIQP
     solves, each with one extra inequality xi ≤ z∗,i − 1 or xi ≥ z∗,i + 1.

  (b) Let C be a cell with corner v and side lengths at most h. If C ⊆ Rz∗ ,S (ζ) and ∆(v) ≥ θgap h
      with θgap = αk w̄, then Wζ = qz∗ ,S (·, ζ) on C, and xz∗ ,S (a, ζ) attains Wζ (a) for every a ∈ C.

  (c) If V (v) − V ∗ ≤ 2E with E ≤ kαh2 /8 and h ≤ 1, and the gap condition of (b) fails, then two
      different labels have feasible points with Fγ ≤ f ∗ + Cgap h, where Cgap = kα/4 + θgap .

   Equality in the gap test is allowed: another label may tie inside the cell without changing
the certified value or the feasible witness. Equal winning labels at all corners do not suffice
(Example 4.15).

Lemma 4.14 (Integer-label isolation). Let Z be a nonempty finite set of integer vectors in
Qnz                                                       nz have independent coordinates with
 i=1 [Li , Ui ], let h : Z → R be arbitrary, and let γ ∈ R


                                                       36
interval concentrations πi . Let ∆ be the difference between the two smallest values of h(z) + γ T z at
distinct labels (∆ = +∞ if |Z| = 1). Then for every ε ≥ 0
                                                 nz
                                                 X
                                   P{∆ ≤ ε} ≤          (Ui − Li ) πi (2ε).
                                                 i=1

    In the application, h(z) is the optimal value of the slice of z with its continuous noise, conditioned
on that noise, so the continuous recourse may be arbitrary. The widths Ui − Li can be exponentially
large, but they enter the algorithms only through logarithms. Isolation of the best discrete solution
under random objective coefficients is classical [8, 38]; the lemma is the version used here, with
finite laws and arbitrary label costs.

Example 4.15 (Equal corner winners do not certify a label). Let z ∈ [0, 1] be continuous and
y ∈ {0, 1} integer, and let F (z, y) = 12 zy + 78 y 2 − 15                                    11
                                                        16 y, α = 1, T = (1, 1/2). Then P = 1 2 ≻ 0.
On a ∈ [0, 1] the label y = 0 has slice value 0 and the label y = 1 has slice value a2 /2 − a/2 + 1/16.
The label y = 0 wins strictly at both endpoints, and its slice formula is valid on the whole cell, but
y = 1 wins at a = 1/2. The gap test of Lemma 4.13 refuses this cell; a test based on equal corner
labels would accept it and return a wrong value. Rescaling to α = 4, T = (1/2, 1/4), and the cell
[0, 1/2] gives the same example with ∥T ∥ < 1.

Definition 4.16 (Closure search). Let a base instance, a convexifying factorization, a residual ζ, a
factor coefficient ξ, a box A as in Lemma 4.5(d), and a level cap J be given. Run the corrected-corner
search of Definition 3.8 on V over A with isotropic meshes and curvature α. At every corner v of a
processed cell, compute Wζ (v) exactly with a polished attaining witness, a winning label zv , a piece
(zv , Sv ) of Lemma 4.12, and, if nz ≥ 1, the gap ∆(v) of Lemma 4.13. A processed cell C of level j
is closed if for some corner v all corners of C lie in Rzv ,Sv (ζ) and, when nz ≥ 1, ∆(v) ≥ θgap hj . A
closed cell is minimized exactly as the quadratic qzv ,Sv (·, ζ) + ξ T a on C by Lemma 4.11(d), and the
minimizer’s affine witness is stored. If no unclosed cell is retained at some level j ≤ J, return the
witness of the incumbent. Otherwise, after level J, run the exact fallback of Lemma 4.11(c) on the
same sampled objective.

    By Lemmas 4.12 and 4.13(b), every closure step is exact, so Theorem 3.9 applies to V on A.
When the search stops before the fallback, its incumbent attains V ∗ , and by Lemma 4.5(a) the
stored witness is a global minimizer of the sampled objective. The output is therefore exact on every
draw, with a rational minimizer and value of polynomial bit length. The search trace, consisting
of closed cells with their pieces and discarded cells with their corner bounds, is a verifiable global
certificate; for nz ≥ 1 its verification includes rerunning the exact mixed-integer solves.
    The probabilistic analysis rests on one deterministic fact: a retained unclosed cell forces the
noise close to one of finitely many hyperplanes that are fixed before sampling.

Lemma 4.17 (Unresolved cells force fixed tubes). There is a family of at most K = R(r + n + 1)
affine functionals ℓκ (ξ, ζ) = uT        T
                                 κ ξ + wκ ζ + cκ with ∥uκ ∥ = 1, determined by the base data and the
factorization, with the following property. Suppose that a cell of level j is retained and not closed,
that its corner v satisfies V (v) − V ∗ √
                                        ≤ 2Ej , that zv wins at v, and that some corner of the cell lies
outside Rzv ,Sv (ζ). Then |ℓκ (ξ, ζ)| ≤ k(α + Θ)hj for some κ. If T has full row rank and ∥T ∥ ≤ 1,
then ℓκ (Ψγ, ΠT γ) = LT κ γ + cκ with a fixed vector Lκ of norm at least 1.

    If the piece matrix HS is invertible, the region boundary crossed inside the cell is mapped by the
affine map a 7→ −∇a qz,S (a, ζ) to a hyperplane in the space of ξ; by Lemma 4.5(c), ξ lies close to the
image of v. If HS is singular, the whole image of that map lies in a hyperplane. The normals are

                                                   37
fixed, and only the offsets depend on ζ, affinely. A region row with zero normal cannot be violated
in a cell that contains the query, so no exceptional case arises. The family is a proof device; the
algorithm never constructs it.
Example 4.18 (An atom that requires the fallback). Let X = [0, 1]2 , F (x, y) = xy − (x − y)/2,
T = (1/2, −1/2), α = 4, and σ = 1, so P = I. Under aligned noise the endpoint draw ξ = 1,
which belongs to every grid U1,M , gives the sampled objective xy. A direct computation gives
V (a) = −1/8 for all a ∈ [−3/4, 1/4], with two critical pieces, {x = 1/2 + 2a, y = 0} for a ≥ −1/4
and {x = 0, y = −1/2 − 2a} for a ≤ −1/4. The fixed box is [−3/4, 3/4], and the switch a = −1/4
lies at relative position 1/3, which is never a dyadic grid node. The cell containing −1/4 is therefore
never closed, and its corners are globally optimal, so it is retained at every level. The draw has
probability 1/M and is solved by the fallback. Closure alone does not make the algorithm terminate
on this draw; the fallback is necessary.

4.5    Main theorems
In the next three theorems, [ℓi , ui ] is the range of (T x)i over P, computed by linear programming.
All sampling parameters are computed from the base data before the draw, and all have bit length
polynomial in I. The algorithm of each theorem is the closure search of Definition 4.16 with a
fallback on the same draw; it never rejects or resamples a draw.
Theorem 4.19 (Gaussian-like noise on all coefficients). Let a base instance with A ̸⪰ 0 be given,
and let (α, T ), with k rows, be either the factorization of Lemma 4.4 (k = n− (A), cfr = 63/64) or a
supplied convexifying factorization with frame constant cfr . There is an algorithm that computes
from the base data, in time polynomial in I, an integer b ≤ poly(I); draws γ1 , . . . , γn independently
from Gb,σ ; and returns, on every draw, a rational global minimizer of Fγ on X and the optimal
value, or reports X = ∅. Its expected number of bit operations is at most f (nz ) C0k (1 + HG )(I + 1)C1 ,
where C0 , C1 are absolute constants, f is the function of Proposition 4.2(b) (f ≡ 1 if nz = 0), and
                                k h

     The theorem assumes neither growth, nor a unique minimizer, nor genericity, nor strict comple-
mentarity, nor full-dimensional feasibility, nor a supplied global certificate. The ratio ν diam(P)/σ
enters through its numerical value; a short binary encoding does not make a large ratio harmless.
The exponent C1 does not depend on k or nz , so the bound is fixed-parameter in (nz , k) and the
numerical ratio. The law Gb,σ is a finite rational law whose accuracy b depends on the base instance;
it is not an exact Gaussian, and the theorem does not cover other approximations of a Gaussian.

Theorem 4.21 (Noise aligned with the factor). Let a continuous base instance (nz = 0) and an

the integer coefficients independently, so the label-gap failures of Lemma 4.13(c) are not controlled.
Remark 4.22 (The original objective). By Proposition 2.6, an exact minimizer x̂ of Fγ satisfies
F (x̂) − minX F ≤ maxx,y∈X γ T (x − y), which is at most σ̄ i wi when every coefficient is bounded
                                                            P

by σ̄; for Gb,σ , σ̄ = (b + 20)σ. Under aligned noise the bound is σ̄ i (ui − ℓi ). Because the work
                                                                     P

bounds grow as σ decreases, these results do not recover the unperturbed optimizer, and they claim
no improvement of worst-case approximation bounds for the unperturbed problem.

4.6    Aligned, uniform, and Gaussian-like noise
The three laws lead to different counts because of the conditional law of the factor coefficients given
the residual. Every local event of the search,

                              Ev :     V (v) ≤ V (v ± hij ei ) + 2Ej        (i ∈ Qv ),

confines each ξi with i ∈ Qv to an interval of length at most (1 + 2k)αhij whose position depends on
ζ (Lemma 3.2 applied to Wζ ). Under aligned noise ζ = 0 and the factor coefficients are independent,
so Corollary 3.6 applies directly. Under uniform ambient noise, ξ = Ψγ is dependent on ζ = ΠT γ,
and the residual-dependent intervals must be handled by a volume estimate.

Lemma 4.23 (Dependent fiber volume). Let γ be uniform on [−σ, σ]n , let T ∈ Rk×n have full row
rank and ∥T ∥ ≤ 1, and let Q ⊆ {1, . . . , k} have t elements. For each i ∈ Q let Ii (ζ) be an interval
of length at most ℓi depending measurably on ζ = ΠT γ. Then

    The factor       n is attained even for perfectly conditioned factors.

Proposition 4.24 (Sharpness of the fiber estimate). Let n = m2 , T = m−1 (1, . . . , 1), and γ
uniform on [−σ, σ]n . Then T T T = 1, Var ξ = σ 2 /3, and for the interval I(ζ) of length ℓ ≤ 2σm
centered at the midpoint of the conditional support of ξ, P{ξ ∈ I(ζ)} = 1 − (1 − ℓ/(2σm))n , whose
                     √
ratio to ℓ tends to n/(2σ) as ℓ → 0. For k disjoint blocks of this form, in dimension N = km2 ,
the ratio of the joint probability to i ℓi /(2σ) tends to mk = (N/k)k/2 .
                                     Q


                                                      39
    A dimension-free variance of ξ does not imply dimension-free concentration on intervals that
may depend on the residual. The loss is not confined to arbitrary intervals: it occurs for the counts
of actual quadratic instances.

Proposition 4.25 (The uniform-noise counts grow with the dimension). For every k ≥ 1 and every
even m ≥ 256 there is a rational continuous instance with N = km2 variables, O(N ) inequalities,
n− (A) = k, and a convexifying factorization with α = 1, T T T = Ik , and α < 4ν, whose ranges
satisfy ui − ℓi = 8, such that under independent uniform noise on all coefficients, either continuous
on [−1, 1] or U1,M with M ≥ 32, the following hold for the meshes on the box of Theorem 4.20. At
some level the expected number of nodes satisfying the local event is at least 128−k (N/k)k/2 , and at
some level the expected number of 2Ej -nearly optimal nodes is at least 64−k (N/k)k/4 .

    The proposition is the case α = 1 of Theorem 9.4, which is proved in Appendix G.1 for a
slightly more general family. Consequently no bound of the form f (k, R)N C with an absolute
exponent C holds for either count, where R records the projected widths, the frame constant,
and the curvature-to-noise ratios. The proposition limits the counting argument, not the closure
algorithm, which may close the few quadratic pieces of these instances early. The ambient diameter
of these instances grows with m, so the proposition does not exclude a bound parameterized by the
full diameter.
    Gaussian noise removes both losses. For γ ∼ N (0, σ 2 In ), the projections ξ = Ψγ and ζ = ΠT γ
are jointly Gaussian and uncorrelated, hence independent, and Cov ξ = σ 2 (T T T )−1 . Conditioning
on ζ does not change the law of ξ, whose density obeys a product bound that depends only on the
frame constant. In addition, Lemma 4.5(c) locates the allowed values of ξi : combined with the two
neighbor comparisons it gives |ξi + α(vi − (T xv )i )| ≤ (1 + 2k)αhij /2, so ξi ∈ α[ℓi − vi , ui − vi ] +
[−(1 + 2k)αhij /2, (1 + 2k)αhij /2], an interval determined by v and the projected range rather than
by the enlarged search box.

Lemma 4.26 (Gaussian-weighted local counts). Let γ ∼ N (0, σ 2 In ), let T have frame constant cfr ,
let Λ = diag(λi ), let [ℓi , ui ] contain the range of (T x)i over X, and use balanced nested meshes on
any fixed box with the constant Ck of Definition 3.5 for the curvature vector (λ1 , . . . , λk ). At every
level j,
                                           k h
                          X               Y         −1/2            Ck λi (ui − ℓi ) i
                                 P(Ev ) ≤      2 + cfr (2Ck + 4) +        √           .
                         v∈Gj             i=1                           σ 2π

The bound does not depend on the location or the size of the box.

    The finite law Gb,σ has none of these independence properties. Its local-event probabilities are
obtained from the Gaussian proxy by Lemma 3.13, using a uniform bound on the number of intervals
in every coordinate section of a local event (Lemma B.2); its tube probabilities are transferred in
the same way.

4.7    Separable mixed recourse with rational breakpoints
When the convex part is separable and the domain is a product, the recourse decomposes into scalar
problems. Integer winners are then certified globally by neighbor inequalities, and the number of
integer variables no longer enters the complexity.

Definition 4.27 (Separable instances). The domain is X = nj=1 Xj , where Xj = [ℓj , uj ] with
                                                                    Q

ℓj < uj (continuous) or Xj = Z ∩ [ℓj , uj ] with at least two points (integer); integer endpoints are
rounded inward, empty domains are rejected, and fixed coordinates are removed beforehand, their

                                                   40
contributions being absorbed into the remaining data. Each φj is a continuous convex function
on [ℓj , uj ] given by rational breakpoints ℓj = κj0 < · · · < κjsj = uj and rational quadratics
ψjm (t) = 12 cjm t2 + µjm t (plus constants) with cjm ≥ 0 on [κj,m−1 , κjm ]. The objective is
                                 n

The bit length I counts all pieces, breakpoints, and binary endpoints. Continuity, the curvature
signs, and the order of the one-sided derivatives at the breakpoints are checked exactly.

    Rational breakpoints are essential. The function max{0, x2 − 2} on [0, 2] has rational quadratic
                                                                                           √
pieces, but max{0, x2 − 2} − x has its unique minimizer at the irrational breakpoint 2. The
parameters k and α are those of the supplied concave term, not the negative inertia or curvature of
the whole objective, and the product domain excludes coupling constraints.

Lemma 4.28 (Scalar recourse). For λ ∈ R consider mint∈Xj [φj (t) − λt].

 (a) If Xj is integer, put δj (t) = φj (t + 1) − φj (t), which is nondecreasing. A point t ∈ Xj is
     optimal if and only if δj (t − 1) ≤ λ ≤ δj (t), where a condition involving a point outside Xj is
     omitted. An optimal t is found by binary search with O(log(uj − ℓj + 2)) evaluations of φj .

 (b) If Xj is continuous, the following at most 2sj +1 states cover all λ ∈ R, and each certifies global

     for λ between the derivatives of ψjm at κj,m−1 and κjm ; and for every breakpoint κjm , the
     constant state t = κjm for λ between the left and right derivatives of φj at κjm , with −∞ and
     +∞ replacing the missing derivatives at ℓj and uj .

Theorem 4.29 (Separable recourse: aligned and uniform noise). Let a separable instance with
k ≥ 1 be given, and let [ℓi , ui ] be the range of (T x)i over X.

 (a) Under ξ1 , . . . , ξk drawn independently from Uσ,M , with M computed from the base data, there

     Theorem 4.21.

 (b) If T has full row rank and ∥T ∥ ≤ 1, the same holds under γ1 , . . . , γn drawn independently
     from Uσ,M , with the bound of Theorem 4.20 without the factor f (nz ).

The number of integer variables is not a parameter.

                                                         41
   A general spectral normalization of the separable objective would destroy the separability of
the convex part. A normalization that only rotates and rescales the rows of the supplied factor
preserves it exactly, at the price of an anisotropic auxiliary metric.
Lemma 4.30 (Anisotropic row normalization). Let T ∈ Qk×n have full row rank and put β = α ∥T ∥2 .
In polynomial time one computes an exactly orthogonal rational Q and dyadic numbers d1 , . . . , dk > 0
such that U = diag(di )−1 QT and Λ = diag(αd2i ) satisfy

                     U T ΛU = αT T T,         1          T
                                              16 Ik ⪯ U U ⪯ Ik ,      max λi < 8β.
                                                                        i

    The identity U T ΛU = αT T T is exact. The approximation in the Jacobi rotations is used only
to bound the frame; it is neither discarded nor moved into the convex part. Small singular values of
T affect only the bit lengths of the transformed data.
Theorem 4.31 (Separable recourse: Gaussian-like noise). Let a separable instance with k ≥ 1 and
T of full row rank after preprocessing be given; no norm or conditioning promise on T is needed. Let
U and Λ be as in Lemma 4.30 and let [ℓi , ui ] be the range of (U x)i over X. There is an algorithm
that computes from the base data an integer b ≤ poly(I), draws γ1 , . . . , γn independently from Gb,σ ,
and returns an exact rational minimizer of Fγ and the optimal value on every draw, with expected
bit work at most C0k (1 + HΛ )(I + 1)C1 , where, with Ck = 1 + 8k,
                   k h
                   Y                        Ck λi (ui − ℓi ) i h           8Ck β diam(X) ik
            HΛ =         2 + 4(2Ck + 4) +         √           ≤ 8Ck + 18 +       √          .
                   i=1                          σ 2π                           σ 2π

Thus the expected work is f (k, 1+β diam(X)/σ)(I +1)C1 with an absolute exponent and no parameter
for the number of integer variables.
    The search uses meshes balanced by the curvatures: dyadic scales ρi with 1 ≤ λi ρ2i < 4 give
cbal < 4 in Definition 3.5, so that no ratio of largest to smallest curvature enters the count. The
parameter β is the curvature of the supplied concave term; it can be much larger than the negative
curvature of the whole objective. The theorem does not identify a minimum-rank representation or
remove dependent rows of T .

4.8   Summary of the regimes
The polynomial exponents are absolute in all rows. The Gaussian-like theorem is the strongest
general statement. The aligned theorem is the simplest and needs no normalization, but its noise
is low-dimensional. The two-direction theorem has a different and, for k ≤ 2, possibly sharper
numerical dependence. The separable theorems trade a restricted convex part for an unrestricted
number of integer variables.


5     Sparse polynomial optimization
This section develops the sparse-bag route. The objective is a sum of explicit polynomial factors
of fixed degree on a bounded mixed box, and every factor scope lies in a bag of a supplied tree
decomposition with at most p coordinates per bag. Every coordinate receives an independent
linear coefficient from one finite rational law chosen before sampling, that is, the ambient model of
definition 2.2 with marginal law Uσ,M . No convexity, uniqueness, growth, nondegeneracy, or bound
on the integer dimension or the negative inertia is assumed.
    The algorithm follows the common plan of the paper.

Table 2: Exact algorithms for sampled problems with few negative directions. All are correct on
every draw and output rational minimizers and values of polynomial bit length. In the uniform-noise
row, the power of n is a property of the counting surrogate (Proposition 4.25). For the intrinsic
factorization, α < 4ν.

  (i) Curvature-corrected refinement of nested bag cells preserves every global optimizer. The
      correction is one global rounding allowance (section 5.2).
 (ii) Independent linear noise bounds the expected number of retained bag cells at every mesh by
      a quantity that does not depend on the mesh (section 5.3).
 (iii) A certified local convexity test on a box that contains every global optimizer finishes the
       sampled problem exactly (section 5.5).
 (iv) A base-selected finite law makes failure of this test rare enough to pay for an exact fallback on
      the same draw (section 5.6).
Theorem 5.2 states the result for fixed-degree polynomials on mixed boxes. Quadratic and mixed-
integer quadratic objectives are the case d = 2; their output is rational on every draw (corollary 5.19).
Section 6 extends the method to feasible sets that are not boxes.

5.1      Setting and main result
Definition 5.1 (Sparse mixed polynomial instance). A sparse mixed polynomial instance consists
of the following rational data.
  (i) A mixed box X = ni=1 Xi after the preprocessing of section 2.1: Xi = [ℓi , ui ] for i ∈ IC
                             Q

      and Xi = [ℓi , ui ] ∩ Z with integers ℓi < ui for i ∈ IZ , where [n] = IC ∪ IZ , n ≥ 1, and every
      width wi = ui − ℓi is positive. Write X    b = Q [ℓi , ui ] for the continuous hull, W = P wi ,
                                                         i                                         i
      wmax = maxi wi , nc = |IC |, nz = |IZ |, and NZ = i∈IZ (wi + 1) for the number of integer
                                                             Q

      assignments.
 (ii) Factors fa , a ∈ A, each an explicit list of monomials with rational coefficients, of total degree
      at most d, with variable scope Sa ⊆ [n]. The objective is F0 = a fa .
                                                                       P

 (iii) A tree decomposition: a tree T whose nodes are bags β ⊆ [n], such that every coordinate and
       every scope Sa lies in some bag and, for every i, the bags containing i form a subtree. The
       bag size is p = maxβ |β|.

                                                   43
 (iv) A noise half-width σ > 0 and a curvature bound L > 0 with

                                   ∂ii F0 (x) ≤ L         for all x ∈ X
                                                                      b and i ∈ [n].                              (3)

The base length I is the binary length of these data. The degree d ≥ 1 is fixed.

    The curvature bound is needed on the whole continuous hull, including the segments between
adjacent integer labels, because the rounding and comparison arguments move one coordinate
along such segments. Inputs with fixed coordinates reduce to this form by substitution, which does
not increase degrees, scopes or (3); the sampled coefficient of a substituted coordinate becomes
an additive constant, which is carried separately and added to reported values. If no coordinate
remains, the feasible set is one rational point. The algorithm then returns this point and its sampled
value before computing any schedule (step (0) of definition 5.17), so every maximum, width sum
and division below concerns n ≥ 1 coordinates of positive width.

Status of the premises. The tree decomposition is verified in time polynomial in I. The bound
(3) is promised: correctness of the pruning and of the patch certificate relies on it. It can always be
verified instead of promised. With R0 = max{1, maxi |ℓi | , maxi |ui |} and F0 = α cα xα ,
                                                                                   P

                                                                                 |α|−2
                                             n            X                              o

satisfies (3) and has encoding length polyd (I), because xα−2ei ≤ R0          on Xb when αi ≥ 2. Its
numerical value can be much larger than a sharper supplied bound, and it is the numerical value of L
that enters the work bound. A sharper bound used in an independently checkable proof record must
be certified, with its verification cost added to the work. No general test of polynomial inequalities
is assumed. The same monomial method gives rational numbers

Noise and growth. The coefficients γ1 , . . . , γn are independent with law Uσ,M of definition 2.1,
Fγ = F0 + γ T x, and f ∗ = minX Fγ ; the minimum exists because X is compact. The point-growth
modulus g∗ (γ) of definition 3.15 is the largest g ≥ 0 such that some minimizer a satisfies

                                 Fγ (x) − Fγ (a) ≥ g ∥x − a∥22             (x ∈ X).                               (6)

The set of admissible g is closed, so the largest one exists. If g∗ (γ) > 0 the minimizer is unique; if
Fγ has two distinct minimizers, g∗ (γ) = 0.

Outputs. On ordinary draws the algorithm returns a patch output, which has the form of the
implicit patch output of definition 2.5(b). On the remaining draws it returns the exact output
of the fallback of theorem 3.20. A patch output is a quadruple Π = (S, x̄S , P, g0 ): a set S ⊇ IZ ;
rational values x̄i ∈ Xi for i ∈ S, each of which is an integer label, an original continuous bound, or
a value shared by every global minimizer because the closure test found a one-point hull interval



                                                          44
                                           / [ai , bi ] with ℓi ≤ ai < bi ≤ ui , which plays the role of
                                                Q
(proposition 5.12); a rational box P = i∈S
the box Q of definition 2.5(b); and a rational g0 > 0 for which the run has verified
                           ∇2S̄ S̄ Fγ (x̄S , y) ⪰ g0 I    (y ∈ P ),     S̄ = [n] \ S.                    (7)
It denotes the unique minimizer ŷ of Fγ (x̄S , ·) on P , and the run also proves that x̂ = (x̄S , ŷ) is the
unique global minimizer of Fγ on X. If S = [n], the box P is empty and x̂ = x̄. Fixing a continuous
coordinate at a singleton hull value is justified by containment of every global minimizer in the hull,
not by a derivative sign, and it changes neither the uniqueness argument nor the evaluation contract.
The patch output has length polyd (I). The global proof record of the run consists of the candidate
lists, incumbents and cell bounds of every level, and the data of the closure tests; it proves that
every global minimizer lies in {x̄S } × P .
Theorem 5.2 (Sparse polynomial optimization on mixed boxes). Fix d ≥ 1. For sparse mixed
polynomial instances (definition 5.1) there is an algorithm and a power of two M , computed from the
base instance with log2 M ≤ polyd (I), such that for the ambient perturbation model with marginal
law Uσ,M the following hold.
  (i) On every draw the algorithm returns an exact global optimizer of Fγ on X. On ordinary
      draws the output is a patch output of length polyd (I) together with its global proof record. On
      the remaining draws, which have probability at most 1/(2B), it is the exact output of the
      fallback of theorem 3.20, computed on the same draw, where B is a base-only factor with
      log B ≤ polyd (I).
 (ii) The expected bit work, including the computation of M and the sampling, and the expected
      length and verification work of the global proof record are at most
                                            h       (1 + n/2)Lwmax ip
                                        C0p 4 +                       polyd (I),                         (8)
                                                          2σ
      where C0 is an absolute constant.
(iii) For every integer q ≥ 0, a q-bit evaluation of the output returns a rational point y ∈ X
      with ∥y − x̂∥2 ≤ 2−q , where x̂ is the returned optimizer, and rationals a and Fγ (y) with
      a ≤ f ∗ ≤ Fγ (y) ≤ a + 2−q . It costs polyd (I + q) bit operations for a patch output and
      B polyd (I + q) for a fallback output; its expected cost over the draw is polyd (I + q).
The validity of L is the only promised premise, and it is verified when L = Lmon . No growth,
uniqueness or nondegeneracy premise is used; such conditions enter only the analysis of the stopping
depth.
    The bound (8) is polynomial for each fixed p when Lwmax /σ is polynomially bounded in I. It
is a fixed-width polynomial bound in the sense of section 2.5, not a fixed-parameter bound in p:
the bracket contains n, so the bound grows as np for fixed numerical scales. Section 5.4 explains
where this factor comes from, and theorem 9.1 shows that it is necessary for the retention rule used
here. The integer widths wi enter through their magnitudes, so huge binary-encoded integer ranges
are not covered at polynomial cost; they enter the stopping depth, the sampling precision and the
fallback factor only logarithmically. The theorem concerns the sampled objective. Its consequence
for the base objective is the regret bound of proposition 2.6 with ωX (γ) ≤ σW . Choosing σ for a
target accuracy ε multiplies the numerical ratio in (8) by a factor of order W/ε (section 2.6); the
resulting work is polynomial in 1/ε when the other numerical parameters are fixed. Finally, the
finite law matters: example 2.7 is an instance in this class on which a tie occurs with probability
1/M , so no patch certificate can succeed on every draw and the fallback is necessary.

                                                         45
5.2   Nested bag cells and sparse dynamic programming
Grids and cells. Let s be the least power of two with s ≥ max{1, wmax } and hj = s2−j for j ≥ 0.
For i ∈ IC , and for i ∈ IZ while hj ≥ 1, the level-j nodes of coordinate i are

                         Gi,j = {ℓi + khj : k ∈ Z≥0 , ℓi + khj < ui } ∪ {ui },

and a coordinate cell is [a, a′ ] ∩ Xi for consecutive nodes a < a′ . For i ∈ IZ and hj < 1, the nodes
are Gi,j = Xi and the coordinate cells are the singletons {a}, a ∈ Xi . While hj ≥ 1, hj is an integer,
so integer nodes are integers. The endpoints of a cell are a, a′ , or a for a singleton. The level-j grid
is Gj = i Gi,j ⊆ X.
         Q


Lemma 5.3 (Coordinate cells). For every coordinate i and level j:

 (a) the coordinate cells cover Xi , and a node contained in a cell is an endpoint of it;

 (b) every node is an endpoint of at most two cells, and of exactly one if the cells are singletons;

 (c) every cell has width at most hj ;

 (d) every level-(j + 1) cell lies in a level-j cell, and every level-j cell is the union of at most two
     level-(j + 1) cells.

Proof. Consecutive nodes differ by at most hj , and the first and last nodes are ℓi and ui ; this
gives (a) and (c) for interval cells, and (b) holds because a node is the right endpoint of at most
one cell and the left endpoint of at most one cell. For (d), hj+1 = hj /2 gives Gi,j ⊆ Gi,j+1 , and
between consecutive level-j nodes a < a′ there is at most one level-(j + 1) node, namely a + hj+1
if a + hj+1 < a′ . When hj = 1 > hj+1 for i ∈ IZ , the level-j cells are {a, a + 1}, the union of the
singleton cells {a} and {a + 1}; singleton cells are never refined further.
                                                        Q
    A bag cell of a bag β at level j is a product C = i∈β Ci of level-j coordinate cells; its corners
are the tuples of endpoints, and its children are the level-(j + 1) bag cells contained in it. By
lemma 5.3, a cell has at most 2|β| corners and at most 2|β| children, its children cover it, and a tuple
of nodes is a corner of at most 2|β| cells. The algorithm keeps for every bag a candidate list Wj (β)
of level-j bag cells. It determines the physical domain

                   Ωj = {x ∈ X : for every bag β, xβ ∈ C for some C ∈ Wj (β)},

the row list Rj (β), the set of corners of the cells in Wj (β), and the allowed grid Aj = {y ∈ Gj :
yβ ∈ Rj (β) for every β}. The physical domain is used only in the proofs; the algorithm stores cells
and rows.

Lemma 5.4 (Allowed grid). Aj = Gj ∩ Ωj .

Proof. Corners of a cell lie in the cell, so Aj ⊆ Ωj . Conversely, if y ∈ Gj ∩ Ωj and yβ ∈ C ∈ Wj (β),
each yi , i ∈ β, is a node contained in Ci , hence an endpoint of Ci by lemma 5.3(a), so yβ is a corner
of C.




                                                   46
Sparse dynamic programming. We may assume that every node of T has degree at most
three: a bag with children β1 , . . . , βk , k ≥ 3, is replaced by a path of k − 1 copies of itself, the lth
copy having child βl and the last copy also βk . Copies preserve the subtree property and the bag
size and at most double the number of nodes [29]. Copies of one bag have identical candidate lists
throughout, because their cell bounds below coincide. Assign every factor fa and every noise term
γi xi to one bag containing its scope, and let ϕβ be the sum of the terms assigned to β, so that
Fγ = β ϕβ (xβ ). For a row v ∈ Rj (β) let
       P


                                mβ (v) = min{Fγ (y) : y ∈ Aj , yβ = v}

be its min-marginal, with min ∅ = +∞, and let mj = minAj Fγ .

the minimizations over the different subtrees are independent and constrained only through their
                                                                                P
separators. The same argument with all neighbors gives mβ (v) = ϕβ (v) + β ′′ µβ ′′ →β (vβ ′′ ∩β ), the
sum over all neighbors; every coordinate lies in some bag, so the assignments are complete. Messages
are computed toward a root and back, recomputing each outgoing sum from at most two incoming
messages instead of subtracting, so that no difference of infinities occurs. Each row is processed a
bounded number of times, and stored argmin pointers give a minimizer.

    This is nonserial dynamic programming on a tree decomposition [11], applied to sparse row
lists. Dense tables would enumerate products of coordinate grids; here each row is a corner of a
retained cell, and adjacent row lists are never multiplied. Every message value is a sum of at most
one assigned cost per bag, so its bit length is polynomial in the bit length of the costs and the
number of bags, independently of the number of rows.

Rounding and pruning. The next lemma is the deterministic core of the method. It rounds all
coordinates at once and keeps the result inside every candidate list.
Lemma 5.6 (Fixed-cell rounding). Let x ∈ Ωj , and let C0 ∈ Wj (β0 ) be a cell with xβ0 ∈ C0 . There
is a random point Y ∈ Aj such that Yβ0 is a corner of C0 , Yi = xi whenever xi ∈ Gi,j , and

Proof. For each i with xi ∈ Gi,j put Yi = xi . Otherwise xi lies strictly between consecutive nodes
ai < a′i ; this happens only for i ∈ IC or for i ∈ IZ with hj ≥ 1, so ai , a′i ∈ Xi . Let Yi = a′i with
probability (xi − ai )/(a′i − ai ) and Yi = ai otherwise, independently over i. Then EYi = xi and
Var Yi = (xi − ai )(a′i − xi ) ≤ h2j /4.
     Let β be a bag and C ∈ Wj (β) a cell with xβ ∈ C. For i ∈ β, if xi is a node then Yi = xi ∈ Ci is

Taking expectations and summing over k gives (9).

   For a quadratic, (9) also follows from cancellation of the off-diagonal terms in expectation. The

rounding error is not a sum of diagonal terms.
   For a cell C ∈ Wj (β) define its cell bound

                                   qC = min{mβ (v) : v a corner of C}.

The incumbent Uj is the least value of Fγ at the allowed grid points produced by the dynamic
programs of levels 0, . . . , j; it is the value of a point of X, and Uj ≤ mj . Initialize U−1 = +∞ and
store a feasible point attaining the smallest value so far, keeping an earlier point when its value
remains smaller. Update this incumbent pair before pruning. The candidate list is updated by the
retention rule

      Wj′ (β) = {C ∈ Wj (β) : qC − Ej ≤ Uj },              Wj+1 (β) = {children of cells in Wj′ (β)},        (10)

starting from W0 (β) = {Xβ }. Ties are retained. Let Ω′j be the physical domain of the retained lists;
it equals Ωj+1 , because children cover their parents.

Proposition 5.7 (Sound pruning and witnesses). On every draw and at every level j ≥ 0:

 (a) every global minimizer x∗ of Fγ on X lies in Ωj , and x∗β lies in a retained cell of every bag;

  (b) f ∗ ≤ Uj ≤ mj ≤ f ∗ + Ej ;

  (c) every x ∈ Ωj \ Ω′j satisfies Fγ (x) > Uj ;

 (d) every retained cell C ∈ Wj′ (β) has a corner v and a point y ∈ Aj with yβ = v and Fγ (y) =
     qC ≤ f ∗ + 2Ej .

Proof. We first note the cell lower bound. Let C ∈ Wj (β) and let x ∈ Ωj have xβ ∈ C. Lemma 5.6
with (β, C) gives Y ∈ Aj with Yβ a corner of C, so Fγ (Y ) ≥ mβ (Yβ ) ≥ qC on every outcome, and

                                               qC − Ej ≤ Fγ (x).                                             (11)



                                                         48
    We prove (a) by induction; Ω0 = X. Suppose every minimizer lies in Ωj , and let x∗ be one.
For each bag β choose C ∈ Wj (β) containing x∗β . By (11), qC − Ej ≤ f ∗ ≤ Uj , so C is retained,
and its children cover it; hence x∗ ∈ Ωj+1 . For (b), Uj ≥ f ∗ because Uj is a value at a point
of X, and rounding a minimizer by lemma 5.6 gives an allowed point of expected value at most
f ∗ + Ej , so mj ≤ f ∗ + Ej . For (c), x ∈ Ωj \ Ω′j lies in some cell C ∈ Wj (β) but in no retained
cell of β, so C was deleted, and (11) gives Fγ (x) ≥ qC − Ej > Uj . For (d), qC ≤ Uj + Ej < ∞, so
qC = mβ (v) for some corner v, and the minimum defining mβ (v) is attained at some y ∈ Aj . By
(b), qC ≤ Uj + Ej ≤ f ∗ + 2Ej .

    Part (a) holds for tied and positive-dimensional optimal sets and for every draw. Part (d) gives
one globally consistent feasible point for each retained cell; the counting argument below needs
exactly this, and a collection of separate per-bag witnesses would not suffice. Part (c) is the global
certificate: a point removed at level j has value above Uj ≥ f ∗ . An incumbent found at an earlier
level may lie outside the current physical domain; it is still feasible and remains a valid upper bound.
