    The expectation is taken over the complete deterministic bag grid, and every retained cell is
charged to a near-optimal node of that grid on every draw. The argument therefore does not assume
that the adaptively retained cells are independent of the noise. It is the bag-conditional analogue
of theorem 3.4 and theorem 3.9(iv). The proof above is self-contained because the grids here are
clipped at the upper bounds and the count is conditioned on the noise outside the bag; the clipped
nodes are treated as exceptional and are never used in comparisons. The requirement M ≥ 2J is
what makes the count independent of the level: for a fixed coarse grid, the atomic term wi /(ai M )
would grow without bound as hj → 0.

5.4    Why the bound is fixed-width but not fixed-parameter
The tree decomposition controls the shape of the dynamic program: tables are indexed by bag rows,
messages by separator tuples, and the number of rows of a bag is charged to Θβ , a product over
the coordinates of that bag only. Treewidth therefore governs the exponent in (8). The base of the
exponential, however, contains the total dimension n. It enters through the rounding allowance: the
cell bound (11) must hold for every feasible point of a cell, and its proof rounds all n coordinates of a
global point simultaneously, so the allowance Ej = nLh2j /8 is a sum of variances over all coordinates.
The witnesses of proposition 5.7(d) are near-optimal only up to 2Ej , and lemma 5.8 turns this
tolerance into noise intervals of length (1 + n/2)Lai for every coordinate of the bag. The resulting


                                                            50
bound on the expected number of retained rows of a bag is of order (nLwmax /σ)|β| when this ratio
is large, which is polynomial in n only for fixed p.
     Two facts show that the factor belongs to the method as specified, not only to its analysis. First,
replacing Ej by an allowance for the bag alone, |β| Lh2j /8, is unsound: proposition 9.2 gives a star
quadratic with bags of size two on which the local allowance deletes the cell that contains every
global optimizer, on every draw of small noise. The error that the global allowance absorbs comes
from minimizing over the grid outside the bag; it varies with the bag’s own coordinates and does
not cancel when min-marginals are compared. Second, for the retention rule (10) and closure on
the whole hull, the factor nΩ(p) actually occurs: theorem 9.1 gives connected instances of treewidth
p − 1, with L = 2, unit widths and σ = 1/100, on which a bag retains more than (5n/(6p))p/2 cells
on every draw before the stopping depth is reached. Both results concern the retention rule and the
bag-local substitution analyzed here. They are not lower bounds for other algorithms.

Certified conditional values. The factor n leaves the count when the true conditional value
of a bag tuple is available with a certified error proportional to the bag size rather than to the
dimension. For a bag β and v ∈ X b β write

                                          Wβ (v) = Vβ (v) + γβT v,

with Vβ from (12), a minimum over the original outside domain. The next proposition gives the
count of a modified search in which an oracle for Wβ replaces the grid min-marginals. It is a
sufficient interface, not an algorithm: an oracle that meets it must account for the optimization
over every coordinate outside the bag, and none is supplied here for general sparse instances.

Proposition 5.10 (Conditional-recourse count). Consider a sparse mixed polynomial instance with
the coordinate cells of section 5.2. Let a ≥ 0, ej = pLh2j /8, and 0 ≤ ηj ≤ aej . Modify the refinement
and retention steps of definition 5.17 as follows. At level j an oracle receives every corner v of every
candidate cell of every bag β and returns rationals ℓβ (v) ≤ Wβ (v) ≤ uβ (v) with uβ (v) − ℓβ (v) ≤ ηj ,
together with a point xv ∈ X such that xvβ = v and Fγ (xv ) ≤ uβ (v). The incumbent Uj is the least
value uβ (v) returned at levels 0, . . . , j, including every call of level j. Initialize U−1 = +∞, store its
associated feasible completion, and update the incumbent before pruning. A candidate cell C of β is
retained if and only if min{ℓβ (v) : v a corner of C} − ej ≤ Uj . Then the following hold.

 (a) On every draw and at every level, for every global minimizer x∗ and every bag β, the projection
     x∗β lies in a retained cell of β.

  (b) On every draw, f ∗ ≤ Uj ≤ f ∗ + ej + ηj , and every retained cell of β has a corner v and its
      returned feasible completion xv with Wβ (v) ≤ Fγ (xv ) ≤ f ∗ + 2(ej + ηj ).

  (c) Let J ≥ 0, M ≥ 2J and 0 ≤ j ≤ J. On every draw the number of retained cells of β at level j
      is at most 2|β| times the number of tuples v ∈ Gj,β with Wβ (v) ≤ f ∗ + 2(ej + ηj ), and under
      the law Uσ,M the expected number of these tuples is at most
                                         Yh               (1 + a)p  Lwi i
                                               4+ 1+                        .                           (14)
                                         i∈β
                                                               2      2σ

The oracle’s answers and errors may depend on the draw; the count uses the fixed envelope aej , not
independence of the queried errors.



                                                      51
Proof. Fix γO , a coordinate i ∈ β and the other bag coordinates. The function θ 7→ Vβ (v + θei ) is a
minimum over z ∈ XO of functions whose second derivative is at most L on the continuous hull,
so θ 7→ Wβ (v + θei ) − Lθ2 /2 is concave there, being a minimum of concave functions plus a linear
term. Moreover f ∗ = minXβ Wβ , with equality at x∗β for every global minimizer x∗ .
    (a) We argue by induction over the levels; at level 0 the only cell of β is Xβ . Let C be a candidate
cell of β that contains x∗β . Round every coordinate i ∈ β of x∗β independently to the endpoints of the
coordinate cell Ci with mean x∗i , as in the proof of lemma 5.6, a coordinate that is a node staying
fixed. The result Y is a corner of C and Var Yi ≤ h2j /4. Rounding one coordinate at a time, the
concavity just shown and Jensen’s inequality give EWβ (Y ) ≤ Wβ (x∗β ) + |β| Lh2j /8 ≤ f ∗ + ej . Some
corner v of C therefore has ℓβ (v) − ej ≤ Wβ (v) − ej ≤ f ∗ , and f ∗ ≤ Uj because Uj bounds the value
of a feasible point. Hence C is retained, and its child that contains x∗β is a candidate at level j + 1.
    (b) The corner v found in (a) is queried at level j, so Uj ≤ uβ (v) ≤ ℓβ (v) + ηj ≤ f ∗ + ej + ηj .
                                                                                           ′
A retained cell has a corner v ′ with ℓβ (v ′ ) ≤ Uj + ej , and then Wβ (v ′ ) ≤ Fγ (xv ) ≤ uβ (v ′ ) ≤
ℓβ (v ′ ) + ηj ≤ f ∗ + 2(ej + ηj ).
    (c) The first claim follows from (b), since a tuple of nodes is a corner of at most 2|β| cells.
Dominate the qualifying tuples by the full grid count with the deterministic tolerance δ = 2(1 + a)ej ,
which bounds 2(ej + ηj ) on every draw. Repeat the proof of proposition 5.9 with this tolerance
in place of 2Ej . Lemma 5.8 uses only Vβ and the curvature bound, so it confines the coefficient
of every regular coordinate, given γO and v, to an interval of length at most Lai + 2δ/ai ≤
Lai + (1 + a)pLh2j /(2ai ) ≤ Lai (1 + (1 + a)p/2), because ai ≥ hj . The rest of that proof is unchanged
and gives (14).

    Compared with Θβ of (13), the dimension n in the bracket is replaced by (1 + a)p, so the count
depends only on the bag size, the error factor a and the numerical ratios Lwi /σ. Whether the
whole search becomes fixed-parameter in the width depends on the cost of the oracle, which the
proposition does not address. The grid min-marginals of section 5.2 do not meet the interface
(proposition 9.2). Exact conditional values can require optimizing over almost the whole instance,
and dividing a global allowance among the outside coordinates would force a finer outside grid and
a dimension-dependent state count elsewhere. The recourse route of section 7 meets the interface
for one search block, a small core whose conditional problem is assumed tractable; its counts are
the case of a single bag. Certified conditional values are one sufficient way to remove the factor
n. The barriers above do not show that they are necessary for every algorithm whose bound is
fixed-parameter in the width. Parts (a) and (b) are a deterministic conditional-recourse filter that
also appears in an unpublished companion manuscript [14]; part (c), the expected count under the
finite law, is what the noise adds.

Deterministic bounds under a growth condition. The companion manuscript [14] de-
velops an alternative deterministic analysis under a quadratic-growth premise. Write Li =
max{0, maxx ∂ii F (x)} and L = maxi Li . Point growth F (x) − F (x∗ ) ≥ g ∥x − x∗ ∥2 defines
κ = max{1, L/g}. Weighted growth instead uses F (x) − F (x∗ ) ≥ γ i Li (xi − x∗i )2 and defines
                                                                            P

κ̄ = max{1, 1/γ∗ } for the largest valid γ∗ . Point growth implies κ̄ ≤ κ, but weighted growth places
no requirement on coordinates with Li = 0. Its approximation theorem certifies accuracy 2−q in
f (p, κ̄)(I + q + 1)5 bit operations, where
                                                      √
                                   f (p, t) = c0 (c1 p t)p t(1 + log2 t)2 .

The condition parameter is not an algorithm input: trials stop when a valid certificate is obtained.
Its exact-output theorem separately assumes point growth
                                                       √      and a unique minimizer, and gives
                C
f1 (p, κ)(I + 1) bit operations. Graded grids use O( κ̄ log(n + 2)) nodes per coordinate; the
                 1




                                                   52
                                                     p
companion’s uniform-grid family retains at least (n − 2)κ + 1 nodes in its specified stages. This is a
limitation of that filtering rule. Under the randomized exponential-time hypothesis, its product-time
lower bound rules out f (p)(κI)p/ψ(p) for unbounded ψ in the stated integer-quadratic model. It
does not establish a matching p/2 + O(1) lower exponent or rule out every smaller linear exponent
in κ. These are bounds with a numerical condition parameter, not bounds in width alone. We do
not analyze graded grids under noise.
    These deterministic bounds do not by themselves give an expected bound that is fixed-parameter
in the width under the noise models of this paper. Their work has a high power of the inverse
growth ratio. Under the noise, Z = L/g∗ (with Z = +∞ when g∗ = 0) satisfies only the estimate
P{Z > t} ≤ A0 /t + ϵM , where A0 = LW/σ and ϵM = 2nCtail /M , by lemma 5.14. For r > 1 and
K ≥ 1, integration gives
                                                    rA0
                           E min{K, Z r } ≤ 1 +         (K 1−1/r − 1) + ϵM K.
                                                    r−1
Thus the linear small-growth tail alone does not bound high inverse-growth moments without a
cutoff-dependent factor. The cutoff-dependent power already arises in the flat noisy-coordinate
example of remark 4.10. When the cutoff is paid by an exact fallback, K can be exponential in the
input length. The count of proposition 5.9 avoids inverse-growth moments: it bounds the retained
cells of every draw by near-optimal nodes of a fixed grid, whose expected number is controlled by the
linear noise alone, and growth enters only the stopping depth, logarithmically, and the probability of
the fallback. The price is the factor n in the bracket. This comparison concerns the two arguments;
it is not a lower bound for other algorithms, and whether some exact algorithm has expected work
fixed-parameter in the width under independent linear noise remains open (section 10.3).

5.5   Certified closure on a strongly convex patch
After the retention step of level j, the algorithm tries to finish. Let g0 > 0 be a rational number
chosen from the base data (definition 5.15) and let κ2 , κ3 be as in (5).
Definition 5.11 (Closure test). At level j:
 (1) For every coordinate i let Qi be the intersection, over the bags β ∋ i, of the smallest closed
     interval containing the ith projections of the cells of Wj′ (β), and let Q = i Qi .
                                                                                 Q

 (2) Record x̄i equal to the point of Qi for every i ∈ IZ with Qi a single point.

 (3) Let c be the midpoint of Q and r the largest half-width of the intervals Qi . For i ∈ IC , record
     x̄i = ℓi if ∂i Fγ (c) − κ2 r > 0, and x̄i = ui if ∂i Fγ (c) + κ2 r < 0.

 (4) If some integer coordinate is unrecorded, the test fails. Otherwise record x̄i equal to the point
     of Qi for every unrecorded i ∈ IC with Qi a single point. Let S be the recorded set and
     S̄ = [n] \ S. If S̄ = ∅, return the point x̄.

 (5) Otherwise let P =
                            Q                          P       and largest half-width rP . If the rational
                              i∈S̄ Qi , with midpoint c
     symmetric matrix
                                        ∇2S̄ S̄ Fγ (x̄S , cP ) − (κ3 rP + g0 )I                       (15)
      is positive definite, return the patch output (S, x̄S , P, g0 ); otherwise the test fails.
    Positive definiteness of a rational symmetric matrix is decided exactly by Gaussian elimination:
all pivots must be positive. All quantities in the test have encoding length polynomial in I, j and
log M .

                                                      53
Proposition 5.12 (Soundness of closure). On every draw, every equality recorded by the closure
test holds at every global minimizer of Fγ on X. If the test returns a point x̄, then x̄ is the unique
global minimizer. If it returns Π = (S, x̄S , P, g0 ), then (7) holds on P , the function Fγ (x̄S , ·) has a
unique minimizer ŷ on P , and (x̄S , ŷ) is the unique global minimizer of Fγ on X.

Proof. By proposition 5.7(a), every minimizer x∗ has x∗β in a retained cell of every bag, so x∗ ∈ Q.
A singleton Qi therefore fixes x∗i ; for i ∈ IZ its point is an endpoint of an integer cell, hence an
integer.
    For the gradient test, let x ∈ Q ⊆ X.    b The mean value theorem along the segment from c to x,
which lies in Q, gives |∂i Fγ (x) − ∂i Fγ (c)| ≤ k supXb |∂ik F0 | |xk − ck | ≤ κ2 r; the linear noise does
                                                    P

not change second derivatives. If ∂i Fγ (c) − κ2 r > 0, then ∂i Fγ (x∗ ) > 0. Were x∗i > ℓi , the point
x∗ − θei would be feasible for small θ > 0, because i is continuous, and would have a smaller value.
Hence x∗i = ℓi . The upper bound is symmetric.
    All recorded values lie in Xi , and P ⊆ i∈S̄ [ℓi , ui ] with S̄ ⊆ IC , so T = {x̄S } × P ⊆ X, and
                                                   Q

every minimizer lies in T . If S̄ = ∅, a minimizer exists by compactness and equals x̄. Otherwise,
for y ∈ P every entry of ∇2S̄ S̄ Fγ (x̄S , y) − ∇2S̄ S̄ Fγ (x̄S , cP ) in row k is at most rP m supXb |∂klm F0 |
                                                                                             P

in absolute value, by the mean value theorem; the spectral norm of a symmetric matrix is at most
its largest absolute row sum, so the difference has norm at most κ3 rP . Positive definiteness of
(15) gives (7). Then Fγ (x̄S , ·) is strictly convex on P and has a unique minimizer ŷ. Since T ⊆ X
contains every global minimizer and at least one, minT Fγ = f ∗ and the global minimizers are
exactly the minimizers on T , that is, (x̄S , ŷ).

    The test fixes coordinates by two different arguments. The gradient rule of step (3) fixes a
continuous coordinate only at an original bound, where first-order optimality in the original box
gives the sign condition; an endpoint of a hull interval that is not an original bound is never used
by this rule. The singleton rule of steps (2) and (4) fixes a coordinate, integer or continuous, at the
value that every global minimizer shares because its hull interval is a single point; this is justified
by containment (proposition 5.7(a)), not by a derivative. The gradient rule is applied only to
continuous coordinates: an interior integer optimum can have a nonzero derivative. A positive
definite continuous block does not justify closure while integer choices remain, which is why step (4)
fails when an integer coordinate is unrecorded.
    The test succeeds early when the sampled instance is well conditioned. The conditions in the
next proposition are not tested by the algorithm; they are used only to bound the stopping depth.

Proposition 5.13 (When closure succeeds). Suppose g∗ (γ) ≥ g0 > 0, let a be the unique minimizer,
and suppose that |∂i Fγ (a)| > τ for every i ∈ IC with ai ∈ {ℓi , ui }. Let A = 2 + nL/g0 . If
                                                 n 1         τ    g0 o
                                      hj ≤ min         ,        ,      ,                                   (16)
                                                  4A       4κ2 A 4κ3 A
then the closure test of level j returns a point or a patch output.

Proof. Let C ∈ Wj′ (β), and let y be its witness from proposition 5.7(d). Growth gives g0 ∥y − a∥2 ≤
                                                    h p
Fγ (y) − f ∗ ≤ 2Ej = nLh2j /4, so ∥y − a∥2 ≤ 2j nL/g0 . Every point of Ci is within hj of yi , so
within hj (1 + 12 nL/g0 ) ≤ Ahj of ai . Since ai ∈ Qi , every Qi lies in [ai − Ahj , ai + Ahj ], and
                 p

r ≤ Ahj and ∥c − a∥∞ ≤ r.
    By (16), hj ≤ 1/(4A) < 1, so integer cells are singletons. For i ∈ IZ , the integer cell of C is {yi }
and |yi − ai | ≤ Ahj ≤ 1/4, so yi = ai . Every retained cell of every bag containing i therefore has
ith projection {ai }, Qi = {ai }, and step (2) records every integer coordinate.


                                                       54
    Let i ∈ IC with ai = ℓi ; then ∂i Fγ (a) > τ by hypothesis and first-order optimality. As in the
proof of proposition 5.12, ∂i Fγ (c) − κ2 r ≥ ∂i Fγ (a) − 2κ2 Ahj > τ − τ /2 > 0, so step (3) records
x̄i = ℓi ; the case ai = ui is symmetric. By soundness no coordinate with ℓi < ai < ui is recorded
at a bound. Hence S̄ consists of continuous coordinates that are interior at a. If S̄ = ∅ the test
returns a point. Otherwise, for every unit vector e supported on S̄, the points a ± θe are feasible
for small θ > 0, and adding the two growth inequalities and letting θ → 0 gives eT ∇2 Fγ (a)e ≥ 2g0 ;
thus ∇2S̄ S̄ Fγ (a) ⪰ 2g0 I. The point (x̄S , cP ) differs from a only in S̄, by at most rP ≤ Ahj , so the
matrix (15) is at least (2g0 − 2κ3 Ahj − g0 )I ⪰ g20 I.

   The two-sided Taylor argument uses only the coordinates that are interior at the optimizer. A
boundary optimizer whose full Hessian is indefinite is therefore covered: the active coordinates are
removed before the matrix test. No lower bound on the distance of the optimizer from inactive
bounds is needed.

5.6   One finite law and the same-draw fallback
Two events make the closure test late: small point growth, and an active continuous coordinate
with a small derivative. Both have small probability under the grid law.

Lemma 5.14 (Finite-law tails on mixed boxes). There is a base-computable integer Ctail ≥ 1 with
log Ctail ≤ polyd (I) such that, for every M and every ε > 0,

                                                    εW   2nCtail
                                     P{g∗ < ε} ≤       +         .                                   (17)
                                                     σ     M
Let D = max{1, d − 1} and K = max{1, nc 3nc NZ Dnc }. For every τ > 0, the probability that g∗ > 0
and the unique minimizer a has a coordinate i ∈ IC with ai ∈ {ℓi , ui } and |∂i Fγ (a)| ≤ τ is at most
K(τ /σ + 1/M ).

Proof. This is lemma 3.17(b) and (d) with m = 0, S = W and RZ = NZ ; the first part combines
theorem 3.16 for the continuous uniform law with the transfer of lemma 3.17(a). The mixed box is
described by 2nc linear inequalities and by disjunctions over the NZ integer labels, so the number s
of atoms satisfies log(s + 1) ≤ poly(I), and the constant Ctail of lemma 3.17 has logarithm polyd (I)
even though the number of labels can be exponential.

    The second bound counts nonsingular stationary points of faces and does not assume that
stationary sets are finite. It is an unconditional estimate for the intersection with {g∗ > 0}, not a
conditional probability given good growth.

Definition 5.15 (Base schedule). Before sampling, compute from the base instance:

 (1) κ2 , κ3 by (5), Ctail and K as in lemma 5.14, and the factor B ≥ 2 of theorem 3.20 for the
     format of the instance (the mixed box X, the degree d, and the monomial support of F0 with
     n added linear terms);

 (2) ρ = 1/(4B), g0 = ρσ/(2W ), τ = ρσ/(2K) and A = 2 + nL/g0 ;

 (3) the stopping depth J, the least j ≥ 0 satisfying (16);

 (4) the grid size M , the least power of two with M ≥ max{2, 2J , 4nCtail /ρ, 2K/ρ}.




                                                   55
     Every quantity in the schedule has encoding length polyd (I): the logarithms of B, Ctail , K, κ2 ,
κ3 , 1/ρ, 1/g0 , 1/τ and A are polynomial in I, so J ≤ polyd (I) and log2 M ≤ polyd (I). The order
matters. The factor B is fixed first; the thresholds and J depend on B but not on M ; and M is
chosen last. The sampled coefficients have bit length b = log2 M + O(size σ), which enters only the
polynomial factor of the fallback.

Lemma 5.16 (Rare late closure). Under the schedule of definition 5.15, the closure test succeeds at
some level j ≤ J except on an event of probability at most 1/(2B).

Proof. By lemma 5.14, P{g∗ < g0 } ≤ ρ/2 + 2nCtail /M ≤ ρ, and the probability that g∗ ≥ g0 while
an active continuous derivative has magnitude at most τ is at most Kτ /σ + K/M ≤ ρ/2 + ρ/2.
Outside these two events proposition 5.13 applies at level J. The union has probability at most
2ρ = 1/(2B).

Definition 5.17 (Sparse cell algorithm). Given a rational input with fixed-degree polynomial
factors on a mixed box and a tree decomposition:

 (0) Preprocess as in section 2.1. If no unfixed coordinate remains, draw γ once, return the unique
     feasible point, which is rational, and its sampled value, and stop. Otherwise the result is a
     sparse mixed polynomial instance with n ≥ 1.

 (1) Compute the schedule of definition 5.15, convert the tree decomposition to maximum degree
     three, assign factors and noise terms to bags, and draw γ once from the grid law with this M .

 (2) Set W0 (β) = {Xβ } for every bag. For j = 0, 1, . . . , J: form the row lists, run the dynamic
     program of lemma 5.5 for all min-marginals and a minimizer of Fγ on Aj , update the incumbent,
     apply the retention rule (10), and run the closure test of definition 5.11. If the test returns,
     stop with its output. Otherwise form Wj+1 by generating children and removing duplicates.

 (3) If no closure test has returned by level J, run the exact fallback of theorem 3.20 on Fγ over X
     and return its output.

In step (0) the law is Uσ,2 ; the draw only determines the reported value, which includes the sampled
constants of the fixed coordinates.

Proof of theorem 5.2. Exactness. A closure output is correct on every draw by proposition 5.12,
and the fallback is correct on every draw by theorem 3.20. The fallback runs only if no closure test
returns, which by lemma 5.16 has probability at most 1/(2B). No draw is discarded or repeated.
    Work of one level. Let j ≥ 1. Generating Wj (β) costs O(2|β| Wj−1 ′  (β) ) cell operations and
a sort, the row list has at most 2|β| |Wj (β)| entries, the dynamic program costs O( β |Rj (β)|)
                                                                                          P

operations by lemma 5.5, and the retention rule and the hulls cost O(2|β| |Wj (β)|) further operations;
level 0 has one cell per bag. Every grid coordinate at level j ≤ J has bit length O(I + j + log s),
every sampled coefficient O(I + log M ), every factor value and message polyd (I), and a sort or
dictionary operation on a list of R rows costs O(log R) comparisons with log R ≤ log |Gj | ≤ polyd (I).
The closure test costs polyd (I) plus the hull computation. Hence the work of level j is at most
8p β (1 + Wj−1
   P          ′  (β) ) polyd (I).
   Expectation. By proposition 5.9, with M ≥ 2J , E Wj−1       ′  (β) ≤ 2p Θβ for 1 ≤ j ≤ J, and
Θβ ≤ [4 + (1 + n/2)Lwmax /(2σ)]p . The work is a sum of nonnegative terms, each bounded by a
constant times a retained-list size, so its expectation is bounded by linearity; no product of random
table sizes occurs. There are J + 1 ≤ polyd (I) levels and polynomially many bags. The fallback

                                                  56
costs B polyd (I + b) and runs with probability at most 1/(2B), so it contributes polyd (I) to the
expectation (lemma 3.18). Computing the schedule and drawing n log2 M random bits cost polyd (I).
This proves (8) with C0 = 16. The global proof record consists of quantities computed during the
run, so its length is bounded by the work; it is verified by recomputing the dynamic programs of
the levels, which costs the same.
   Evaluation. Part (iii) is proposition 5.18 below.

5.7   Exact implicit output and arbitrary-precision evaluation
A patch output determines one point exactly, but the coordinates of that point are usually irrational
algebraic numbers whose minimal polynomials can have large degree. The output contract does not
include them. What it includes is evaluation to any requested precision in time polynomial in the
precision.

Proposition 5.18 (Evaluation). Let q ≥ 0.

 (a) Given a patch output Π = (S, x̄S , P, g0 ) of theorem 5.2 with optimizer x̂, a rational y ∈ X with
     yS = x̄S and a rational a with a ≤ f ∗ ≤ Fγ (y) ≤ a + 2−q and ∥y − x̂∥2 ≤ 2−q are computed in
     polyd (I + q) bit operations. If S = [n], then y = x̂ = x̄ and a = Fγ (x̄).

 (b) Given a fallback output, a rational y ∈ X with the same integer labels as the returned optimizer
     x◦ , and a rational a with a ≤ f ∗ ≤ Fγ (y) ≤ a + 2−q and ∥y − x◦ ∥2 ≤ 2−q , are computed in
     B polyd (I + q) bit operations.

In particular the expected cost of a q-bit evaluation is polyd (I + q).

     The proof is in appendix C.2. A point output is rational and is evaluated exactly. For a positive-
dimensional patch, part (a) applies weak convex optimization in the bit model [23, Definition 2.1.10
and Corollary 4.2.7] to the capped epigraph of Fγ (x̄S , ·) over P , with an explicit repair of the
approximately feasible output; the modulus g0 converts value accuracy into distance, including at
boundary minimizers, and enters the cost only through its encoding length. The cost does not
depend on the numerical ratio L/g0 , as a grid-based evaluation would. Evaluation does not decide
exact comparisons of f ∗ with a rational threshold, and it does not identify the active set of ŷ.
Without a verified modulus, value accuracy alone would not localize the optimizer: a function that
is flat along a direction has points of equal value far from each other.

5.8   Quadratic and mixed-integer quadratic objectives
For d = 2 the patch and the fallback can both be solved in rational arithmetic, so the output is
rational on every draw.

Corollary 5.19 (Sparse mixed-integer quadratic programs). Let F0 (x) = 12 xT Hx + bT x + c0 be
rational on a mixed box X as in definition 5.1, with a tree decomposition of bag size p containing
{i, k} whenever Hik =
                    ̸ 0, and let L > 0 be rational with L ≥ maxi Hii (verified). There is a power
of two M , computed from the base instance with log2 M ≤ poly(I), such that for the ambient
perturbation model with marginal law Uσ,M :

  (i) on every draw the algorithm returns a rational global minimizer of Fγ on X and the optimal
      value, of bit length poly(I);

 (ii) the expected bit work is at most C0p [4 + (1 + n/2)Lwmax /(2σ)]p poly(I).


                                                  57
No growth, uniqueness, convexity, bound on negative inertia, bound on variable occurrences, or
bound on the integer dimension is used.

    The algorithm is that of definition 5.17 with two changes. After a patch output, the strongly
convex quadratic program min{Fγ (x̄S , y) : y ∈ P } is solved exactly; its unique solution is rational
of polynomial bit length and is computed in polynomial time by the ellipsoid method for convex
quadratic programming [30]. The fallback is the elementary enumeration of integer assignments and
continuous faces of lemma C.3, with B = 3nc NZ ; it needs no real algebraic geometry. The proof
is in appendix C.3. For a quadratic κ3 may be replaced by zero in (15), because the Hessian is
constant.
    The bound is fixed-width polynomial, as before. It does not contradict the strong NP-hardness
of box-constrained quadratic programming at treewidth two [17]. That result concerns the threshold
question for the unperturbed objective, whereas the corollary solves the sampled instance, with
a bound that is polynomial only when Lwmax /σ is polynomially bounded. For the treewidth-two
reduction itself, remark 9.8 exhibits no-instances, with r a size parameter, that have a point of value
2−2r−4 above the threshold with a coordinate equal to one. By proposition 9.7, the sampled minimum
of such an instance falls below the threshold with probability at least 12 − 2−2r−5 /σ − 1/(2M ), so
on these instances the exact sampled optimum does not answer the original threshold question. We
make no claim about other hardness reductions.

5.9   Relation to earlier work
The ingredients are classical. Dynamic programming over tree decompositions is nonserial dynamic
programming [11]. The allowance Lw2 /8 per coordinate is the maximal separation of the αBB
underestimator [1], used here with the opposite sign as a lower-bound correction. For linear tilts of
a polynomial objective on a regular compact semialgebraic set, uniqueness of the minimizer and a
growth inequality hold for generic tilts [31]; these statements are qualitative and give no probability
bound for a finite law. Smoothed analyses of discrete optimization use isolation of winners [8, 38];
a continuous box has no positive gap between distinct feasible values, which is why the count
above uses near-optimal grid nodes instead. Treewidth-based linear programming approximations
of sparse polynomial optimization [12] give accuracy-dependent formulations rather than exact
optimizers. Exact quadratic optimization on a box is polynomial on forests and strongly NP-hard at
treewidth two [17]. An unpublished companion manuscript [14] develops a deterministic counterpart
for factored objectives on mixed boxes: corrected coordinate grids, dynamic programming of min-
marginals on a tree decomposition, filtering with certificates whose validity does not use growth, the
conditional-recourse filter of proposition 5.10(a),(b), and running-time bounds fixed-parameter in the
width under a quadratic growth condition of the instance; the end of section 5.4 explains why those
bounds do not average to an expected bound under noise. This section re-proves the parts of that
pipeline it needs. The new element is the composition under noise: an expected count of retained
bag cells, for every base instance and without a growth premise, under one finite independent-noise
law that does not grow with the mesh, combined with sound closure on a certified strongly convex
patch, an exact same-draw fallback, and an output whose evaluation costs polynomial time in the
requested precision.

The proof of theorem 5.2 used four properties of a mixed box.

(P1) For every bag, the set of feasible values of the coordinates outside the bag does not depend
     on the bag’s values. The conditional value Vβ of (12) is then a minimum over a fixed set,
     independent of the bag’s own noise, with the curvature of the objective.

(P2) Every feasible point of a candidate cell can be rounded, with its mean preserved and a
     second-order error, to feasible corners that lie in the same candidate cells of every bag.

(P3) Sound first-order tests identify the active constraints near an optimizer, after which a certified
     strongly convex patch contains every optimizer.

(P4) The point-growth and margin events have finite-law tails, and an exact fallback exists on the
     same domain.

Arbitrary sparse affine constraints do not have these properties, and the method cannot be extended
to them without further structure: theorem 9.5 shows that bounded-width affine equalities with
objective noise can encode Subset Sum, and example 9.6 shows that even a single totally unimodular
inequality destroys the coordinatewise count of lemma 5.8. This section proves the method for
four structured classes. Each supplies these four proof steps or replaces one of them by a proved
substitute. Graph constraints and affine-state recurrences satisfy (P1) after a reduction to a box of
retained or free coordinates, and products of simplices satisfy it because every bag contains whole
blocks. Order constraints do not satisfy (P1): the feasible values outside a bag depend on the
bag’s values. There, a transport of feasible points supplies the semiconcave upper bound on the
conditional value that (P1) gives for boxes (lemma D.8). Table 3 lists the classes.
    Two reductions below are conditional applications of theorem 5.2 to a family of objectives
indexed by part of the noise. The constants of its schedule must then be chosen uniformly over the
family, before any noise is drawn; choosing them after observing part of the noise would make the
law depend on the draw. The following lemma records what this requires.

Lemma 6.1 (Uniform schedules). Let X be a mixed box with n ≥ 1 coordinates and a tree
decomposition of bag size p, and let H ⊆ Rm be a box. For every rational η ∈ H let Gη be an explicit
rational polynomial of degree at most d′ on X whose monomial support lies in a fixed set computed


                                                        59
from the base instance and whose factor scopes lie in the bags. Suppose that there are polynomials
P1 , P2 , whose degrees depend only on fixed format constants such as degrees and depths, such that
for every b ≥ 1 and every rational η ∈ H with entries of bit length at most b, the coefficients of
Gη have bit length at most P1 (I + b) and Gη is computed from the base instance and η in at most
P2 (I + b) bit operations. Suppose also that

 (a) rationals L > 0, κ2 and κ3 , computed from the base instance, satisfy (3) and (5) for every Gη ,
     η ∈ H, and

  (b) a deterministic algorithm solves every problem minX (Gη + γ T x) with rational η ∈ H and
      γ ∈ Qn exactly, and refines its output to precision 2−q , within B poly(I + b + q) bit operations
      when the entries of η and γ have bit length at most b; here B ≥ 2 depends only on the base
      instance and log B ≤ poly(I).

Compute the schedule of definition 5.15 from L, κ2 , κ3 , W , this B, and the constants Ctail and
K = max{1, nc 3nc NZ max{1, d′ − 1}nc } of lemma 5.14 for degree d′ , and let b be the largest bit
length of an atom of the resulting law Uσ,M . Let η be a random rational point of H whose entries
                                                                                                 ⊗n
have bit length at most b, with an arbitrary law, and let γ be independent of η with law Uσ,M        .
                                                                                    T
Then the algorithm of definition 5.17, with the fallback (b), applied to Gη + γ x is correct on
every draw, runs its fallback with probability at most 1/(2B), and has expected work at most
C0p [4 + (1 + n/2)Lwmax /(2σ)]p poly(I + b), where the degree of the polynomial factor depends on d′ ,
P1 and P2 . Since b ≤ poly(I), this is C0p [4 + (1 + n/2)Lwmax /(2σ)]p poly(I).

Proof. Every statement of section 5 about one objective uses only the validity of L, κ2 and κ3 for
that objective, the tail constants, the fallback, and the bit length of the objective’s coefficients, which
enters the cost of each arithmetic operation polynomially. The constant Ctail of lemma 3.17 depends
on the number of variables, the degree and the number of atoms of the domain formula, and K on
nc , NZ and d′ ; neither depends on the coefficients of Gη . All schedule quantities are therefore fixed
before η and γ are drawn, and b = O(log2 M + size σ) is polynomial in I. Conditionally on η, the
coefficients γ are independent with law Uσ,M , so lemma 5.14, proposition 5.9, lemma 5.16 and the
proof of theorem 5.2 apply to Gη with the same schedule, after the cost P2 (I + b) of computing Gη
and with factor values of bit length polynomial in P1 (I + b). Averaging over η gives the claim.

     In the applications below, the fallback (b) is theorem 3.20 applied to the original constrained
problem: an explicit polynomial on a compact semialgebraic domain (definition 3.19) tilted by the
ambient noise on all coordinates. Its minimizers correspond to those of the reduced problem, and
its factor B is fixed by the base instance, while the sampled coefficients enter only through b.

6.1    Global graph parameterizations
Some coupled equality constraints can be eliminated by a global chart that keeps a set of retained
coordinates ranging over a mixed box. The retained coordinates remain ambient coordinates with
their own independent noise. Conditioning on the noise of the eliminated coordinates then leaves
exactly the setting of section 5.

Definition 6.2 (Graph instance). A graph instance consists of rational data as follows.

  (i) Retained coordinates x in a mixed box X ⊆ Rn as in definition 5.1 (i), with n ≥ 1, and
      dependent coordinates y ∈ Rm given by a chart y = ψ(x) of one of two kinds.

          of dependencies has length at most Dg ; e and Dg are fixed.
       (I) Implicit: qj (xSj , yj ) = 0 and yj ∈ Vj = [ℓ′j , u′j ], j = 1, . . . , m, where qj is an explicit

the derivative floor must hold on the whole real hull, including between integer labels, because
the algorithm evaluates the chart at such points. Brackets at individual query points would not
establish uniqueness, smoothness, or the validity of rounding between grid points. A dependent
coordinate whose chart has no retained argument is a constant. It is substituted only when it is
rational, that is, given by a constant polynomial in case (E) or by a one-point interval Vj in case
(I). Other constant roots, such as the root of y 2 = 2 in [1, 2], stay in the chart and in the original
constrained problem used by the fallback, so that no algebraic constant enters the rational data. If
preprocessing fixes every retained coordinate, the feasible set is one point; the algorithm returns
it directly, in case (I) as the fixed retained values together with the chart, which determines the
dependent roots.

Status of the premises. In case (E), ψ is an explicit polynomial map obtained by substitution,
and (19) is verified by monomial bounds that hold uniformly for |ηj | ≤ σ, or promised if sharper.
In case (I), (18) and (19) are promised, or certified with their verification cost added to the work;
arbitrary polynomial positivity is not tested. Curvature is measured after substitution. An ambient
diagonal bound does not suffice (example 6.8), and the dependent noise η can contribute to it.
Lemma 6.3 (Expanded decomposition). For a dependent coordinate yj let Λ(yj ) be the set of
retained coordinates on which it depends: its retained ancestors in case (E) and Sj in case (I); let
Λ(xi ) = {xi }. Replacing every bag β by β ′ = v∈β Λ(v) gives a tree decomposition, on the same tree,

every term ηj ψj (x), and its bag size is p′ ≤ p max{1, k Dg } in case (E) and p′ ≤ p max{1, k} in case

    The proof is in appendix D.1. The equality scopes must lie in the supplied bags: a decomposition
of the objective alone does not give running intersection after substitution.

Theorem 6.4 (Sparse optimization over global graphs). Fix d, e and, in case (E), Dg . For
graph instances there is an algorithm and a power of two M , computed from the base instance with

following hold.

  (i) On every draw the algorithm returns an exact global optimizer of Fγ,η on X. On ordinary
      draws the output is a charted patch output (definition 6.5) of length poly(I) with its global
      proof record. On the remaining draws, of probability at most 1/(2B), it is the exact output of
      theorem 3.20 for Fγ,η on the compact semialgebraic set X, computed on the same draw.

 (ii) The expected bit work is at most

      with p′ from lemma 6.3; the global proof record obeys the same bounds in expectation.

(iii) A q-bit evaluation returns a rational retained point x̃ ∈ X within distance 2−q of the retained
      part x̂ of the optimizer and a rational interval [aq , uq ] of width at most 2−q with aq ≤ f ∗ ≤
      Fγ,η (x̃, ψ(x̃)) ≤ uq , where f ∗ = minX Fγ,η . In case (E) the lift (x̃, ψ(x̃)) is rational, satisfies
      every graph equation, and lies within distance 2−q of the optimizer. In case (I) the lift (x̃, ψ(x̃))
      is an exactly feasible point given by rational retained coordinates and isolating brackets of its

      this rational point need not satisfy the graph equations. The cost is poly(I + q) on ordinary
      draws and B poly(I + q) on fallback draws, and its expectation is poly(I + q).

No growth, uniqueness or strict-complementarity premise is used.

    The exponents of the polynomials depend on the fixed degrees. The bound is fixed-width
polynomial in the expanded bag size p′ ; it is not a fixed-parameter bound. Case (I) loses the factor
1 + n/2 to 1 + n because its costs are algebraic and are computed with certified error (lemma 6.7).
The two cases are not comparable: (E) allows chains of dependent coordinates of bounded depth
and gives rational feasible points, whereas (I) allows charts that are not polynomial but only one
layer of dependent coordinates. Even one cubic equation can force irrational dependent coordinates
at rational retained points, so no rational feasible point need exist in case (I).

Definition 6.5 (Charted patch output). For a graph instance and a draw (γ, η), a charted patch
output (definition 2.5(c)) is a patch output (S, x̄S , P, g0 ) in the sense of section 5.1 for the reduced
objective Gη + γ T x on X, verified with (7), together with the chart: the polynomials Pj in case (E);
the equations qj , the intervals Vj and the bounds µj in case (I). It denotes the point (x̂, ψ(x̂)), where
x̂ = (x̄S , ŷ) and ŷ is the unique minimizer of Gη (x̄S , ·) + γ T (x̄S , ·) on P . Equivalently, x̂ is the unique
solution of the box KKT system of this reduced problem, and in case (I) the dependent coordinates
are the unique roots of qj (x̂Sj , yj ) = 0 in Vj , with graph multipliers λj = −(∂yj F0 + ηj )/∂y qj at that
point.

    The descriptor determines one point because the reduced objective is strongly convex on P , and
the global proof record shows that this point is the global optimizer. No positive definiteness of the
ambient Hessian is claimed or needed.


                                                        62
The explicit case. By lemma 6.3, the reduced objective Gη + γ T x is an explicit polynomial of
degree at most deDg on X whose factor scopes lie in the bags β ′ . Substitution of a dependent-noise
vector η with entries of bit length b produces coefficients of bit length polynomial in I + b, in
time polynomial in I + b, with exponents that depend only on d, e and Dg ; repeated products of
supplied coefficients can make these lengths exceed I + b, but only by a fixed polynomial. The
pair (x, ψ(x)) 7→ x is a bijection from X onto X that preserves objective values, so the sampled
problem is equivalent to minimizing Gη + γ T x on X. Conditionally on η, the coefficients γ are
independent with law Uσ,M . Lemma 6.1 therefore applies, with L from (19), κ2 , κ3 from monomial
bounds uniform in |ηj | ≤ σ, degree d′ = deDg , and the fallback of theorem 3.20 on X, whose domain
formula consists of the mixed box and the polynomial equations yj = Pj . The rest of the proof is in
appendix D.1.

The implicit case. Here the reduced objective is algebraic but not polynomial, and three
ingredients replace exact rational arithmetic: a chart oracle (lemma 6.6), a dynamic program on
certified lower costs (lemma 6.7), and closure tests with certified derivative errors. The tails use the
original polynomial graph, and so does the fallback.

Lemma 6.7 (Certified lower costs). In case (I), at level j compute for every row of every bag a
rational lower cost ϕβ with ϕβ ≤ ϕβ ≤ ϕβ + δj , where ϕβ is the sum of the reduced terms assigned
to β and δj = Ej /NB with NB the number of bags. Run the dynamic program on the lower costs,
update the incumbent with Uj = min{Uj−1 , mj + Ej }, where mj is the lower-cost minimum, initialize
U−1 = +∞, and store the feasible implicit lift associated with the chosen upper bound. Update this
bound and point before pruning. Retain a cell when q C − Ej ≤ Uj , with q C the least lower-cost
min-marginal over its corners. Then on every draw all global optimizers are retained, every point
removed at level j has value above Uj ≥ f ∗ , and every retained cell has a corner v = yβ of an
allowed grid point y with Gη (y) + γ T y ≤ f ∗ + 4Ej .

   The proof (appendix D.1) is that of proposition 5.7 with the error Dj = NB δj ≤ Ej of the
summed lower costs. The witness tolerance 4Ej replaces 2Ej ; lemma 5.8 then gives intervals of


                                                       63
length Lai + 8Ej /ai ≤ (1 + n)Lai , which explains the factor in case (I). Exact comparisons of
algebraic values are never made: the dynamic program is exact rational arithmetic on certified
rational costs, and different bags may use different brackets of the same root without affecting global
consistency.
     The closure test of definition 5.11 is used with certified derivatives. In step (3), a rational ĝ with
  ĝ − ∇(Gη + γ T x)(c)    ≤ ϵg replaces the exact gradient, and a continuous coordinate is recorded
                         ∞
at its lower bound when ĝi − κ2 r − ϵg > 0 and at its upper bound when ĝi + κ2 r + ϵg < 0. In step (5),
a rational symmetric Ĥ with Ĥ − ∇2S̄ S̄ Gη (x̄S , cP ) ≤ ϵH replaces the exact Hessian; it is obtained
                                                        2
from enclosures of width at most ϵH /n of the entries by taking midpoints and symmetrizing, since a
symmetric error matrix with entries at most ϵH /n in absolute value has spectral norm at most ϵH .
The test is Ĥ − (κ3 rP + ϵH + g0 )I ≻ 0. With ϵg = τ /8 and ϵH = g0 /8, these tests are sound on
every draw and succeed under the hypotheses of proposition 5.13 (appendix D.1).
Example 6.8 (Two necessary premises). Curvature after substitution. Under the equality y = x,
the ambient polynomial F0 (x, y) = Hxy + ε(x2 + y 2 ) has both ambient diagonal second derivatives
equal to 2ε, while its pullback (H + 2ε)x2 has second derivative 2H + 4ε. The chart is linear
and well conditioned, yet the ambient cross derivative becomes retained curvature. Retained
ambient coordinates. Under the parameterization (x1 , x2 ) = (t1 + t2 , t1 + 2t2 ), t ∈ [0, 1]2 , which has
determinant one and a bounded inverse, independent ambient coefficients pull back to c1 = γ1 + γ2
and c2 = γ1 + 2γ2 . Under the grid law, the event c2 = 3σ forces γ1 = γ2 = σ, so c1 = 2σ is
conditionally deterministic: a singleton receives conditional probability one instead of at most 1/M ,
and the conditional count of lemma 5.8 fails. This does not show that the parallelogram is hard; it
shows that a bounded inverse alone does not preserve the noise model.
Example 6.9 (A connected chain of products). Let x ∈ [0, 1]n , where some coordinates may be
binary, and impose yi = xi xi+1 for i < n, with F0 = i<n (yi −ai )2 +λ i x2i , λ ≥ 0. The constraints
                                                       P                 P

form a connected chain of triangles {xi , xi+1 , yi }, not a product of separate blocks. Substitution
gives a quartic with path bags {xi , xi+1 }, and ∂ii Gη = 2x2i−1 + 2x2i+1 + 2λ ≤ 4 + 2λ for all targets
ai and all η, since the dependent noise terms ηi xi xi+1 have zero pure second derivatives. Thus
L = 4 + 2λ, independently of n, and case (E) applies with p′ = 2.

6.2    Affine-state recurrences with polynomial actuators
Bounded-depth substitution does not cover dynamics over a long horizon, because the states depend
on all earlier controls. For affine state dependence and affine state costs, an adjoint recursion
eliminates the states while adding only unary terms.
Definition 6.10 (Actuator instance). For a horizon N ≥ 1 and k = 0, . . . , N − 1, the states and
controls satisfy
                           sk+1 = ak sk + χk (uk ), |ak | ≤ a < 1,                            (21)
with rational ak and a, explicit rational univariate polynomials χk of degree at most d, nonempty
closed rational state intervals Ik , k = 0, . . . , N , and control domains Uk that are closed rational
intervals or native integer intervals. Box invariance holds:
                                 ak Ik + χk (hull Uk ) ⊆ Ik+1      (k < N ).                              (22)
There are no other constraints. The free variables are z = (s0 , u0 , . . . , uN −1 ); a fixed initial state is
allowed. The objective is F0 = q(z) + N
                                       P
                                         k=1 ck sk , where q is a sum of explicit rational polynomial
factors of degree at most d whose scopes lie in the bags of a supplied tree decomposition of the free
variables with bag size p. The noise is ambient on all original coordinates, states and controls.

                                                      64
    Condition (22) is verified in polynomial time at fixed degree, by comparing the rational interval
endpoints with the extreme values of χk at the endpoints of hull Uk and at the real roots of χ′k ,
using univariate root isolation. It makes the state intervals redundant: every free point has exactly
one feasible trajectory. If preprocessing fixes every free variable, that is, the initial state is fixed and
every control domain is a single point, the unique feasible trajectory is rational and the algorithm
returns it with its sampled value directly.
    Condition on the state coefficients η = (γs1 , . . . , γsN ) and define the adjoints λN = cN + ηN
and λk = ck + ηk + ak λk+1 for k = N − 1, . . . , 1. Substituting (21) backward from k = N gives the
identity

                                                                         N

                     Qη (z) + ξ T z,    Qη (z) = q(z) + a0 λ1 s0 +              λk+1 χk (uk ),

for every horizon. With Cs ≥ maxk |ck |, induction gives |λk | ≤ Λ := (Cs + σ)/(1 − a) for every
conditioned draw. Let Lq be a rational upper bound on the pure second derivatives ∂ii q on the free

most |λk+1 | |χ′′k | ≤ ΛG2 to ∂uk uk Qη and nothing to ∂s0 s0 Qη . Analogous bounds with ΛG2 and ΛG3
give uniform κ2 and κ3 . The adjoints are computed by N affine steps, so their denominators divide
products of the denominators of the ak , ck and ηk , and their bit lengths are bounded by sums of
the lengths of these numbers plus the length of Λ; the coefficients of Qη therefore have bit length
polynomial in I + b, as lemma 6.1 requires.

Theorem 6.11 (Affine-state recurrences with polynomial actuators). Fix d. For actuator instances,
with L as in (24) or any uniform valid bound, there is an algorithm and a power of two M , computed
from the base instance with log2 M ≤ polyd (I), such that for the ambient model with law Uσ,M the
following hold, where n is the number of free variables after substituting fixed ones. The all-fixed
branch returns its rational trajectory directly; the displayed width bound concerns the remaining
nonempty free box.

  (i) On every draw the algorithm returns an exact global optimizer, as a patch output in the free
      variables together with the recurrence (21), or, with probability at most 1/(2B), as the exact
      output of theorem 3.20 for the sampled objective on the set of feasible trajectories, described by
      (21) and the variable bounds.

 (ii) The expected bit work is at most C0p [4 + (1 + n/2)Lwmax /(2σ)]p polyd (I).

(iii) A q-bit evaluation returns a rational free point and its exactly feasible rational trajectory,
      within distance 2−q of the optimal trajectory, and a rational interval of width 2−q containing
      the optimal value, in expected bit work polyd (I + q).


                                                    65
    The proof is in appendix D.2. The bound holds for every horizon, because the reduced
decomposition is the free-variable decomposition. The restrictions are substantive. Nonlinear state
dependence or nonlinear state costs raise the degree or couple many controls under elimination;
for instance sk+1 = sk /2 + u2k /4 with terminal cost s2N produces the monomial u2i u2l for every pair
of controls. Additional path or terminal constraints destroy the free product domain. The next
example shows why the equalities cannot simply be kept as constraints in the sparse algorithm.
Example 6.12 (Predecessor fibers). Let s1 = u20 and s2 = u21 with s0 , s1 , s2 ∈ [0, 1] and
u0 , u1 ∈ [−1, 1], linear objective γ T (s, u), and bags {s0 , u0 , s1 } and {s1 , u1 , s2 }. Conditioning on the
coefficients √outside the second√bag, its conditional value at (s1 , u1 , s2 ) = (t, 0,      √0)√is min{0, γs0 } +
γs1 t − |γu0 | t, because u0 = ± t. Its second difference at 0, h, 2h is |γu0 | (2 − 2) h, which is not
O(h2 ). Under the grid law γu0 ̸= 0 on every draw, since M is even. No finite coordinate curvature
bound holds for this conditional value, so the count of lemma 5.8 does not apply, although the
dynamics are box invariant and smooth. The elimination of (23) avoids predecessor fibers altogether.
Example 6.13 (A stateful nonlinear actuator). On [−1, 1], the transition sk+1 = 14 sk + 14 uk + 18 u2k
maps [−1, 1] × [−1, 1] into [− 83 , 58 ], so (22) holds. Continuous or integer controls in [−1, 1], affine
                                                          P
state costs, and any coupled nonconvex control cost β qβ (s0 , uβ ) with the supplied decomposition
satisfy definition 6.10.

6.3    Products of simplices
Resource constraints inside disjoint blocks are not eliminated by a chart: a stick-breaking map from
a box onto a simplex makes the ambient linear noise nonlinear. Instead, feasible rounding and the
count are adapted to the faces of the simplices.
Definition 6.14 (Simplex instance). The continuous coordinates are partitioned into blocks b; each
block is an inequality block with feasible set ∆b = {xb ≥ 0, i∈b xi ≤ 1}, or an equality block with
                                                               P

∆=b = {xb ≥ 0,      i∈b xi = 1} and |b| ≥ 2. The remaining coordinates are native integer intervals as
                 P

in definition 5.1, and there is at least one coordinate. The objective F0 is a sum of explicit factors
of degree at most d, and a supplied tree decomposition contains every factor scope and whole blocks:
a bag containing a coordinate of b contains all of b. Its largest number of scalar coordinates is p. A
rational L > 0 satisfies

             ∇2bb F0 ⪯ LI for every block b,         ∂ii F0 ≤ L for every integer coordinate i,              (25)

on the continuous hull of the domain. The noise is ambient on all coordinates. Let wmax be the
                                                          P
largest integer width, or 1 if there is none, and W = nc + i∈IZ wi .
    The bound (25) on the block Hessian is stronger than a bound on its diagonal entries, and it is
needed: rounding within a block is correlated. It is verified, for example, by the row-sum bound
maxi∈b k∈b sup |∂ik F0 |, or promised. The derivative bounds κ2 , κ3 of (5) are taken on the enclosing
        P

coordinate box [0, 1]nc × Xb Z , not only on the product of simplices, because the closure tests evaluate
derivatives at midpoints of coordinate hulls, which can lie outside a simplex. Whole-block bags
are essential: they make the domain outside a bag independent of the bag’s values. A model with
simplex constraints in the scalar primal graph satisfies this after enlarging bags by at most the
largest block size; a single resource constraint over all variables gives no width advantage.
Theorem 6.15 (Products of simplices). Fix d. For simplex instances there is an algorithm and a
power of two M , computed from the base instance with log2 M ≤ polyd (I), such that for the ambient
model with law Uσ,M :

                                                       66
  (i) on every draw the algorithm returns an exact global optimizer: on ordinary draws a relative
      patch output of length polyd (I) (fixed integer labels, fixed coordinates, recorded tight budgets, a
      rational relative polytope P , and a verified modulus g0 with v T ∇2 Fγ v ≥ g0 ∥v∥22 on P for all
      directions v of its affine hull), or a rational point, with its global proof record; on the remaining
      draws, of probability at most 1/(2B), the exact output of theorem 3.20 on the same domain;

 (ii) the expected bit work is at most C0p [4 + (2 + n/2)L max{1, wmax }/(2σ)]p polyd (I);

(iii) a q-bit evaluation, with an exactly feasible rational point, costs polyd (I + q) on ordinary draws
      and has expectation polyd (I + q).

    The proof is in appendix D.3. We describe its four ingredients.
    Cells and rounding. The mesh hj = s2−j of section 5.2 is shared with the integer coordinates,
so it can exceed one. While hj ≥ 1, each block has one cell, its simplex, whose corners are
the vertices of the simplex. For h = hj = 1/m < 1 the cells of a block are the nonempty sets
C = ∆b ∩ i∈b [hki , h(ki + 1)] with ki ∈ {0, . . . , m − 1}, or the same with ∆=
             Q
                                                                                 b , and the corners of
C are its points in (hZ)b . After scaling, C is the unit cube intersected with i zi ≤ m − i ki (or
                                                                                 P            P

equality), whose right-hand side is an integer, so every vertex of C is a cube corner (lemma D.3).
Every point of a cell is therefore a convex combination of its corners, which gives mean-preserving
feasible rounding with E ∥Yb − xb ∥2 ≤ |b| h2j /4; coordinates on grid hyperplanes stay fixed, so all
candidate lists are preserved. Rounding blocks independently and using (25) gives the allowance
Ej = nLh2j /8, and proposition 5.7 holds verbatim.
    Count by faces. Classify a block grid point vb by its smallest face of the simplex: its positive
support S and whether its budget is tight. On a slack face the directions ei , i ∈ S, are feasible in
both signs; on a tight face, with the least index ι ∈ S as anchor, the directions ei − eι , i ∈ S \ {ι},
are. Conditioning also on the anchor coefficients, each direction confines one remaining independent
coefficient to an interval of length at most Lh ∥d∥2 + 4Ej /h ≤ (2 + n/2)Lh. A face of dimension r
has at most h−r relative-interior grid points, which cancels the probability hr (lemma D.4). The
face classification, not a coordinate classification, is what makes the count independent of h.
    Closure on original faces. For an inequality block, a positive partial derivative throughout the
hull forces xi = 0; a negative one forces the budget to be tight; and a positive difference ∂i F − ∂l F
forces xi = 0. For an equality block only the last test is valid: decreasing one coordinate alone
is infeasible, and a strictly convex objective can have positive partial derivatives at an interior
minimizer of a probability simplex. The margins concern active inequalities only: zero coordinates,
and tight budgets of inequality blocks. The budget of an equality block is imposed structurally;
its multiplier is unrestricted in sign and no margin is required of it. For F0 = 0 on x1 + x2 = 1,
x ≥ 0, the multiplier of the budget in the sign convention of lemma D.5 is − min{γ1 , γ2 }, which is
negative whenever both coefficients are positive, although the point growth is positive whenever
γ1 ̸= γ2 ; a margin requirement on it would fail with probability close to 1/4. At an optimizer whose
active inequalities have multipliers larger than τ , the tests detect every active inequality without a
lower bound on positive coordinates: in an inequality block with a tight budget, the derivative of
a positive coordinate is minus the budget multiplier, and in every block with a tight or equality
budget, the multiplier of a zero coordinate is its derivative difference from a positive coordinate, in
which the budget multiplier cancels. The patch test is Z T ∇2 Fγ (cP )Z − (κ3 rP + g0 )Z T Z ≻ 0 for
a rational tangent basis Z of the patch, a rational point cP of it, and the largest hull width rP ;
subtracting a multiple of Z T Z rather than of I keeps the curvature in the metric of the original
coordinates (lemma D.5).
    Margins. The face stationarity equations depend only on tangent noise, the differences γi − γι ,
and the multiplier of each active inequality depends with slope ±1 on one further original coefficient.

                                                    67
The transformed coefficients are not independent under the grid law, and the proof does not treat
them as independent: it counts transformed tuples, with at most 2M − 1 values per difference
(lemma D.6).

6.4   Order constraints with continuous and binary coordinates
Order constraints xi ≤ xl may couple the whole instance; they are not confined to blocks, so (P1)
fails. Instead, the conditional value is controlled by transporting feasible points, and the active
constraints are identified by linear programming.

Definition 6.16 (Order instance). Let [n] = IC ∪ IZ , nc = |IC | ≥ 1, nz = |IZ |, and let E be a set
of m directed edges on [n]; cycles are allowed. The feasible set is

                  X = {x ∈ [0, 1]n : xi ≤ xl for (i, l) ∈ E, xi ∈ {0, 1} for i ∈ IZ }.

The objective F0 is a sum of explicit factors of degree at most d, and a supplied tree decomposition
contains every factor scope and every edge. Let p be its bag size and pc the largest number of
continuous coordinates in a bag. A rational L̄ ≥ 1 satisfies ∇2 F0 ⪯ L̄I on [0, 1]n . The noise is
ambient on all coordinates.

     The zero vector is feasible. If nc = 0, ordinary finite-state dynamic programming over binary
bag rows solves the instance exactly for every γ in 2O(p) polyd (I) bit work, and no noise is needed.
The full Hessian bound L̄ is needed because feasible rounding of ordered coordinates is correlated;
it is verified by a row-sum bound or promised.

Theorem 6.17 (Order constraints). Fix d. For order instances with nc ≥ 1 there is an algorithm
and a power of two M , computed from the base instance with log2 M ≤ polyd (I), such that for the
ambient model with law Uσ,M :

  (i) on every draw the algorithm returns an exact global optimizer: on ordinary draws a binary
      assignment together with a relative patch output on the continuous slice, consisting of fixed
      coordinates, a block structure xIC = Dy + x̄ with disjoint zero-one columns, a rational box and
      the remaining order inequalities in y, and a modulus g0 with verified ∇2y Fγ (Dy + x̄) ⪰ g0 DT D
      on the patch, or a rational point if no block remains; on the remaining draws, of probability at
      most 1/(2B), the algebraic fallback output;

 (ii) the expected bit work is at most
                                                  h        3nc L̄ ipc
                                   C0p pc ! (pc + 1) 2 +              polyd (I);                   (26)
                                                            4σ

(iii) a q-bit evaluation, with an exactly feasible rational point, costs polyd (I + q) on ordinary draws
      and has expectation polyd (I + q).

     The number of binary coordinates is unrestricted, and they do not enter the bracket. For nz = 0
the bound is C0p p! (p + 1)[2 + 3nL̄/(4σ)]p polyd (I). The factorial is a function of p alone; the bound
is fixed-width polynomial, not fixed-parameter, because the bracket contains nc . Only binary integer
coordinates are covered: transporting points with arbitrary integer levels would need fixed interior
knots and a different count. The proof is in appendix D.4; its ingredients are as follows.
     Rounding. Continuous coordinates use dyadic cells of width hj = 2−j , and binary coordinates
the cell {0, 1} at level 0 and singletons afterwards. Rounding all continuous coordinates by one

                                                   68
common threshold, Yi = h(⌊xi /h⌋ + 1{U < frac(xi /h)}) with U uniform on [0, 1], is a nondecreasing
map for each value of U . It therefore preserves every order constraint and every binary value, fixes
grid nodes, and stays in every candidate cell. It is mean preserving with E ∥Y − x∥2 ≤ nc h2 /4, and
the full Hessian bound gives the allowance Ej = nc L̄h2j /8. The threshold is a proof device; the
algorithm does not sample it. A similar mean-preserving coupling of ordered coordinates by one
quantile appears in isotonic optimization [5].
    Transport and count. Fix a bag, a binary bag row, and the noise outside the continuous bag
coordinates. For a feasible continuous bag tuple v in the relative interior of a face of a standard order
simplex {0 ≤ vπ(1) ≤ · · · ≤ vπ(c) ≤ 1}, take a conditional minimizer and apply to all its coordinates
the nondecreasing piecewise-affine map that moves the distinct interior values of v to those of a
target w in the same closed face while fixing 0 and 1. The result is feasible, keeps every binary value,
has bag part w, and is affine in w; along the edge directions of the face every coordinate moves
at rate in [0, 1], so the transported objective is an upper support for the conditional value with
directional curvature at most nc L̄ (lemma D.8). The transported fiber need not equal the true fiber,
and the projected mixed domain need not be convex; an upper support is what the comparison
needs. Counting relative-interior grid points of the faces of the pc ! order simplices, as in lemma 5.8,
gives the bracket of (26) (proposition D.9).
    Closure by linear programming. When every binary hull is a singleton, all optimizers lie in one
continuous slice, a polytope with zero-one vertices. With g the gradient at the hull midpoint and
δ = κ2 r a bound on its error, a proposed equality xi = xl is certified when the least value of g T x on
the slice face {xi = 0, xl = 1} exceeds the least value on the slice by more than nc δ; bound equalities
use the faces {xi = 1} and {xi = 0}. These are rational linear programs. At every optimizer the
true slice gradient exposes a face containing it, and if the equality failed some zero-one vertex of
that face would lie in the opposite face, which bounds the computed gap by nc δ (lemma D.10). The
certified equalities merge coordinates into blocks, and all other order inequalities are kept in the
patch, so no lower bound on inactive slacks is needed.

Example 6.18 (Binary labels must be fixed before exposure). On 0 ≤ x ≤ z ≤ 1 with z binary, the
objective (x − 12 )2 + 10z 2 − 11z has the unique mixed optimizer ( 12 , 1) with value −1 and point-growth
modulus one. Its full gradient there is (0, 9). Over the continuous relaxation, the least value of
9z is 0, while on the face {x = 0, z = 1} opposite to the equality x = z it is 9. The relaxed test
would certify x = z, which is false. After z = 1 is fixed, the slice gradient is zero and no equality is
certified. The algorithm therefore applies the linear-programming test only after all binary hulls are
singletons.

6.5    What is not covered
General totally unimodular constraints are not covered. For P = {x ∈ [0, 1]n : Ax ≤ b} with A
integral and totally unimodular and b integral, aligned feasible rounding holds: after scaling a cell
of width h = 2−j , the cell polytope {z ∈ [0, 1]n : Az ≤ b/h − Ak} has an integral right-hand side
and hence integral vertices [25], so every feasible cell point is a mean of feasible corners, and a full
Hessian bound L̄ gives the allowance nL̄h2 /8. With every constraint inside a bag, the pruning of
proposition 5.7 is then sound. What is missing is a count of retained cells whose constants are
controlled by the input: example 9.6 shows that coordinate comparisons fail for a single totally
unimodular inequality, and the arguments of this paper provide neither such a count nor closure and
tail estimates for general totally unimodular systems. Order constraints are the totally unimodular
case in which the transport count, the linear-programming closure and the tails are all complete.
Overlapping resource constraints, affine images of simplices, general integer ranges in order systems,


                                                   69
extra constraints that cut a graph chart, and nonlinear state recurrences are also outside the
theorems of this section.

Relation to earlier work. The geometric ingredients are classical: order polytopes have zero-one
vertices and are triangulated by order simplices [43]; isotonic optimization uses the common-quantile
coupling [5]; totally unimodular systems have integral vertices [25]; and implicit elimination of states
inside validated global search for dynamic models is established [47]. The new element is again the
composition: an expected near-optimal row count under one finite independent-noise law for each
domain class, sound closure tests adapted to the domain, and an exact same-draw fallback with a
polynomial-time evaluable output.


7     Conditional recourse: a small nonconvex core
Conditional optimization can reduce a global search to a few coordinates. We call these coordinates
v ∈ [0, 1]k the core, and write z for the residual variables. At each core point an oracle optimizes
over one fixed residual feasible set Y , returning an exact value or a certified interval. The core
changes the residual costs, while Y stays the same. A few design or control parameters that enter the
operating costs of a large convex or combinatorial model are an example. A two-stage problem has
this form only if its first-stage decisions change second-stage costs and leave second-stage constraints
unchanged; when first-stage decisions enter second-stage right-hand sides or bounds, the method
below fails, as example 7.3 shows. Of the three structural routes in this paper, a small nonconvex
factor, sparse bags, and a small core with tractable conditional recourse, these two sections develop
the third. Its parameter is the core size k together with the ratio L/σ of core curvature to noise.
The number of residual variables, their coupling, and their curvature enter only through the input
length.
    The mechanism has four parts.

    (i) Minimizing over a residual set that does not depend on the core preserves upper coordinate
        curvature. The conditional value V (v) = minz Fγ (v, z) therefore obeys the corrected-corner
        inequality of lemma 3.7 on the core cube, and no residual coordinate is rounded.

 (ii) Independent linear noise on the core coefficients bounds the expected number of near-optimal
      core grid points by a product of k one-dimensional factors. Residual noise, when present, is
      conditioned on.

 (iii) Residual coordinates are never refined, so closure needs a separate global certificate. A few
       restricted recourse calls prove that every global optimizer lies in a small product patch (this
       section) or uses one residual integer label (section 8).

 (iv) A base-selected finite law makes failure of these certificates rare enough to pay for an exact
      fallback on the same draw (section 2.3).

its encoding length.

7.1      The conditional value function
Definition 7.1 (Core–recourse instance). A core–recourse instance consists of integers k, nR ≥ 0
with n = k + nR ≥ 1, a nonempty compact residual set Y ⊆ RnR , an explicit rational polynomial
F0 (v, z) of total degree at most d in the core variables v ∈ Rk and residual variables z ∈ RnR , a
rational L > 0 with
                      ∂ 2 F0
                                                 i = 1, . . . , k, (v, z) ∈ [0, 1]k × conv Y ,
                                                                                               
                             (v, z) ≤ L                                                                              (28)
                       ∂vi2

and a rational noise half-width σ > 0. The feasible set is X = [0, 1]k × Y . We write C and R for
the core and residual index sets. In this section Y = [0, 1]nR ; section 8 uses integer sets.
     The essential restriction is that the residual feasible set does not depend on the core: the core
enters only the objective. Core coordinates with other rational ranges can be mapped affinely onto
[0, 1]; this rescales L and the physical noise, and the rescaled data are part of the input. A valid
L can always be computed from monomial coefficients, but the numerical factors below use the
supplied value, which may be much smaller. A sharper value is a certified premise: the encoding of
its proof is part of I, and the work of checking the proof is part of the stated work bound. For the
sampled objective Fγ (v, z) = F0 (v, z) + γCT v + γR

Lemma 7.2 (Conditional values). Let G ≥ sup{                                                  k

                                                           P

 (a) The function V (v) − γCT v depends on γR but not on γC .

 (b) For every i ∈ C and every v, the function t 7→ V (v + tei ) − Lt2 /2 is concave on the interval
     where v + tei ∈ [0, 1]k .

 (c) For every nonempty closed Y ′ ⊆ Y , the function VY ′ is G-Lipschitz with respect to the
     maximum norm on the core: |VY ′ (v) − VY ′ (w)| ≤ G ∥v − w∥∞ .
None of these statements uses convexity of the residual problem.

                                                         71
    Part (b) is the reason for fixed residual feasibility. It makes V satisfy the curvature hypothesis
of theorem 3.4 in dimension k: for a dyadic cell of side hj = 2−j in the core cube,

                                                                                  kLh2j
                          min V (v) ≥          min        V (v) − Ej ,     Ej =         .                 (30)
                          v∈C             v corner of C                            8
Only core coordinates contribute to Ej . When residual feasibility depends on the core, (30) fails,
even for linear data.

Example 7.3 (Core-dependent feasibility). Let k = nR = 1, F0 (v, z) = z, and replace the fixed
residual set by Y (v) = {z ∈ [0, 1] : z ≥ v − 13 , z ≥ 13 − v}. Then ∂vv F0 = 0 and V (v) = v − 31 . At
every level j the cell containing 13 has corners at distances hj /3 and 2hj /3 from 13 , because 2j ̸≡ 0
(mod 3). Its minimum corner value is hj /3, while minC V = 0. Thus (30) fails with L = 0, and for
every L > 0 it fails at all levels with hj < 8/(3kL). A search based on it would report the value
interval [Uj − Ej , Uj ], which excludes f ∗ = 0.

    The corrected-corner inequality also shows why conditional values must be exact or certified,
rather than computed by rounding the residual variables to a grid. Rounding residual variables
reintroduces a discretization error whose variation over core states grows with the number of residual
variables, and normalizing grid messages does not remove it.
