min-marginals. These examples concern the retention rule; they are not hardness results.

The core search. A conditional oracle receives a rational core point v. In exact mode it returns
V (v) and a residual point z ∈ Y with Fγ (v, z) = V (v). In certified mode it also receives a rational
accuracy η > 0 and returns rationals ℓ ≤ V (v) ≤ u with u − ℓ ≤ η and a residual point z ∈ Y with
Fγ (v, z) = u. For uniform notation, exact mode returns ℓ = u = V (v).
    The core search processes levels j = 0, 1, . . .. At level 0 the only generated cell is [0, 1]k . At level
j ≥ 1 the generated cells are the 2k children of every cell retained at level j − 1; the full level-j grid
is never enumerated. At every level the search queries the oracle once at every distinct corner of a
generated cell. In certified mode the accuracy is

                                ηj = Ej     (k ≥ 1),           ηj = 4−j   (k = 0),                        (31)

                                                          72
and in exact mode we put ηj = 0. Let Uj be the least upper value u returned at this level, attained
at a corner cj with completion zj . A generated cell is retained if and only if
                                             min         ℓ(v) − Ej ≤ Uj .                          (32)
                                         v corner of C

The retained hull Hj is the smallest box containing all retained cells. For k = 0 there is one empty
core cell, which is always retained, Ej = 0, each level makes one query, and Hj is a point. In that
case a certified answer of width 4−j gives only an incumbent within 4−j of f ∗ , never an exact one;
the positive allowance ηj is what the stopping analysis uses.
Proposition 7.5 (Core search). Let εj = Ej + ηj ; thus εj = Ej in exact mode, εj = 2Ej in certified
mode with k ≥ 1, and εj = 4−j in certified mode with k = 0. On every draw and for every admissible
oracle answer:
 (a) for every global minimizer (a, z ∗ ) of Fγ on X, a retained cell contains a, so a ∈ Hj ;
  (b) f ∗ ≤ Uj ≤ f ∗ + εj , and cj is a corner of a retained cell, so cj ∈ Hj ;
  (c) every retained cell has a corner v with V (v) ≤ f ∗ + 2εj .
Suppose the core coefficients γC are independent with law Uσ,M and independent of γR , and M ≥ 2J .
Conditionally on γR , for every j ≤ J the expected number of points v of the full grid (hj Z)k ∩ [0, 1]k
with V (v) ≤ f ∗ + 2εj is at most Qex in exact mode and Qap in certified mode. The expected number
of oracle calls through level J is at most 2k + 8k JQ, with Q = Qex or Q = Qap respectively.
    The expectation statement uses a deterministic grid fixed before sampling. Each retained cell is
charged to a near-optimal corner, and near-optimality of a fixed grid point is a condition on the
true function V , so arbitrary noise-dependent choices of the oracle within its error bounds do not
affect the count. The bound is theorem 3.4 applied, conditionally on the residual noise, to V − γCT v,
which has upper coordinate curvature L and does not depend on γC . With exact values the search
is a variant of the corrected-corner search of theorem 3.9 on the core, with the incumbent taken
over the current level. The proof is in appendix E.2.

7.2    Global containment and closure certificates
The core search alone does not finish the problem: it localizes the core but says nothing about
                                                                                     Q
residual coordinates. The next two lemmas supply sound certificates. Here Y = i∈R [ℓi , ui ] is a
box.
    Let c be a point of a core box H, let zc ∈ Y , and let r > 0. The residual patch is
                                   Y                                          
                             P =         max{ℓi , zc,i − r}, min{ui , zc,i + r} .
                                   i∈R

For each i ∈ R with ℓi < zc,i − r form the lower slab Yi− = {z ∈ Y : zi ≤ zc,i − r}, and for each
i ∈ R with zc,i + r < ui the upper slab Yi+ = {z ∈ Y : zi ≥ zc,i + r}. No slab is formed on a side
where the patch reaches an original bound; a singleton slab at an original bound could contain an
optimizer and destroy the certificate.
Lemma 7.6 (Excluded slabs). The slabs cover Y \ P . Let λout be at most the least conditional
value VY ± (c) over all slabs (λout = +∞ if there are none), let U ≥ V (c), and let w∞ (H) be the
         i
largest coordinate width of H. If
                                       λout − U > 2G w∞ (H),                                 (33)
then for every v ∈ H every minimizer of Fγ (v, ·) on Y lies in P . If moreover H contains the core of
every global minimizer, then every global minimizer of Fγ on X lies in H × P .

                                                          73
    The certificate needs a certified lower bound on every slab. A feasible value on a slab, which is
all an approximate solver returns by itself, does not suffice: a better competitor could be hidden in
the slab. It also needs restricted recourse on slabs, so the conditional oracle must accept arbitrary
residual subboxes. This is the box-stability requirement of the following sections.
    Once global containment is certified, two sound local tests finish the problem. Let Q ⊆ [0, 1]n be
a box containing every global minimizer, with midpoint m and maximum-norm radius rQ , and put
                       n         X               o                  n         X                o
            K2 ≥ max 1, max          sup |∂ij F0 | ,        K3 ≥ max 1, max       sup |∂ijl F0 | ,    (34)
                             i           n                              i                 n
                                 j [0,1]                                      j,l [0,1]

rational bounds computed from monomial coefficients.

Lemma 7.7 (Sign fixing and convexity tests).

 (a) If the original lower bound of coordinate i lies in the ith interval of Q and ∂i Fγ > 0 on Q,
     every global minimizer has xi at that bound; symmetrically for an upper bound and ∂i Fγ < 0.
     The midpoint test ±∂i Fγ (m) > K2 rQ is a sufficient condition for the sign; for quadratics the
     sign can be decided exactly. Every global minimizer lies in the box Q′ obtained by fixing all
     coordinates that pass.

 (b) Let m′ and rQ′ be the midpoint and radius of Q′ and let g0 > 0. If the Hessian of F0 in the
     unfixed coordinates satisfies ∇2 F0 (m′ ) − (K3 rQ′ + g0 )I ⪰ 0, then Fγ is g0 -strongly convex on
     Q′ , its unique minimizer on Q′ is the unique global minimizer of Fγ on X, and (Q′ , g0 ) is an
     implicit patch output (definition 2.5).

 (c) If F0 is quadratic and its Hessian in the unfixed coordinates is positive semidefinite, then every
     exact minimizer of the convex quadratic program minQ′ Fγ is a global minimizer of Fγ on X.

    The tests may fix only original bounds, and only bounds that lie in the current interval. Fixing
an artificial patch endpoint would be unsound. A coordinate whose derivative vanishes at a contained
optimizer never passes a strict sign test. All tests in this section are exact rational computations
and are sound on every draw; the probabilistic analysis only shows that they pass early.

7.3   Exact recourse for quadratic objectives
Definition 7.8 (Box-stable exact recourse). Let F0 (x) = 12 xT Ax + aT x + c0 be a rational quadratic
on [0, 1]n with core C and residual R. A box-stable exact recourse oracle receives a rational core point
v ∈ [0, 1]k , a rational box i∈R [ℓ′i , u′i ] ⊆ [0, 1]nR , possibly with degenerate intervals, and rational
                             Q

linear coefficients γ, and returns an exact rational global minimizer of z 7→ Fγ (v, z) on that box and
its value. Its bit work is bounded by a polynomial in the query length whose exponent does not
depend on k.

   An oracle that solves only the unrestricted residual problem does not satisfy this definition, and
the slab certificate cannot be run with it.

Theorem 7.9 (Exact quadratic recourse). Let F0 (x) = 12 xT Ax + aT x + c0 be a rational quadratic
on X = [0, 1]n , let C be a supplied core of size k ≥ 1, and let L > 0 be rational with Aii ≤ L for
i ∈ C (verified). Assume a box-stable exact recourse oracle. There is a power of two M , computed
from the base instance with log2 M ≤ poly(I), such that for the ambient perturbation model with
marginal law Uσ,M the following hold.



                                                       74
  (i) On every draw the algorithm returns a rational global minimizer of Fγ on X and the optimal
      value, of bit length poly(I).

 (ii) The expected bit work, including oracle calls and the computation of M , is at most 8k Qex poly(I).

No growth, uniqueness, nondegeneracy, or residual convexity premise is used.

    The polynomial factor depends on the oracle’s bound but has an exponent independent of
k. The algorithm runs the core search in exact mode; at each level it tests lemma 7.6 with at
most 2nR additional oracle calls and then the tests of lemma 7.7. If they have not passed by a
base-computed level J, it enumerates the 3n faces of the box on the same draw (lemma C.3). The
proof in appendix E.5 shows that under point growth g0 and active-gradient margin τ , both chosen
from base data, all tests pass by level J, and that the growth and active-gradient tails make the
fallback rare. The stopping depth depends only on base data: no rational-reconstruction bound
that grows with the sampling precision is needed.

Corollary 7.10 (Residual forests). In theorem 7.9, suppose that deleting the core C from the
interaction graph of A (vertices [n], an edge ij when i ̸= j and Aij ≠ 0) leaves a forest. Then the
box-stable exact recourse oracle exists, and the expected bit work is 8k Qex poly(I). This bound is
fixed-parameter in the size k of the supplied feedback vertex set and in L/σ.

    The oracle is the exact algorithm of Del Pia and Khajavirad [17] for box-constrained quadratic
programs whose interaction graph is a forest: fixing core coordinates and adding linear terms change
only linear coefficients, and restricting a residual box and rescaling its nondegenerate intervals keeps
the residual graph a forest. The residual quadratic need not be convex, have bounded negative
inertia, or be separable. The corollary is not fixed-parameter in treewidth alone: a graph of treewidth
two can require a large feedback vertex set, and box-constrained quadratic programming is strongly
NP-hard at treewidth two [17].

7.4   Certified convex recourse for polynomial objectives
For nonlinear recourse the conditional values are usually irrational, and exact comparisons between
them are unavailable. The theorem below uses only certified intervals for conditional values and
feasible rational completions.

Definition 7.11 (Box-stable certified recourse). For an explicit rational polynomial F0 on [0, 1]n
with core C, a box-stable certified recourse oracle receives a rational core point v, a rational residual
box Y ′ ⊆ [0, 1]nR , rational linear coefficients γ, and a rational η > 0. It returns a rational ℓ and a
rational z ∈ Y ′ with ℓ ≤ VY ′ (v) ≤ Fγ (v, z) ≤ ℓ + η. Its bit work is polynomial in the query length,
including the length of η, with an exponent independent of k.

Lemma 7.12 (Certified convex recourse). Fix d. If ∇2RR F0 ⪰ 0 on [0, 1]n , a box-stable certified
recourse oracle exists with bit work polyd in the query length. Moreover, the returned lower bound
can be taken as the tangent bound

                          ℓ = f (z) + min′ ∇f (z)T (y − z),     f = Fγ (v, ·),                      (35)
                                      y∈Y

which a verifier checks by evaluating f and ∇f at the rational point z and choosing one endpoint
per coordinate.



                                                   75
    The proof (appendix E.4) uses weak convex optimization over a capped epigraph [23, Defini-
tion 2.1.10 and Corollary 4.2.7] to find a feasible point of small objective error; convexity alone
makes (35) a global lower bound, and a smoothness bound along a feasible segment makes its gap
small. No strong convexity modulus is used. The premise ∇2RR F0 ⪰ 0 says that the polynomial
q(x, y) = y T ∇2RR F0 (x) y is nonnegative on [0, 1]n × RnR . The algorithms do not decide it; it is
certified. One certificate writes q = σ0 + i σi xi (1 − xi ) with sum-of-squares polynomials σ0 , σi in
                                           P

(x, y) given by rational Gram matrices; it is checked by a polynomial identity and exact rational
semidefiniteness tests. A structural certificate writes F0 as a sum of a polynomial in v, terms that
are affine in z for fixed v, and even powers (aT z + b(v))2p with rational vectors a and polynomials b;
each term has a positive semidefinite residual Hessian. A sum-of-squares representation of F0 itself
is not a certificate: a nonnegative polynomial need not be convex.
Theorem 7.13 (Polynomial recourse with certified conditional values). Fix d. Let F0 be an explicit
rational polynomial of degree at most d on X = [0, 1]n , let C be a supplied core of size k ≥ 0, and
let L > 0 be rational with (28) (verified by monomial bounds or certified). Assume a box-stable
certified recourse oracle; by lemma 7.12 one exists when ∇2RR F0 ⪰ 0 on X (certified). There is a
power of two M , computed from the base instance with log2 M ≤ polyd (I), such that for the ambient
perturbation model with marginal law Uσ,M the following hold.
  (i) On every draw the algorithm returns an exact global optimizer of Fγ on X. On ordinary
      draws the output is an implicit patch output (S, zS , Q, g0 ) of length polyd (I), with a global
      proof record. On the remaining draws it is the output of the exact fallback of theorem 3.20
      on the same draw: an algebraic output (definition 2.5(d)) of the lexicographically least global
      minimizer and of the optimal value, whose length is at most B polyd (I) for a base-computable
      B = 2polyd (I) .
 (ii) The expected bit work and the expected size of the global proof record are at most 8k Qap polyd (I).
(iii) A q-bit evaluation of an implicit patch output costs polyd (I + q) bit operations; the expected
      cost of a q-bit evaluation of the output, over all draws, is polyd (I + q).
No growth, uniqueness, or nondegeneracy premise is used.
    The ordinary branch decides no exact comparison between irrational conditional values. The
patch descriptor determines one point because Fγ is strongly convex on Q, and the global proof
record shows that this point is the global optimizer; the record contains the pruning trace and has
only an expected size bound. An expanded algebraic representation of the optimizer can have large
degree even on an ordinary draw; the output contract does not include one. For k = 0 there is
no core, and each level makes one certified call on the whole residual box with accuracy 4−j (31);
the problem need not be convex, since the oracle is an assumption of the theorem. In the convex
specialization of lemma 7.12 it is a convex polynomial on a box with ambient noise, and the patch
certificate replaces any need to decide uniqueness or the active set.
    The residual objective may be nonlinear, nonseparable, dense, and not strongly convex. For
example, F0 (v, z) = ψ(v) + i zi4 + ∥Dz − Ev∥2 has convex recourse for any rational matrices D, E
                            P

and any polynomial ψ. The core curvature bound need not reflect the size of the core–residual
coupling, and a small core need not arise from a low-rank quadratic convexifier.
Proposition 7.14 (A core not given by a low-rank convexifier). For integers m ≥ 1 and rationals
λ, T > 0, t0 ∈ [0, 1], β ∈ Qm , let
                                                                        m              m
                         λ                                              X             X         4
               F (t, z) = (t − t0 )2 + T t P (z) − β T z,     P (z) =         zi4 +         zi        ,
                         2                                              i=1           i=1


                                                   76
on [0, 1] × [0, 1]m . Then ∂tt F = λ, the residual problem is convex for every t, and the core–residual
cross derivatives grow linearly with T . If a symmetric positive semidefinite matrix K makes
F + 12 (t, z)T K(t, z) convex on the box, then Kzz ≻ 0; in particular rank K ≥ m.

    Theorem 7.13 applies to this family with one core coordinate and L = λ, so its numerical factor
does not depend on T ; the coefficient T enters only the polynomial factor through its bit length. By
the proposition, no fixed positive semidefinite quadratic correction of rank independent of m makes
the objective convex. The proposition excludes only fixed quadratic corrections. A one-coordinate
correction Ct log t with C of order T max P does convexify the family, so the example separates the
theorem from small-rank quadratic convexifiers, not from all difference-of-convex representations. It
is not a hardness result.

7.5   Core-only noise and strongly convex recourse
Theorem 7.13 perturbs every coordinate. Residual noise is what produces full point growth and
nonzero active gradients at residual bounds with high probability. Under core-only noise neither
holds in general: the residual optimal set can be a segment on every draw, and residual bound
multipliers can vanish identically. A residual modulus restores the patch closure developed here.
    It is one sufficient condition for that closure, not a necessary condition for evaluating every
full optimizer. The exact-arithmetic companion [20] already proves a full selected-point evaluator
for residual-convex cubics on product boxes without a residual modulus or a joint convexifier. Its
selector is the lexicographically least optimal core followed by the least-norm point in its optimal
residual fiber. Under one base-selected finite core-noise law, point and value evaluation has expected
cost k O(k) (1 + L/σ)k poly(I + q). For general residual-convex quartics, the same companion gives
the core-noise point obstruction reproduced in theorem 9.10(c). Thus the degree-four obstruction
does not rule out the degree-three result. The theorem below treats fixed degree with a uniform
residual modulus and returns an exact patch descriptor.

Theorem 7.15 (Core-only noise with strongly convex recourse). Fix d. Let F0 , X = [0, 1]n , C,
and L be as in theorem 7.13, with k ≥ 1 and nR ≥ 1, and let µ > 0 be rational with

                                    ∇2RR F0 (x) ⪰ µI      (x ∈ X)                                  (36)

(certified). There is a power of two M , computed from the base instance with log2 M ≤ polyd (I),
such that for the core-only perturbation model with marginal law Uσ,M (all residual coefficients
unperturbed) the conclusions (i)–(iii) of theorem 7.13 hold. The ordinary output is an implicit patch
output in which some original core and residual bounds may be fixed.

    No residual interiority, strict complementarity, active-set, or growth premise is used, and residual
bound multipliers may vanish identically. The numerical factor contains the core curvature L only.
The modulus µ and the size of mixed derivatives enter the polynomial factor through the bit lengths
of the derived constants. By proposition 2.6, the exact sampled optimizer is within kσ of minX F0
in original objective value, independently of the number of residual variables. With σ = ε/(2k) and
an evaluation of accuracy ε/2, this certifies an ε-approximate solution of F0 on every draw, with
expected work 8k [3 + (1 + k)kL/ε]k times a polynomial in the input length. That is polynomial
in 1/ε for fixed k and L, and polynomial in I and 1/ε only when L is polynomially bounded; a
binary-encoded L can be exponentially large.
    The class admits a fixed rank-k quadratic convexifier: the Schur complement of the residual
block and (34) give ∇2 F0 + α diag(IC , 0) ⪰ 0 for α = K2 + K22 /µ. Its curvature can be much
larger than L. The contribution of theorem 7.15 is that the numerical factor keeps L/σ under

                                                  77
core-only noise, despite changing and weakly active residual bounds; it is not that the class       lacks
                                                                                                     
                                                                        2               2        L T
a low-rank convexifier. For example, the Hessian of F0 (v, z) = Lv /2 + T vz + µz /2 is T µ , so
a core quadratic convexifier must have scale α ≥ max{0, T 2 /µ − L}. The direct count uses L/σ
even when this scale grows as T 2 /µ. The companion’s full selected-point theorem under a supplied
convexifier instead uses k O(k) (1 + α/σ)k poly(I + q); it applies to cubic objectives convexified on the
feasible polytope, or to fixed-degree objectives whose convexification is convex on all of Rn . These
output and convexity premises differ from the patch theorem here.
    The proof (appendix E.6) has three new ingredients. First, the residual selector s(v) =
arg minz∈Y F0 (v, z) is unique and (K2 /µ)-Lipschitz, the conditional value is continuously differen-
tiable, and projected growth gV of V at its optimal core lifts to full point growth min{gV /(1 +
2K22 /µ2 ), µ/4}. The growth tail of theorem 3.16 applies to V on the k-dimensional core box.
Second, core noise keeps the optimal core away from changes of the residual active set, with a
coefficient-independent probability bound. Third, small residual multipliers can be released without
losing a positive Hessian modulus. We state the last two ingredients because they are the reason
the theorem needs no strict complementarity.
    For a face K of [0, 1]k with q ≥ 1 free coordinates, identified with a q-dimensional box, let

Proposition 7.16 (Active-set changes are rarely near the optimum). In the setting of theorem 7.15,
let K be a face of [0, 1]k with q ≥ 1 free coordinates.

 (a) SK and EK are compact semialgebraic sets of dimension at most q − 1. If a ∈ relint K and
     0 < η ′ < dist(a, SK ), the relative ball of radius η ′ about a lies in relint K, and the bound status
     of each residual coordinate of s(v) is constant on it.

 (b) There is a base-computable D∗ with log D∗ ≤ polyd (I) such that EK lies in the zero set of a
     nonzero polynomial of degree at most D∗ .

 (c) If γK has independent coordinates with law Uσ,M , then for every δ ≥ 0

    At an optimal core a ∈ relint K the free coordinates of the noise satisfy γK = −∇K V0 (a), so (c)
bounds the probability that the optimal core is close to a change of the residual active set. Part (b)
uses fixed-block quantifier elimination only as an analysis device: the algorithm never computes SK
or the polynomial. Part (c) combines a tube estimate for singular algebraic sets [6] with a jitter
argument that covers every atom of the grid, including atoms on EK . The conclusion is a stable
residual active set near the optimum; it gives no positive lower bound on active multipliers.

Proposition 7.17 (Releasing small residual multipliers). In the setting of theorem 7.15, let
x∗ = (a, s(a)) with a in the relative interior of a face K with q ≥ 1 free coordinates. Suppose the
residual bound status is constant on the relative ball of radius η > 0 about a, ∇K Vγ (a) = 0, and



                                                   78
∇2K Vγ (a) ⪰ 2gI with g > 0. For an active residual coordinate i let λi = ±∂zi F0 (x∗ ) ≥ 0 be its
inward multiplier. Put H = K2 /µ, Kλ = max{1, K3 (1 + H)3 }, and
                                 n K η2         µg o                    nµ       min{g, µ/2} o
                                    λ
                       θ = min            ,          ,        ν = min        ,                .
                                    2         4nR Kλ                     2        1 + 2H 2
Let J be any set of active residual coordinates with λi ≤ θ. Then the Hessian of F0 at x∗ , restricted
to the free coordinates of K, the residual coordinates strictly inside their bounds, and J , is at least
νI. The same holds for every principal submatrix.

    The bound uses the two-sided ball in the free core directions: nonnegativity of a multiplier on
both sides of a and a Hessian bound force a small multiplier to have a small gradient, which controls
the cross terms between released coordinates and the core. The algorithm never identifies the active
set or classifies multipliers. It fixes the coordinates whose derivative signs are certified, which include
all active residual coordinates with λi > θ once the patch is small, and tests the Hessian on the rest.
Remark 7.18 (Interior recourse). If, in addition, every conditional residual minimizer is interior to
the residual box (certified, for example by strict inward derivative signs on the residual facets), then
no residual coordinate is active. After the active core coordinates are fixed, point growth alone
gives a positive Hessian on all remaining coordinates, and the proof of theorem 7.15 needs neither
proposition 7.16 nor proposition 7.17. No quantitative distance from the residual bounds is used,
and an accepted patch may touch them.
    The following examples show which hypotheses cannot be dropped. Proofs are in appendix E.7.

Example 7.19 (Flat residual fibers). On [0, 1]3 with core v and residual (z1 , z2 ) let Fγ (v, z) =
(v − 21 )2 + (z1 − vz2 )2 + γv. The residual Hessian is positive semidefinite, ∂vv F0 ≤ 4, and for every
γ ∈ (−1, 1) the optimal core aγ = 12 − γ2 is unique with projected growth constant one. The optimal
set {(aγ , aγ s, s) : s ∈ [0, 1]} is a segment, and the full Hessian is indefinite at every point with
z1 ̸= vz2 . Hence no full-dimensional box in X carries a convex restriction of Fγ . Residual convexity
without a modulus does not support the closure of theorem 7.15. If the residual term is replaced by
(z1 − 12 − v(z2 − 12 ))2 + (z2 − 12 )4 , the residual problem is strictly convex with the unique interior
minimizer ( 12 , 21 ) for every v, the optimizer (aγ , 12 , 12 ) is unique, and still the full Hessian is indefinite
at points arbitrarily close to it. Strict residual convexity and interior uniqueness therefore do not
replace the uniform modulus µ.

Example 7.20 (Vanishing multipliers). On [0, 1]2 let F0 (v, y) = ϕ(v) + y 2 , with residual y. The
residual modulus is 2, the selector is s ≡ 0, and the multiplier of the active bound y = 0 vanishes
identically, on every draw of core-only noise. The coupled variant F0 = (v − 21 )2 + (y + (v − 12 )2 )2
has multiplier 2(v − 21 )2 , which vanishes at an interior core point while the conditional value has
quadratic growth. A closure that requires residual multipliers bounded away from zero fails here;
proposition 7.17 keeps such coordinates free.

Example 7.21 (Why active core coordinates are fixed first). On [0, 1]2 let Fγ (v, y) = 14 v 2 + vy +
1 2                                                         1 2
2 y + γv with residual y. The selector is y = 0, Vγ (v) = 4 v + γv, the residual modulus is one, and
the full Hessian 1/21 1
                       1
                          is indefinite. The multiplier λ(v) = v is nonnegative on the feasible side
only; at the core vertex v = 0 it vanishes while its derivative is one. For γ > 0 the optimum is (0, 0),
the core derivative there is γ, and the closure succeeds only because the sign test fixes v = 0 first.
Proposition 7.17 applies only in the free directions of the optimal core face.




                                                         79
7.6   Scope
All results in this section optimize the sampled objective exactly; none recovers an optimizer of
F0 . The core, the curvature bound L, the residual convexity or modulus, and the box-stable oracle
are inputs. The results do not recognize a good core, and they do not decide residual convexity
of an arbitrary polynomial. The residual set must not depend on the core (example 7.3), and
the oracle must handle every residual subbox. The numerical factors depend on the magnitude
of L/σ, so these are expected polynomial bounds only when that ratio is polynomially bounded.
The certificates are exact and sound on every draw, while their early success, the stopping depth,
and the size of the sampling grid rest on growth and active-gradient tails for the base-selected
finite law (theorem 3.16 and lemma 3.17). All four results have polynomial sampling precision.
For a fixed oracle implementation, corollary 3.21 therefore replaces the instance-selected grid
size by one depending only on the input length, with the same output contracts and the same
numerical factors. Under core-only noise, residual convexity without a modulus is not enough for
the closure used here (example 7.19). The companion’s residual-convex cubic theorem shows that
other completion certificates can evaluate full points without such a modulus; its general quartic
obstruction prevents an unrestricted extension with the same polynomial evaluation guarantee
unless the stated complexity consequences hold. For integer residual variables the patch and Hessian
tests are replaced by label certificates; section 8 treats that case, together with core-only noise for
integer flows.


8     Integer recourse, flows, and random components
This section treats residual variables that are native integers. The core search of section 7 applies
unchanged, because it uses only the conditional value function on a fixed residual set. Closure
changes. A patch and a Hessian test are meaningless for integer coordinates; instead, a few bound-
restricted recourse calls certify that one residual integer label is optimal throughout the retained
core hull, and an exact solver for the small continuous core completes the problem. Under core-only
noise residual ties can persist on every draw, and for integer flows and totally unimodular systems
the label certificate is replaced by one that works with the whole set of tied optimal labels. The
section ends with two results of a different kind: exact optimization of mixed boxes under strong
noise through random components, and an exact lattice argument for separable integer problems
with a low-rank concave part.
    Table 5 lists the results. The factor Qex is defined in (27), and cd is the constant of theorem 8.1.

8.1   An exact solver for a small continuous core
All label certificates end with the same task: minimize an explicit polynomial exactly over a box of
small dimension. A general exact method returns separate defining polynomials for the coordinates;
pairing their roots costs a product of degrees, and it does not by itself say which coordinate roots
belong to the same optimizer. The following solver returns all coordinates and the optimal value
as polynomials in one common real algebraic number, with a base that is exponential only in the
dimension.
Theorem 8.1 (Exact box solver through joint critical limits). Fix d ≥ 1, and let D be the least even
integer greater than d. There are a constant cd , depending only on d, and a deterministic algorithm
with the following properties. The input is an explicit rational polynomial f of degree at most d in k
variables and a nonempty closed rational box B ⊆ Rk ; let H be its length. The algorithm returns a
global minimizer x∗ of f on B and the value f (x∗ ) in the algebraic format of definition 2.5(d): one

Coordinates whose interval is a point have constant maps. As an auxiliary representation, used
only for comparisons, the algorithm also returns a nonzero integer polynomial with the root f (x∗ )
and an isolating interval of that root. The bit work and the output length are at most ckd polyd (H).
For every q ≥ 0, a rational point within Euclidean distance 2−q of x∗ , a feasible rational point with
certified objective gap at most 2−q , and a rational interval of width at most 2−q containing f (x∗ )
are computed within ckd polyd (H + q). No genericity is assumed: ties, singular critical points, and
positive-dimensional sets of minimizers are allowed.
    For each face of the box, the solver deforms the restricted objective by ε i xD
                                                                                                        P
                                                                                       i . Written in
z = 1/ε, the deformed stationary equations have the pure-power leading monomials xD−1        i    over
the field of rational functions in z and after every finite specialization of z. In particular this
proves the claim for symbolic ε and for every ε ̸= 0. They therefore form a Gröbner basis, and
the quotient algebra has the fixed dimension (D − 1)r on a face with r free coordinates. At ε = 0
the deformation disappears and the original stationary set can be positive dimensional; the limit
ε → 0 is handled by a branch analysis, not by specializing. View the complex critical points of the
deformed problem as branches parametrized by ε. Call a linear form λT x good if it does not vanish
on the leading coefficient vector of any branch that escapes to infinity as ε → 0, and if it separates
the distinct finite limits of the bounded branches. For a good form, the leading coefficient in z of
the characteristic polynomial of λT x in the quotient algebra vanishes exactly at the projections
of these finite limits, and its derivatives with respect to independent coefficients of λ, divided by
a greatest common divisor, recover all coordinates of each limit as polynomials in its projection.
For other forms the leading coefficient can retain bounded projections of escaping branches, so
its roots need not be projections of limits. A deterministic list of O(r(D − 1)2r ) moment-curve

                                                         81
forms contains a good form. Every listed form is screened by degree and divisibility tests, and a
point reconstructed from any form is kept only after an exact test that it lies in the box. A bad
form that passes the screening can therefore add only feasible candidates, whose values are at least
the minimum. The deformed minimizers have a convergent subsequence whose limit is a global
minimizer of f , and a good form recovers it, so the kept candidate of least value is optimal. The
proof is in appendix F.1; it uses finite-dimensional quotient algebras, simultaneous triangularization,
Puiseux series, and exact univariate algorithms, and lemma F.9 gives the explicit choice cd = 210000D
for one elementary implementation. That constant is a proved bound used to define noise laws
below, not a practical estimate. The ingredients are classical. Recovering all coordinates from one
univariate polynomial and derivatives of a characteristic polynomial is the mechanism of the rational
univariate representation [39], and deformations of stationary systems are standard in critical-point
methods [7]. We give a self-contained proof for boxes, including singular and positive-dimensional
critical sets, because the recourse theorems need its exact contract and its constant-base bound; we
do not claim a new general algebraic complexity result.
Corollary 8.2 (Mixed boxes and exact comparisons). For a mixed box with k continuous coordinates
and native integer coordinates with N1 , . . . , Nt labels, enumerating the labels and applying theorem 8.1
to each gives an exact global minimizer and value, in the algebraic format with the integer labels
listed, in ckd i Ni polyd (H) bit operations. Values of two algebraic outputs of theorem 8.1 can be
              Q

compared exactly, including equality, within ckd polyd (H); the comparison uses the two auxiliary value
polynomials and forms no field containing both.

8.2    Native integer recourse
Definition 8.3 (Native integer recourse). A native integer recourse instance is a core–recourse
instance (definition 7.1) whose residual set is

                                 Y = {z ∈ ZnR : Az ≤ b, ℓ ≤ z ≤ u},

with a rational matrix A, a rational vector b, and integer bounds encoded in binary; the curvature
bound (28) is required on [0, 1]k × i [ℓi , ui ] (verified by monomial bounds or certified). Put
                                        Q

NZ = i (ui − ℓi + 1) and S = k + i (ui − ℓi ). A box-stable exact integer oracle receives a rational
       Q                              P

core point v, rational linear coefficients, and integer bounds ℓ ≤ ℓ′ ≤ u′ ≤ u, and returns the exact
optimal value of min{Fγ (v, z) : z ∈ Y, ℓ′ ≤ z ≤ u′ } and an attaining integer point, or reports
infeasibility, in bit work polynomial in the query length with an exponent independent of k.
    The core changes costs, never feasibility: supplies, right-hand sides, and bounds do not depend
on v. The residual set is nonempty by definition 7.1; one oracle call with the original bounds decides
this before sampling, and an empty Y is reported as such. Exact values are rational because the
core point is rational and residual points are integral. The oracle must handle arbitrary tightened
coordinate bounds, which is how competing labels are excluded.
Theorem 8.4 (Native integer recourse). Fix d. Let a native integer recourse instance with k ≥ 1 be
given, together with a box-stable exact integer oracle. There is a power of two M , computed from the
base instance with log2 M ≤ polyd (I), such that for the ambient perturbation model with marginal
law Uσ,M the following hold.
  (i) On every draw the algorithm returns a global minimizer (v ∗ , z ∗ ) of Fγ on X = [0, 1]k × Y
      and the optimal value: the label z ∗ exactly, and v ∗ and the value in the algebraic format of
      theorem 8.1. Its length is at most ckd polyd (I) on every draw, and a q-bit refinement costs
      ckd polyd (I + q).

                                                    82
 (ii) The expected bit work and the expected size of the global proof record are at most [8k Qex +
      ckd ] polyd (I).

 (iii) If F0 is quadratic, the output is rational on every draw and the expected work is [8k Qex +
       3k ] poly(I).
No growth, label-gap, uniqueness, or residual convexity premise is used.
    For k = 0 there is no core, and one oracle call with the original bounds solves the sampled
problem exactly. For k ≥ 1 the certificate is the following. At the incumbent corner cj with label zj ,
the 2nR restrictions zi ≤ zj,i − 1 and zi ≥ zj,i + 1, each together with the original constraints, have
union exactly Y \ {zj }, however the residual coordinates are coupled. A restriction that leaves the
original bounds (zj,i = ℓi in the first case, zj,i = ui in the second) is empty and receives the value
+∞ without an oracle call, as does a restriction that the oracle reports infeasible. Let Vother (cj ) be
the least of the 2nR values; it is +∞ when all restrictions are empty, that is, when zj is the only
feasible label; the minimum of an empty restriction list is also +∞. If

                                    Vother (cj ) − V (cj ) > 2G w∞ (Hj ),                                (38)

then zj is the unique optimal label at every core point of the retained hull Hj (lemma 7.2(c)), so
every global minimizer lies in Hj × {zj }. The whole slice [0, 1]k × {zj } is feasible and contains a
global minimizer, so theorem 8.1 applied to Fγ (·, zj ) on [0, 1]k finishes the problem, whether the
core minimizers are unique, tied, or form a continuum. Feasible values for the restricted problems
would not suffice; (38) needs their exact optimal values. Distinct integer labels are at unit distance,
so point growth forces (38) at a base-computed level; no label gap is supplied or charged separately.
On the remaining draws the algorithm enumerates the NZ labels on the same draw, solves each core
slice, and keeps the best by exact comparisons (corollary 8.2). It returns only the winning label and
its core representation, so the output bound does not depend on how many labels were examined.
The proof is in appendix F.2.
Corollary 8.5 (Implicit core output). Under the hypotheses of theorem 8.4, an algorithm with
expected bit work 8k Qex polyd (I) returns on ordinary draws the exact label z ∗ and an implicit patch
output for the core slice Fγ (·, z ∗ ), and on the remaining draws the output of theorem 8.4. A q-
bit evaluation of the ordinary output costs polyd (I + q), and its expected cost over all draws is
polyd (I + q).
      The variant replaces the exact core solve by the sign and Hessian tests of lemma 7.7 on the core
coordinates of the certified slice; it pays for an additional active-gradient event instead of the term
ckd .

Separable convex recourse over totally unimodular systems. The main source of exact
integer oracles in this paper is the following classical result.
Proposition 8.6 (Separable convex integer oracles). Let Y = {z ∈ ZnR : Az = b, ℓ ≤ z ≤ u},
where A is an integer totally unimodular matrix (promised; automatic for network matrices), b
                                                                                P
is integral, and the bounds are integral and finite. Let F0 (v, z) = ϕ(v) + i fi (v, zi ) with explicit
rational polynomials of fixed degree, and suppose t 7→ fi (v, t) is convex on [ℓi , ui ] for every v ∈ [0, 1]k
(promised, or certified). Then a box-stable exact integer oracle exists for every rational linear
perturbation, with bit work polynomial in the query length and logarithmic dependence on the
numerical bounds. This includes integer flows on a directed network with integral supplies and arc
bounds; inequality systems Cz ≤ b with C totally unimodular reduce to this form (corollary 8.15).

                                                     83
    The oracle is the proximity-scaling algorithm of Hochbaum and Shanthikumar [24, Theorem 4.3],
which solves separable convex integer minimization over totally unimodular systems with logarithmi-
cally many linear programs in the numerical bounds and function evaluations at prescribed points.
At a rational core point all evaluations are rational of polynomial length; tightening coordinate
bounds and adding linear terms keep the class; and convex tangent extensions beyond the native
intervals supply evaluations if the algorithm requests them (appendix F.2). The convexity premise
concerns each scalar cost on its whole real interval. For networks, optimality also has a short
certificate.

Lemma 8.7 (Marginal potentials). Let Y be the integer flows of a directed network with s nodes,
fixed integral supplies, and arc bounds, where parallel arcs and loops are allowed, and let each
t 7→ fa (t) be convex on [ℓa , ua ]. A feasible z minimizes a fa (za ) over Y if and only if there are
                                                              P

node potentials π for which every arc of the residual network of z has nonnegative reduced cost,
where a forward arc a = (p, p′ ) with za < ua costs fa (za + 1) − fa (za ) and a backward arc with
za > ℓa costs fa (za − 1) − fa (za ), and the reduced cost adds πp − πp′ along the residual arc from p
to p′ . If the residual arc costs are rational numbers of bit length at most Hm , shortest-path distances
from an added zero-cost source supply such potentials, of bit length polynomial in s and Hm .

    This is a certificate, not an algorithm: repeated unit augmentation is not claimed to run in
polynomial time. The characterization holds for arbitrary real convex costs. The bit-length statement
needs rational marginal costs of polynomial length; explicit rational polynomials of fixed degree have
them at rational core points, while an arbitrary convex real function need not. With proposition 8.6,
theorem 8.4 applies to separable convex integer flows and totally unimodular systems whose costs
depend arbitrarily on the core. For instance, F0 (v, z) = ϕ(v) + a ψa (za ) + v T W z with convex ψa
                                                                  P

allows arbitrary core-to-arc coupling W ; the curvature bound L is that of ϕ, and W enters only
through its bit length. Convexity of a fixed-degree univariate ψa on its interval can be verified by
univariate sign determination.

8.3    Core-only noise for integer flows
Under core-only noise the residual costs are not perturbed, and many optimal labels can persist on
every draw. Full point growth then fails, and the competing-label certificate cannot pass when two
labels are optimal at the optimal core. Throughout this subsection the instance is a core–recourse
instance (definition 7.1) in which Y is the set of integer flows of a directed network with s nodes and
nR arcs (parallel arcs and loops allowed), integral supplies, and integral arc bounds [ℓa , ua ] in binary;
in particular Y is nonempty. Nonemptiness is decided before sampling by one exact feasibility linear
program, whose vertices are integral by total unimodularity, and an infeasible network is reported
as such. The sampled objective is
                                          X
                     Fγ (v, z) = ϕ(v) +       fa (v, za ) + γ T v,   v ∈ [0, 1]k , z ∈ Y,             (39)
                                          a

with explicit rational polynomials of degree at most d ≥ 1 in expanded form, each fa (v, ·) convex on
[ℓa , ua ] for every v ∈ [0, 1]k (promised or certified), and a curvature bound L as in (28) (verified or
certified). Only the k core coefficients are perturbed. By proposition 2.6 the sampled optimizer is
within kσ of minX F0 in original objective value, whatever the size of the network.
      A deterministic baseline shows what the noise adds. It uses no flow structure: exact or certified
conditional values on a fixed core grid give an additive approximation for every core–recourse
instance.

For integer flows, proposition 8.6 supplies exact values.

    The grid rounds only the k core coordinates. The error and the number of calls do not depend
on the number of residual variables, which the oracle optimizes exactly; rounding them as well
would reintroduce a dimension-dependent error (example 7.4). The corollary is deterministic and
uses no noise; its proof is in appendix F.3. It is an approximation, not an exact method. With
σ = ε/(2k) and an evaluation of accuracy ε/2, the core-only theorems below also certify an ε-
approximate solution of F0 on every draw (proposition 2.6), but their expected    pwork contains the
factor [3 + (1 + k/2)kL/ε]k , whose dependence on 1/ε is worse than the (2 + kL/(8ε))k calls of
the grid. The smoothed theorems do not improve additive approximation of F0 ; what they add is
exact optimization of the sampled objective, with exact output, on every draw.
    A natural exact certificate selects one flow and proves it optimal on the retained hull by
polynomial potentials. Boundary core optima defeat it: proposition 9.11 gives two parallel arcs
                                                         1
and a two-dimensional core on which, with probability 16    for every grid size, the optimal core is a
vertex with projected growth constant at least one, both flows are optimal there, and no single flow
is optimal on any box at that vertex, while the core search retains such a box at every level. With
m stages, an algorithm that insists on a single-flow certificate and enumerates labels when it fails
does work at least 2m /16 in expectation. The obstruction concerns that certificate; the instance
itself is easy. Two remedies follow: a premise that all optimal cores are interior, and a certificate
that uses all tied flows at once.

Theorem 8.9 (Core-only flow recourse with interior optimal cores). Fix d, and assume, in addition
to the model (39) with k ≥ 1, that for every γ ∈ [−σ, σ]k every optimal core point of Fγ lies in
(0, 1)k (promised, or certified, for instance by ∂i F0 ≤ −σ − τ ′ on {vi = 0} and ∂i F0 ≥ σ + τ ′ on
{vi = 1} for all z in the bounding box a [ℓa , ua ] and some rational τ ′ > 0). There is a power of
                                           Q

two M , computed from the base instance with log2 M ≤ polyd (I), such that for core-only noise with
marginal law Uσ,M the algorithm returns on every draw an optimal integer flow and an algebraic
output of one optimal core and the optimal value, of length ckd polyd (I), with q-bit refinement in
ckd polyd (I + q), and with expected bit work [8k Qex + ckd ] polyd (I). For quadratic objectives the output
is rational.

    The interiority premise is used only in the running-time analysis; correctness holds on every
draw without it, and its sufficient certificate enters no numerical factor. The premise is not vacuous
because Y is nonempty. At the incumbent corner the algorithm computes an optimal flow, a tight
shortest-path tree of its residual network, and the corresponding polynomial potentials, and it tests
exactly whether every residual reduced cost is nonnegative on the whole retained hull; identically
zero reduced costs pass, so persistent ties are allowed. If the test passes, the flow is optimal on
the hull, and the exact solver finishes its whole core slice. The analysis uses a finite family of
reduced-cost polynomials over all labels and trees, fixed before sampling: an optimal core in the
interior is a stationary point of every attaining fixed-flow slice, so the noise equals a negative gradient

                                                     85
there, and the event that the optimal core is near a zero of a chart polynomial has small probability
by an algebraic tube estimate.

All tied flows at once. At a core point c on a face A of the core cube, let z 0 be an optimal flow
and π potentials certifying it (lemma 8.7). The adjusted costs ga (c, t) = fa (c, t) + (πp − πp′ )t of the
arcs a = (p, p′ ) are convex, and their integer minimizers form intervals Ia ∋ za0 , found by binary
search on monotone first differences. The set Y0 = {z ∈ Y : za ∈ Ia for all a} is exactly the set of
optimal flows at c: the potential terms sum to a constant over Y , and the objective gap is a sum
of nonnegative scalar gaps. Minimizing an inward core derivative over Y0 is again a convex flow
problem, because on an interval with at least three integers the adjusted cost is constant, so the
original arc cost is affine there and its inward derivative is convex; two-point intervals use linear
interpolation, and singleton intervals are substituted. Finally, every feasible flow is close to Y0 .
Lemma 8.10 (Proximity to the optimal set). For every z ∈ Y there is z̄ ∈ Y0 with ∥z − z̄∥1 ≤
  P
nR a dist(za , Ia ).
    The deterministic certificate uses these facts on a rational core box D whose intervals in the
normal coordinates of A contain the corresponding bounds, with projection DA onto the face and
c ∈ DA . Let ςi = +1 for a normal coordinate fixed at 0 and ςi = −1 for one fixed at 1, let T1 and
T∞ be the sum and the maximum of the normal widths of D, let RA bound ∥w − c∥ on DA , and let
H ≥ 1 and K ≥ 0 be rational bounds on ∇2v F0 and on |∂vi ∂za fa | on the real bounding domain.
Theorem 8.11 (An optimal-face certificate). Suppose that, with the potentials taken as polynomials
of the core obtained from a tight shortest-path tree at c:
 (a) for every arc, ga (w, t) = ga (w, za0 ) for all w ∈ DA and all integers t ∈ Ia ;

  (b) every first adjusted marginal outside Ia that stays in the native interval is at least µout = nR KT1
      on DA ; and

  (c) if A has normal coordinates, β = mini βi satisfies β − HRA − H2 T∞ > 0, where βi =
      minz∈Y0 ςi ∂vi Fγ (c, z).
Then every flow of Y0 is optimal at every point of DA , and every point of D × Y off the face is
strictly worse than some feasible point on it. If D contains the core of every global minimizer, the
slice [0, 1]k × {z 0 } contains a global minimizer. Conditions (a)–(c) are decided exactly, (a) by at
most d + 1 polynomial identities per arc, (b) by exact polynomial minimization on a rational box
(theorem 8.1), and (c) by one convex flow problem per normal coordinate.
    The certificate is sound on every draw and needs neither uniqueness of the optimal flow nor a
noise assumption. Both (b) and (c) are needed: (c) uses the minimum over all optimal flows, since
a single tied flow can have the wrong inward sign, and without (b) a flow that loses at c can become
optimal off the face. In the instance of proposition 9.11 the certificate with the origin face closes
every box of maximum width less than 2 mini γi .
Theorem 8.12 (Core-only flow recourse at arbitrary core faces). Fix d. For the model (39) with
k ≥ 1 there are an effective function fd and a power of two M , computed from the base instance with

                                       log2 M ≤ fd (k) polyd (I),

such that for core-only noise with marginal law Uσ,M the algorithm returns on every draw an
optimal integer flow and an algebraic output of one optimal core and the optimal value, of

                                                   86
length fd (k) polyd (I), with q-bit refinement in fd (k) polyd (I + q), and with expected bit work
fd (k)Qex polyd (I). No interiority, growth, or uniqueness premise is used, and arbitrarily many
optimal flows may persist.
    At each level the algorithm tries the certificate of theorem 8.11 on every face of the core cube
compatible with the retained hull, at the midpoint of the projected hull, and solves the whole core
slice of z 0 when one passes. The analysis needs three probability bounds: projected growth, distance
of the optimal core from the zeros of a base-only family of chart polynomials (tube estimate), and a
margin τ for the minimal inward derivative over all optimal flows. The last is obtained without
conditioning on a random label: on each fixed face, the optimal core and its whole optimal flow set
do not depend on the normal noise coefficients. A tube controls the distance from a zero set, not
the value of a polynomial away from it. Converting distance into a value margin uses fixed-block
quantifier elimination with its coefficient-height bound, and costs a precision of fd (k) polyd (I) bits.
This is why the sampling precision in theorem 8.12 depends on k.
Corollary 8.13 (Bilinear coupling). If Fγ (v, z) = ϕ(v) + a ψa (za ) + v T W z + γ T v with a rational
                                                                     P

matrix W , fixed degree d ≥ 2, convex ψa on [ℓa , ua ], and ∂ii ϕ ≤ L on [0, 1]k , then the conclusions
of theorem 8.12 hold with log2 M ≤ polyd (I), output length ckd polyd (I), refinement ckd polyd (I + q),
and expected bit work [8k Qex + ckd ] polyd (I). For quadratic ϕ the output is rational.
     All chart polynomials are then affine in the core, and their value margins have polynomial length.
Let p be such a chart, nonzero, with coefficients of bit length at most H0 , on a face identified with
[0, 1]q . If its zero set Z in the cube is nonempty, then p is not constant and |p(x)| ≥ bmin dist(x, Z),
where bmin ≥ 2−H0 is the least absolute value of a nonzero linear coefficient. If p is a nonzero
constant or has no zero in the cube, then |p| is at least its least absolute vertex value, a positive
rational at least 2−(q+1)H0 ; this case includes vertex faces. Identically zero charts are discarded
from the exceptional family, and they pass the non-strict certificate tests. The curvature in the
numerical factor is that of ϕ alone. This would fail for nonlinear coupling: a term vi2 za2 makes the
core curvature grow quadratically with the capacity of arc a.

8.4    Totally unimodular systems
Theorem 8.14 (Totally unimodular recourse). Fix d and k ≥ 1. Let Y = {z ∈ ZnR : Az = b, ℓ ≤
z ≤ u} be nonempty, where A is an integer totally unimodular matrix (promised), b is integral, and
the bounds are finite integers encoded in binary. Let
                                          X
                     Fγ (v, z) = ϕ(v) +        fi (v, zi ) + γ T v   (v ∈ [0, 1]k , z ∈ Y ),
                                           i

where ϕ and the fi are explicit rational polynomials of degree at most d in expanded form, each
fi (v, ·) is convex on [ℓi , ui ] for every v ∈ [0, 1]k (promised or certified), L > 0 is a rational bound (28)
on [0, 1]k × i [ℓi , ui ] (verified or certified), and only the k core coefficients are perturbed, with law
               Q

Uσ,M . Then the conclusions of theorem 8.12 hold. If moreover Fγ = ϕ(v) + i ψi (zi ) + v T W z + γ T v
                                                                                     P

with the premises of corollary 8.13, its conclusions hold.
    These are the premises of the flow model, with the network replaced by a totally unimodular
system; certificates are part of the input length, and nonemptiness is decided by one linear program.
The premises are used: the proof evaluates and differentiates explicit rational polynomials at rational
core points, needs rational unit marginals of polynomial length for its charts, and uses L for the
count. Total unimodularity with arbitrary convex costs, or with succinctly encoded costs, does not
provide these interfaces. Two further ingredients replace network structure. Shortest-path potentials

                                                         87
become a dual vector satisfying at most 2nR adjacent-slope inequalities, which exist because the
piecewise-linear interpolation of the separable costs has an integral optimum over the real totally
unimodular polytope; a dual vertex in a pre-draw box has inverse-basis entries in {0, ±1}, which
bounds the chart coefficients. Directed cycles become conformal circuits of ker A with entries in
{0, ±1}, which gives lemma 8.10 with the same factor nR . Both ingredients use total unimodularity;
neither holds for general integer matrices.
Corollary 8.15 (Bounded totally unimodular inequalities). Under the premises of theorem 8.14 on
the core and the costs, its conclusions hold for a nonempty Y = {z ∈ ZnR : Cz ≤ b, ℓ ≤ z ≤ u} with
C totally unimodular, b integral, and finite integral bounds.
    Add the slack si = bi − Ci z with the explicit bounds 0 ≤ si ≤ bi − minℓ≤z≤u Ci z; the matrix [C I]
is totally unimodular, projection is a bijection onto Y , and slacks have zero cost, so the curvature
bound and all objective values are unchanged. A negative slack bound proves infeasibility.

8.5    Strong noise and random components
The previous results allow every noise scale σ > 0, with numerical ratios such as L/σ in their bounds,
and they need a small core. The following result needs neither a core nor a decomposition, but it
requires a lower bound on the noise: noise that dominates the variation of every coordinate derivative.
          Q

an explicit rational polynomial of degree at most d. If n = 0 the objective is a constant, which is
returned directly; assume n ≥ 1. The primal graph joins two coordinates when they occur together
in a nonzero monomial, so each monomial support is a clique; let ∆+ = max{1, maximum degree}.
Before sampling, compute rational enclosures ∂i F0 ∈ [∂ i , ∂ i ] on the continuous hull, by multiplying

A q-bit evaluation of the output, that is, a rational point within Euclidean distance 2−q of the

                                                     88
     Outside the closed interval in the definition of qi , the sampled derivative ∂i Fγ has a strict sign
throughout the continuous hull, so every global minimizer has xi at the corresponding bound; this
holds for integer coordinates as well, by monotonicity between labels. Call a coordinate bad when
its coefficient lies inside that closed interval, so this sign test leaves it unpinned. These bad events
depend on one coefficient each, so they are independent Bernoulli events with the exact probabilities
qi ; recomputing the enclosures after pinning would destroy this independence and is not needed.
After pinning, every surviving monomial lies in one connected component of the graph induced
by the bad coordinates, so the objective splits into independent component problems, each solved
exactly by corollary 8.2 or, for quadratics, by face enumeration. At most (4∆+ )t−1 connected sets
of size t contain a given vertex, and a subcritical branching bound gives (40). The q-bit evaluation
refines each of the at most n components with q + ⌈log2 (n + 1)⌉ bits, for its point, its value, and its
certified objective gap separately. Combining their point errors in Euclidean norm and adding their
value widths and gaps gives the stated joint contract; an objective gap alone would not bound the
distance to the optimizer on a flat or singular component. The proof is in appendix F.5.
     The algorithm is exact on every draw, also when 4∆+ β ≥ 1, but the theorem then gives no
useful bound. The probabilities qi are not monotone in M : grids of sizes M and 2M are not nested,
so an interval without atoms of one grid can contain atoms of the other. A value of β computed for
one grid size therefore does not transfer to another; qi and β must be computed for the M actually
used. The sufficient condition (41), in contrast, holds for every larger power of two. This sufficient
regime scales with the full derivative variation, with the graph degree, and, for integer coordinates,
with the numerical number of labels; for continuous coordinates of nonquadratic objectives it scales
with the large effective constant cd . The total value is reported as a rational constant plus a sum of
component values. Expanding that sum into one defining polynomial can have degree exponential in
the number of components, and exact sign or equality tests for sums of unrelated algebraic numbers
are not claimed; rational enclosures of the total are obtained by refining each component. The
                                        P
regret bound of proposition 2.6 is σ i wi , which is large in this regime.

at the predetermined level J = log2 M , while every original value Fξ (x) lies in D0 (M       −1) Z. At an

no denominator, and one common grid denominator suffices because the noise enters the original
objective linearly. The auxiliary values may have denominators involving (M − 1)2 , but no lattice
property of them is used. The proof is in appendix F.6.
    The argument uses discrete convexity of the unaries, their exact evaluation, and a common
denominator of their values, and nothing else about them. The fixed degree and the explicit piece
lists guarantee exact evaluation in polynomial time with values of polynomial length. A succinct
                                                 b
encoding would not: evaluating a monomial t2 with a binary exponent at t = 2 produces an integer
of length 2b . Continuous coordinates and coupled integer constraints are outside the theorem:
continuous minima can be irrational, and coupling destroys the separable scalar recourse.
    The numerical ratios αwi /σi are explicit; binary-encoded bounds alone do not make them
small. For binary variables, and more generally for explicitly listed labels, deterministic fixed-
rank algorithms already exist. On {0, 1}n each gj agrees with the affine function gj (0) + cj xj ,
cj = gj (1) − gj (0), so F is a concave function of (T x, cT x); it attains its minimum at a vertex of the
zonotope that is the image of the cube, and this zonotope in dimension k + 1 has O(nk ) vertices.
With explicitly listed labels, the planar polytopes conv{(T·j t, gj (t)) : t ∈ Xj } give a Minkowski-sum
enumeration that, for fixed k, is polynomial in the total number of labels. The theorem does not
improve these cases; it allows binary-encoded intervals without listing their labels.

8.7    Scope
The residual feasible set must not depend on the core, and the exact integer oracle must handle all
tightened coordinate bounds. The flow and totally unimodular results need nonempty residual sets

                                                    90
and separable costs that are convex in each residual coordinate for every core point; they do not cover
nonseparable integer convexity, arbitrary integer matrices, or core-dependent supplies and right-hand
sides. Under core-only noise, single-flow certificates fail at boundary cores (proposition 9.11); the face
certificate needs a parameter-dependent sampling precision for nonlinear coupling, which bilinear
coupling avoids. The strong-field theorem is a strong-noise statement, and the low-rank theorem
uses aligned rather than ambient noise. All results optimize the sampled objective exactly; none
optimizes F0 .
    The native, interior-flow, and bilinear results, including the bilinear totally unimodular case,
and the lattice theorem have polynomial sampling precision. By corollary 3.21, with a fixed oracle
implementation where an oracle is used, their grid size can therefore be replaced by one depending
only on the input length; in the lattice theorem the level cap is reset to log2 M . For the nonlinear
results at arbitrary core faces (theorem 8.12 and the corresponding case of theorem 8.14), the
level cap and the grid exponent are bounded by Fd (k)Pd (I) for an effective nondecreasing Fd . A
common resolution then exists for the instances with a supplied bound K ≥ k, and Fd (K) enters
their sampling, output, and work bounds; taking K = I gives a law that depends only on I, but its
sampling cost need not stay within fd (k) polyd (I) for the actual k. The strong-noise theorem holds
for every M , with qi and β computed for the M used.
    The integer oracles, fixed-block elimination, tube estimates, and connected-set counting are
established tools; what this section proves is their composition with one base-selected finite law,
the label and face certificates, and the bit and output bounds, including exact algebraic output in
which all coordinates and the value within one completion or component share one root.


9     Boundaries
The positive results combine four ingredients: retention of grid cells by a rounding allowance,
objective-only linear noise, a choice of which coefficients are perturbed, and exact closure certificates
of specific kinds. This section shows by explicit families how each ingredient limits the scope. Table 6
lists the results, including the examples of section 7 that concern the same questions.
     None of these results is an unconditional lower bound for all algorithms. Each is either a
limitation of a specified mechanism or a conditional complexity implication. Some mechanism
bounds hold on every draw; others concern an expected count or an event of positive probability.
Several of the instances are easy for a different method, and the text says so. The purpose is to
show that the restrictions of the positive theorems are not artifacts of their proofs: removing them
requires a different algorithmic idea, and in some cases it would resolve an open complexity question.

9.1    The width exponent of global-error retention
The sparse theorems have bounds of the form C p [c + (1 + n/2)Lwmax /(2σ)]p polyd (I). The factor
n inside the bracket comes from the rounding allowance: rounding a feasible point to the corners
of its cell changes the objective by up to nLh2 /8 in expectation, and every coordinate contributes
(section 5.4). We show that, for the retention rule as specified, the resulting power of n is real.

Closure is attempted on the hull Qj = i [ai , bi ], where [ai , bi ] is the intersection, over the bags

on the Hessian of the restricted objective. If no closure succeeds by the stopping depth J, the exact
fallback runs. This is definition 5.17 for a continuous box. The statements below hold for every
implementation of these rules, every sound gradient enclosure, and every sound convexity test.

Proof. (a) Expanding ϕ(xi ) = x2i −2x3i +x4i shows that the only monomials involving two variables are
the products xi xl with {i, l} ∈ E, each with coefficient η ̸= 0. Hence the interaction graph is G. Every
tree decomposition of a graph containing a p-clique has a bag containing that clique, so the treewidth
is at least p − 1. The displayed bags, arranged as the path {1, . . . , p}, {p, p + 1}, {p + 1, p + 2}, . . .,
cover every edge and every variable, and each variable occurs in one bag or in two adjacent bags; so
they form a tree decomposition of width p − 1. The linear and bilinear terms have zero diagonal
second derivatives, so
                               ∂ii Fγ (x) = ϕ′′ (xi ) = 2 − 12xi (1 − xi ) ≤ 2.
There are O(n + p2 ) monomials, each with indices and coefficients of O(log n) bits, and the
decomposition has O(n + p) entries; this gives the bound on I.

    We show by induction that at every level j with h2j ≥ 3p/(10n) all cells are candidates and all

vertex, so (44) gives qC ≤ mβ (v) ≤ f ∗ + 3p/40. Here Ej = nh2j /4 ≥ 3p/40, and Uj ≥ f ∗ because Uj
is the value of a feasible point. Therefore qC − Ej ≤ f ∗ ≤ Uj , and C is retained. As every level-j
cell is retained, every level-(j + 1) cell is a candidate.
    Since all cells are retained, every coordinate projection of every bag covers [0, 1], and Qj = [0, 1]n .
Set all coordinates other than i to zero. Then ∂i Fγ = ϕ′ (xi ) + γi , and ϕ′ (t) = 2t(1 − t)(1 − 2t) gives
the values γi + 3/16 > 0 at xi = 1/4 and γi − 3/16 < 0 at xi = 3/4. A sound enclosure contains
both values, so it is neither strictly positive nor strictly negative, and no coordinate is fixed. The
remaining box is therefore [0, 1]n . At its midpoint c = ( 12 , . . . , 21 ),

                                            ∇2 Fγ (c) = −I + ηAG ,

where AG is the adjacency matrix. Its largest eigenvalue is at most −1 + η(n − 1) < 0. A function
convex on a box is positive semidefinite at interior points, so no sound convexity test can succeed.
     (c) Since h0 = 1 and 3p/(10n) < 1, the level j∗ exists, and h2j∗ +1 = h2j∗ /4 < 3p/(10n) gives

(b). No closure succeeds at levels up to j∗ , so if J > j∗ the fallback does not run before level j∗ , and
the search generates all these cells. The stopping depth of definition 5.15 satisfies hJ ≤ 1/(4A) with

exceeds f (p)I c for all sufficiently large n.

    The lower bound holds on every draw in the noise box, so it also bounds the expected work
for every law supported there, including U1/100,M for every M . It does not contradict theorem 5.2,
whose bound has the same form nO(p) for fixed numerical scales; it shows that this exponent is
necessary for the retention rule as specified. It is not a lower bound for sparse optimization: the
instances are easy, since every optimizer is a vertex and binary dynamic programming with O(2p )
states per bag solves them. Tests on individual cells, rather than on the whole hull, could also prune
these instances much earlier.
    The natural repair is to replace Ej by an allowance for the bag alone, |β| Lh2j /8. Example 7.4
shows that this is unsound for the unperturbed star quadratic: the error comes from minimizing
over the grid outside the bag, and it varies with the bag’s own coordinates. The failure persists on
every draw of small noise, so it is a failure of the smoothed algorithm and not of one exceptional
draw.

Proposition 9.2 (A bag-local allowance is unsound on every draw). Let m = 32 and let F0 be the

C = [ 12 , 1] × [0, 21 ] of the bag {v, z1 }, and C is deleted. The global allowance (1 + m)Lh21 /8 = 33/16
retains C.
