C     Proofs for the sparse polynomial section
This appendix proves the evaluation statement proposition 5.18 and corollary 5.19. The evaluation
lemma lemma C.2 is stated for general polytopes and approximate oracles, because section 6 uses
it for simplices, order polytopes and implicit graphs. The margin bounds of appendix D count
nonsingular stationary points with the following form of the refined Bézout inequality, which is also
used in the proof of lemma 3.17(d).

C.1     Nonsingular common zeros
Lemma C.1 (Nonsingular common zeros). Let k ≥ 1 and D ≥ 1, and let P1 , . . . , Pk ∈ C[u1 , . . . , uk ]
have degree at most D. At most Dk points u ∈ Ck satisfy P (u) = 0 and det ∂P/∂u (u) ̸= 0.

Proof. Take any finite set of N distinct nonsingular common zeros. We show N ≤ Dk , which also
rules out a larger or infinite set. Consider

                          Gi (t, u) = (1 − t)Pi (u) + t(uD
                                                         i − 1),           i = 1, . . . , k.

By the holomorphic implicit function theorem, each of the selected zeros continues to a zero of G(t, ·)
for all sufficiently small complex t. Choose disjoint neighborhoods of the original zeros. Shrinking
the common parameter neighborhood keeps these N continued zeros distinct.
    Homogenize each Gi to degree D in the variables x0 , u1 , . . . , uk , and call the resulting polynomial
Hi (t). Choose an integer r ≥ max{D, k(D − 1), N − 1}. Let Vr be the vector space of homogeneous
polynomials of degree r in these variables, and let
                                                      k
                                                      X
                                           Ir (t) =         Hi (t)Vr−D .
                                                      i=1



                                                       127
The multiplication map from k copies of Vr−D to Vr has matrix entries polynomial in t. At t = 1 its
generators are uD       D
                   i − x0 . Successive division by these monic polynomials gives a unique remainder
whose exponent in each ui is below D. Equivalently, the quotient ring is a free C[x0 ]-module with
basis i uai i , 0 ≤ ai < D. Since r ≥ k(D − 1), its P degree-r part has exactly Dk basis monomials,
     Q
                                                   r−   i
                                                            ai
obtained by multiplying each such product by x0                  . Thus

                                         dim(Vr /Ir (1)) = Dk .

A matrix minor attaining the rank at t = 1 is a nonzero polynomial in t. Away from its finitely
many zeros, the rank is at least that value and dim(Vr /Ir (t)) ≤ Dk .
    Choose such a t sufficiently close to zero that all N continued zeros exist and remain distinct.
Evaluation at their affine representatives (1, u) maps Vr onto CN : products of N − 1 affine linear
factors give Lagrange polynomials for any N distinct affine points, and homogenizing them to degree
r preserves their values at x0 = 1. Every element of Ir (t) vanishes at these points. Hence the
evaluation map factors through the quotient, giving N ≤ dim(Vr /Ir (t)) ≤ Dk .

    This is the weak Bézout bound for nonsingular zeros [7, Theorem 4.106]; Fulton [22] gives the
intersection-theoretic background. The proof above does not need a degree estimate for the other
components of the common zero set.
    The lemma counts only nonsingular zeros. Stationarity equations of a face can have singular zeros
and positive-dimensional components; these are neither excluded nor counted. In every application,
positive point growth is what places the optimizer among the nonsingular zeros.

C.2    Weak convex optimization on a certified patch
We use two problems of Grötschel et al. [23, Section 2.1] for a convex body K ⊆ Rm , with S(K, ε)
the closed ε-neighborhood of K and S(K, −ε) the set of points whose ε-ball lies in K. The weak
separation problem asks, for a rational w and a rational δ > 0, either to assert w ∈ S(K, δ) or to
find a rational π with ∥π∥∞ = 1 and π T x ≤ π T w + δ for all x ∈ S(K, −δ). The weak optimization
problem asks, for a rational objective π and a rational ε > 0, either to find a rational w ∈ S(K, ε)
with π T x ≤ π T w + ε for all x ∈ S(K, −ε), or to assert that S(K, −ε) is empty. By Grötschel et al.
[23, Corollary 4.2.7], for a convex body contained in the ball of radius R about the origin and given
by a weak separation oracle, weak optimization is solved in oracle-polynomial time in the dimension,
the encoding lengths of R, π and ε; by their Section 4.1 conventions, a polynomial-time oracle with
polynomial-length answers makes this ordinary polynomial bit time. The returned point need not
lie in K, and its objective is compared with S(K, −ε) only; the proof below repairs both.

Lemma C.2 (Evaluation of a certified convex patch). Let P ⊆ RN be a nonempty polytope given
by rational linear equations and inequalities, and let rational data of total encoding length Σ be given
as follows.

 (a) A point c ∈ P ; a matrix Z ∈ QN ×k , k ≥ 1, whose columns span the direction space of the
     affine hull of P ; and rationals r ∈ (0, 12 ], R ≥ 1 and ζ ≥ max{1, ∥Z∥2 } such that c + Zt ∈ P
     whenever ∥t∥2 ≤ r, and ∥t∥2 ≤ R whenever c + Zt ∈ P .

 (b) A function f , continuously differentiable on an open set containing P and convex on P ; a
     norm ν on RN equal to ∥·∥2 or ∥·∥∞ , with dual norm ν∗ ; and rationals V ≥ max{1, supP |f |}
     and G ≥ max{1, supP ν∗ (∇f )}.



                                                  128
  (c) An oracle that, for a rational y ∈ P and a rational β > 0, returns rationals l ≤ f (y) ≤ u with
      u − l ≤ β and a rational ĝ ∈ Qk with ĝ − Z T ∇f (y)     ≤ β, in time polynomial in Σ, the
                                                             ∞
      length of y, and log(1/β).

 (d) A repair map ϱ, computable in time polynomial in Σ and the length of its argument, that sends
     every w ∈ QN to a rational point of P with ν(ϱ(w) − ȳ) ≤ ∥w − ȳ∥2 for every ȳ ∈ P .

Then for every rational η ∈ (0, 1] one computes a rational y ∈ P and rationals a ≤ U with

                                      a ≤ min f ≤ f (y) ≤ U ≤ a + η
                                            P

in time polynomial in Σ and log(1/η). If moreover f is twice continuously differentiable and
v T ∇2 f (x)v ≥ g ∥v∥22 for all x ∈ P , all v in the p
                                                     column space of Z, and a rational g > 0, then f has
                         ∗                    ∗
a unique minimizer y on P , and ∥y − y ∥2 ≤ 2η/g.

Proof. Let P ′ = {t ∈ Rk : c + Zt ∈ P }, f˜(t) = f (c + Zt), and

                           K = {(t, θ) ∈ Rk+1 : t ∈ P ′ , f˜(t) ≤ θ ≤ V + 2}.

This is a compact convex set. It contains the ball of radius r about o = (0, V + 1): a point of this
ball has t ∈ P ′ and V + 12 ≤ θ ≤ V + 32 , while f˜(t) ≤ V . It lies in the ball of radius RK = R + 2V + 1
about o, because −V ≤ θ ≤ V + 2. Translating by o gives a circumscribed body in the sense of the
cited corollary.
    Weak separation. Let D1 = 2kR, so that ∥t′ − t∥1 ≤ D1 for t, t′ ∈ P ′ . Given a rational (t, θ)
and δ > 0: if c + Zt violates a defining constraint αT x ≤ α0 of P (equations being treated as two
inequalities), then Z T α ̸= 0, because αT (c + Zt) = αT c ≤ α0 would hold otherwise, and (Z T α, 0)
separates strictly; if θ > V + 2, the vector (0, 1) separates strictly. Otherwise call the oracle at
y = c + Zt with β = δ/(2D1 + 2). If θ < l − βD1 , then for every (t′ , θ′ ) ∈ K, convexity gives

             θ′ ≥ f˜(t′ ) ≥ f˜(t) + ∇f˜(t)T (t′ − t) ≥ l + ĝ T (t′ − t) − βD1 > θ + ĝ T (t′ − t),

because ∇f˜(t) = Z T ∇f (y); so (ĝ, −1) separates strictly. Otherwise f˜(t) − θ ≤ u − l + βD1 ≤ δ,
so either (t, θ) ∈ K or (t, f˜(t)) ∈ K lies within distance δ; in both cases (t, θ) ∈ S(K, δ). A strictly
separating vector, divided by its ∞-norm, is a valid answer for every δ. All answers are rational of
polynomial length.
      Weak optimization. Let Ξ = Gζ + 2 + (2V + 1)/r and ε = min{r/2, η/(2Ξ)}, and apply weak
optimization with objective (0, −1). Let t∗ minimize f˜ on P ′ , put z ∗ = (t∗ , f˜(t∗ )) and λ = ε/r ≤ 12 .
The point zλ = (1 − λ)z ∗ + λo lies in S(K, −ε): for ∥w∥ ≤ ε, zλ + w = (1 − λ)z ∗ + λ(o + w/λ)
with ∥w/λ∥ ≤ r, a convex combination of points of K. So S(K, −ε) ̸= ∅, and the algorithm returns
(to , θo ) ∈ S(K, ε) with
                                                                                       2V + 1 
             θo ≤ (zλ )θ + ε ≤ min f + λ(V + 1 − min f ) + ε ≤ min f + ε 1 +                     .
                                  P                     P               P                 r
Put a = θo − ε(1 + (2V + 1)/r), so a ≤ minP f .
    Repair. Some (t̄, θ̄) ∈ K satisfies (to , θo ) − (t̄, θ̄) ≤ ε. Let w = c + Zto , ȳ = c + Z t̄ ∈ P and
                                                             2
y = ϱ(w) ∈ P . Then ∥w − ȳ∥2 ≤ ζε and ν(y − ȳ) ≤ ζε. Along the segment from ȳ to y, which lies
in P , the mean value theorem gives

                         f (y) ≤ f (ȳ) + Gν(y − ȳ) ≤ θ̄ + Gζε ≤ θo + ε + Gζε.

                                                     129
Call the oracle at y with β = η/2 and let U = u. Then f (y) ≤ U ≤ f (y) + η/2 and

                                          η             2V + 1 
                                U −a≤       + ε Gζ + 2 +          ≤ η.
                                          2                r
All tolerances have polynomial encoding length, so the running time is polynomial.
    Distance. Under the curvature hypothesis, f is strictly convex on P , so y ∗ is unique. For
y ∈ P the function φ(θ) = f (y ∗ + θ(y − y ∗ )) on [0, 1] has φ′ (0) ≥ 0 by first-order optimality on
the convex set P , and φ′′ ≥ g ∥y − y ∗ ∥22 because y − y ∗ lies in the column space of Z. Hence
f (y) − f (y ∗ ) ≥ g2 ∥y − y ∗ ∥22 , and ∥y − y ∗ ∥22 ≤ 2(U − a)/g ≤ 2η/g.

    No inner-radius assumption on the location of the minimizer is used: the known inner ball is
centered at c, and boundary minimizers cause no loss. The tolerance ε is exponentially small when
r or V −1 is, but its encoding length is polynomial; the cost depends on log(1/r) and log V , not on
their magnitudes.
    The lemma concerns positive tangent dimension. A patch consisting of a single rational point is
handled before invoking it: evaluate a polynomial objective exactly, or use the proved value oracle
for an algebraic chart. In the latter case fixed retained coordinates still require refinement of the
dependent roots and the objective, as in appendix D.1.

Proof of proposition 5.18. (a) If S̄ = ∅ the point is rational and there is nothing to do. Otherwise
apply lemma C.2 with N = S̄ , the box P of the patch, f = Fγ (x̄S , ·), c its midpoint, Z = I, ζ = 1,

polynomial length. The oracle evaluates f and its gradient exactly at rational points, and the repair

    The expectation follows because the fallback output occurs with probability at most 1/(2B).

C.3    The quadratic case
Lemma C.3 (Face enumeration for quadratics). Let Fγ (x) = 12 xT Hx + (b + γ)T x + c0 be rational
on a mixed box X. For every integer assignment z and every partition IC = L ∪ U ∪ F, if HFF is
positive definite (or F = ∅), solve the linear system ∇F Fγ (x̃) = 0 for the point x̃ with integer part z,
values ℓL , uU on the fixed sets and unknowns on F, and keep x̃ if it lies in X. Some kept point is
a global minimizer of Fγ on X. The enumeration costs 3nc NZ poly(I + b) bit operations, where b
bounds the bit length of the coefficients of γ, and every kept point is rational of bit length poly(I + b).



                                                    130
Proof. Let z ∗ be the integer part of some global minimizer. Among the global minimizers with
integer part z ∗ choose x∗ whose set F of continuous coordinates strictly inside their bounds is
smallest. If F = ∅, x∗ is a kept point. Otherwise ∇F Fγ (x∗ ) = 0 and HFF ⪰ 0 by the first- and
second-order conditions in the open box. If HFF v = 0 for some nonzero v supported on F, then
Fγ (x∗ + θv) = Fγ (x∗ ) for all θ, because the gradient term and the quadratic term along v vanish;
moving θ until a coordinate of F reaches a bound gives a global minimizer with integer part z ∗ and
fewer interior coordinates, a contradiction. So HFF is positive definite and x∗ is the unique solution
of its system, hence kept. Every kept point is feasible, so a kept point of least value is a global
minimizer. There are NZ 3nc systems; each is a rational linear system whose solution has polynomial
bit length by Cramer’s rule and Hadamard’s inequality.

   The argument uses that a quadratic is constant along a null direction of its Hessian at a stationary
point. It does not extend to polynomials of higher degree, for which theorem 3.20 is used instead.

Proof of corollary 5.19. Run definition 5.17 with the factor B = 3nc NZ of lemma C.3 in the schedule
and with this enumeration as the fallback; the scalar-section count Ctail of lemma 5.14 is the one for
d = 2. All statements about pruning, counting, closure and the schedule are those of the case d = 2 of
theorem 5.2; κ3 may be replaced by 0 because the Hessian is constant. When the closure test returns
a point, it is rational. When it returns (S, x̄S , P, g0 ), the problem min{Fγ (x̄S , y) : y ∈ P } is a convex
quadratic program with a positive definite Hessian, whose unique solution is the global minimizer
by proposition 5.12; it is rational of polynomial bit length and is computed in polynomial time [30].
On fallback draws, lemma C.3 returns a rational global minimizer at cost B poly(I + log M ), with
probability at most 1/(2B) by lemma 5.16. The work bound is that of theorem 5.2.


D     Proofs for the feasible-domain extensions
Throughout, NB denotes the number of bags of the tree decomposition used by the algorithm, and
C0 an absolute constant that may change between occurrences.

D.1     Global graphs
Proof of lemma 6.3. Substitute only explicit rational constants and implicit coordinates with rational
singleton brackets. A constant implicit root need not be rational: the root of y 2 = 2 in [1, 2] has no
parents. Such coordinates remain in the chart and in the rational original-domain formula used
by the fallback. Their ancestor sets are empty and do not affect scope containment or running
intersection. In case (E), induction along the dependency order shows that ψj depends only on
Λ(yj ) and that |Λ(yj )| ≤ k δ for a coordinate at dependency depth δ ≤ Dg : Λ(yj ) is the union of
the sets Λ(π) over its at most k parents π. In case (I), ψj depends on xSj and |Sj | ≤ k. Hence
|β ′ | ≤ v∈β |Λ(v)| ≤ p max{1, k Dg }, respectively p max{1, k}.
        P

      Every retained coordinate lies in a bag of the original decomposition and hence in the corre-
sponding expanded bag. A reduced factor fa (x, ψ(x)) depends only on v∈Sa Λ(v), which lies in β ′
                                                                          S

for a bag β ⊇ Sa . The term ηj ψj depends on Λ(yj ), which lies in β ′ for every bag β containing yj .
      For running intersection, fix a retained coordinate xi and let Di be the set of dependent
coordinates y with xi ∈ Λ(y). The expanded bags containing xi are those of the original bags
that contain xi or a member of Di , that is, the union of the subtrees Tv of bags containing v, over
v ∈ {xi } ∪ Di . Each Tv is connected. In case (I), yj ∈ Di means i ∈ Sj , and the bag containing
Sj ∪ {yj } lies in Txi and in Tyj . In case (E), a member of Di is the end of a dependency path
starting at xi , consecutive elements of which are a parent and its child; the bag containing the

child’s constraint scope lies in both of their subtrees. In both cases every Ty , y ∈ Di , is connected
to Txi within the union, which is therefore connected.

candidate cell C of β, the rounded point Y satisfies G(Y ) ≥ H(Y ) ≥ q C , so q C − Ej ≤ G(x). The
dynamic-programming minimizer y (j) has G(y (j) ) ≤ mj + Dj ≤ mj + Ej , so Uj is an upper bound
for the value of a feasible point and Uj ≥ f ∗ ; rounding a global minimizer gives mj ≤ f ∗ + Ej
and Uj ≤ f ∗ + 2Ej . As in proposition 5.7, every minimizer is retained and every removed point
has value above Uj . A retained cell has q C ≤ Uj + Ej , attained at an allowed y with yβ a corner,
and G(y) ≤ H(y) + Dj ≤ f ∗ + 4Ej . Initialize U−1 = +∞, and store the point associated with the
smallest certified upper value: replace the stored point by y (j) when mj + Ej < Uj−1 , and otherwise
keep the earlier point. Its implicit lift is feasible and has value at most Uj ; its exact value is never
compared. Update this pair before pruning.

    The certified rows at level j need precision δj = nLh2j /(8NB ), of encoding length poly(I + j), so
the cost per row is polynomial by lemma 6.6. Rows reused from an earlier level are recomputed
at the new precision. With the witness tolerance 4Ej , the proof of proposition 5.9 applies to the
reduced objective conditionally on η, with interval lengths Lai + 8Ej /ai ≤ (1 + n)Lai ; the expected
number of retained cells of a bag is at most 2|β| i∈β [4 + (1 + n)Lwi /(2σ)].

Proof of theorem 6.4, case (I). Schedule. Let B be the factor of theorem 3.20 for the base instance
with domain formula consisting of the bounds and integer constraints of X, the bounds yj ∈ Vj ,

    Tails. Conditionally on η, the set X is compact and described by the formula above, F = F0 +η T y
has rational coefficients, and the reduced objective miny {F (x, y) : (x, y) ∈ X} is Gη (x), because the
fiber is the single point ψ(x). Hence lemma 3.17(a),(b) give P{g∗ < g0 | η} ≤ g0 W/σ+2nCtail /M ≤ ρ,
and lemma D.2 gives probability at most ρ for small active gradients. By lemma D.1, closure fails
at level J with probability at most 2ρ = 1/(2B), conditionally on every η.
    Correctness and work. Closure outputs are correct by lemmas 6.7 and D.1 and the strong
convexity of the reduced patch. Otherwise theorem 3.20 is applied to Fγ,η on X. The work of the
levels is bounded as in the proof of theorem 5.2, with the count above and the polynomial cost per
certified row; the fallback contributes poly(I) in expectation by lemma 3.18(a).
    Evaluation. First handle a point patch. Its retained coordinates are rational and fixed, but its
dependent coordinates can still be irrational. Use lemma 6.6 to enclose the sampled objective to
width 2−q and to refine each dependent root to width at most 2−q−1 / max{1, m}. Their exact roots

                                                    134
give the feasible lift; their rational midpoints give an ambient approximation within 2−q /2 of it.
This costs poly(I + q), with q + O(log(n + m + 1)) coordinate precision and the oracle’s polynomial
encoded allowance for objective evaluation. No positive-dimensional convex optimization is invoked.
    For a positive-dimensional ordinary patch apply lemma C.2 to the reduced objective on the
patch box, with Z = I, coordinatewise clipping as repair, ν = ∥·∥2 , chain-rule bounds V and
G, and the approximate oracle of lemma 6.6; its curvature hypothesis holds with g = g0 . With
ηGLS = min{2−q , g20 (2−q /(2Kψ ))2 } it returns a rational x̃ with ∥x̃ − x̂∥2 ≤ 2−q /(2Kψ ) and a value
interval of width at most 2−q . By (20) the exactly feasible lift (x̃, ψ(x̃)), given by x̃ and root brackets,
lies within 2−q /2 of the optimizer, and refining the brackets to total width 2−q /2 gives a rational
point within 2−q . On fallback draws, let G′ ≥ max{1, supXb ∥∇G∥1 } be a rational chain-rule bound,
refine each retained continuous coordinate to error at most 2−q /(4n max{1, Kψ , G′ }), clip it to its
original interval, and identify the integer labels exactly as in proposition 5.18(b). The retained
distance is at most 2−q /(4Kψ ) and the objective gap at most 2−q /4. Attach the exact chart roots,
refine their brackets to total width 2−q /2, and refine the optimal value to width 2−q /2. These give
both the feasible lift with a certified objective gap and the ambient rational approximation. All
extra precision is q + poly(I) and costs B poly(I + q) under theorem 3.20.

D.2     Affine-state recurrences
Proof of theorem 6.11. Feasible set. For every free point the recurrence defines one trajectory, and by
(22) and induction on k, sk ∈ Ik for all k, because uk ∈ hull Uk . Conversely every feasible trajectory
arises in this way. The identity (23) follows by substituting sN = aN −1 sN −1 + χN −1 (uN −1 ) into
(cN + ηN )sN , which turns the coefficient of sN −1 into cN −1 + ηN −1 + aN −1 λN = λN −1 , and repeating
down to λ1 s1 = λ1 (a0 s0 + χ0 (u0 )). Hence, with constants from substituted fixed coordinates carried
separately, the sampled objective of a trajectory equals Qη (z) + ξ T z at its free point.
     Substitute fixed free coordinates before computing widths or a schedule. If none remains, use
M = 2, draw the ambient coefficients once, and return the unique rational trajectory and its exact
sampled value. The remaining argument concerns a nonempty free box with positive coordinate
widths.
     Uniform data. By induction from |λN | ≤ Cs +σ and |λk | ≤ Cs +σ +a |λk+1 |, every |λk | ≤ Λ. The
added terms are unary, so the coordinate curvature, the Hessian row sums and the third-derivative
row sums of Qη exceed those of q by at most ΛG2 , ΛG2 and ΛG3 , which gives (24) and uniform
κ2 , κ3 . The adjoints are computed by N affine steps, so their bit lengths are sums of the lengths
of the ak , ck and ηk , polynomial in I + b. Producing the coefficients of Qη also costs polyd (I + b);
neither height nor production cost is asserted to be bounded by the literal I + b. The fallback of
lemma 6.1(b) is theorem 3.20 applied to the base instance with variables (s, u), domain formula
V
  k [sk+1 = ak sk + χk (uk )] together with the variable bounds and integer constraints, and objective
F0 tilted on all coordinates; its minimizers are the trajectories of the minimizers of Qη + ξ T z.
Lemma 6.1 with the free-variable decomposition gives (i) and (ii).
     Evaluation. For a rational free point, the states are rational: each step evaluates a fixed-degree
polynomial at its own control and adds an affine function of the previous state, so bit lengths add
over the horizon instead of being raised to powers, and the trajectory has length polynomial in I
and the length of the free point. It is exactly feasible, and integer labels are exact. For two free
points, |∆sk+1 | ≤ a |∆sk | + G1 |∆uk |, so the vector (|∆sk |)k≥1 is dominated by a lower-triangular
Toeplitz matrix with entries al applied to (|∆s0 | , G1 |∆u0 | , . . . ). Its row and column sums are at
most 1/(1 − a), so its spectral norm is at most 1/(1 − a), and the trajectory map has Lipschitz
constant at most Ktraj = 1 + (1 + G1 )/(1 − a). The free evaluation of proposition 5.18, with log2 Ktraj
extra bits, gives (iii); objective values and gaps transfer exactly by (23).

At levels with hj ≥ 1 the cell of a block is its original simplex and its corners and grid are the
original simplex vertices: 0, ei for an inequality block and ei for an equality block. At levels
with h = hj = 1/m < 1 the cells are the nonempty sets C = ∆b ∩ i∈b [hki , h(ki + 1)], with
                                                                             Q

ki ∈ {0, . . . , m − 1}, or the same with ∆=                                         b
                                           b ; their corners are their points in (hZ) . These bounded
indices exclude exterior cubes that would otherwise add duplicate boundary cells. Integer coordinates
use the cells of section 5.2. A bag cell is a product of block cells and integer cells.

Lemma D.3 (Feasible block rounding). Every block cell is the convex hull of its corners, which
are original simplex vertices when hj ≥ 1 and cube corners when hj < 1. For x ∈ Ωj and a cell
C0 ∈ Wj (β0 ) containing xβ0 , there is a random Y ∈ Aj with Yβ0 a corner of C0 , Yi = xi whenever
xi is a grid value, and EFγ (Y ) ≤ Fγ (x) + nLh2j /8.

Proof. For hj < 1, with zi = xi /h − ki , a cell becomes {z ∈ [0, 1]b : i zi ≤ ρ}, or the same with
                                                                           P

equality, where ρ = m − i ki is an integer. At a vertex, b linearly independent constraints are
                             P

tight. If the sum constraint is not among them, every coordinate is 0 or 1; if it is, at least b − 1
coordinates are 0 or 1, and the remaining one equals ρ minus an integer and lies in [0, 1]. So all
vertices are cube corners, and the bounded polytope is their convex hull. A whole simplex has the
vertices 0 and ei , or ei .
    For each block b, choose a cell Cb ∋ xb (the one of C0 if b ⊆ β0 ), write xb as a convex combination
of the corners of Cb , and let Yb equal each corner with its coefficient, independently over blocks;
round integer coordinates as in lemma 5.6. Then EYb = xb , and each coordinate of Yb takes the two
values hki , h(ki + 1), so its variance is at most h2 /4 (at most 1/4 ≤ h2 /4 while h ≥ 1). If xi = hki ,
then Yi ≥ hki with mean hki forces Yi = xi ; the same holds at the upper cube face. Hence Yb lies in
every level-j cell of b that contains xb : coordinates of xb strictly inside a grid interval have the same
interval in every such cell, coordinates on a grid value stay fixed, and Yb is feasible. As a grid point
of that cell it is one of its corners. Every bag cell containing xβ therefore contains Yβ as a corner,
so Y ∈ Aj . Rounding the blocks one after another, the segment from xb to Yb lies in Cb , so (25)
and Taylor’s theorem give E[Fγ | earlier blocks] ≤ Fγ + L2 E ∥Yb − xb ∥2 , and summing over blocks
and integer coordinates gives the allowance.

   With this lemma the proof of proposition 5.7 applies verbatim.

Lemma D.6 (Multiplier margins on simplices). Let D = max{1, d − 1} and

                                    K∆ = max{1, 2nc NZ 3nc (2D)nc }.

For τ > 0, the probability that g∗ > 0 and some active inequality at the unique minimizer has
multiplier at most τ is at most K∆ (τ /σ + 1/M ). Only zero-coordinate inequalities and tight budgets
of inequality blocks enter this event; equality-budget multipliers are excluded.

Proof. Fix integer labels z, a face of every block (support and tightness, at most 3|b| choices),
and one active inequality κ of the resulting product face Φ (at most 2nc choices). Let t ∈ Rk
be the tangent coordinates: the support coordinates of slack blocks and the support coordinates
other than the anchor ι (least support index) of tight blocks. On Φ the linear noise equals a
constant plus θT t, where θ consists of the γi of slack supports and the differences γi − γι of tight
supports. The stationarity system of Φ, ∇t [F0 (x(t))] + θ = 0, has at most Dk nonsingular zeros
by lemma C.1 and depends on the noise only through θ. For k = 0 there is one empty stationary
tuple, counted with weight D0 = 1, without applying the positive-dimensional zero lemma. At
a point of Φ the active-inequality multipliers are λb = −∂ι F0 − γι for a tight inequality budget,


                                                   138
λi = ∂i F0 − ∂ι F0 + γi − γι for a zero coordinate of a tight block, and λi = ∂i F0 + γi for a zero
coordinate of a slack block. Thus λκ is affine with slope ±1 in its normal coefficient νκ , namely γι
or γi , once θ and the other coefficients are fixed.
    The map from γ to (θ, ν), where ν lists the original coefficients not differenced, is injective. Its
transformed coordinates are not independent under the grid law, so we count tuples. A difference of
two grid values takes at most 2M − 1 values. For each of the at most (2M − 1)k M n−k−1 values
of the transformed coordinates other than νκ , the zeros are fixed, and for each zero the condition
|λκ | ≤ τ allows at most τ (M − 1)/σ + 1 grid values of νκ . Dividing by M n , the probability that the
event occurs at some nonsingular zero is at most 2k Dk (τ /σ + 1/M ).
    If g∗ > 0, let a be the minimizer and Φ its smallest face. Its tangent coordinates are a zero of
the system, nonsingular because two-sided growth along tangent directions gives a positive definite
tangent Hessian. The event is therefore contained in the union over NZ 3nc 2nc choices of the events
above, with k ≤ nc .

Proof of theorem 6.15. Schedule. Let B be the factor of theorem 3.20 for the product of simplices
and integer intervals, and Ctail the constant of lemma 3.17 for its linear description and label
disjunctions. Put
                            1              ρσ             ρσ                 nL

                           4B              2W            2K∆                 g0

let J be the least level with hJ ≤ min{1/(4A), τ /(8κ2 A), g0 /(8κ3 A)}, and let M be the least power

and wi for integers, so lemma 3.17(b) gives P{g∗ < g0 } ≤ ρ, and lemma D.6 gives probability at

sound on every draw; otherwise the fallback runs on the same draw.
    Work. The cells have at most 2|b| corners, children and incident cells per block; lemma D.4
and the argument of theorem 5.2 give the expected work, and the fallback contributes polyd (I) by
lemma 3.18(a).
    Evaluation. If the tangent dimension is zero, return the rational point and its exactly evaluated
objective; no convex-body oracle is used. Otherwise apply lemma C.2√        to the patch P in the free

r = min{ 21 , mins σs /(k ∥αs ∥1 )}, where αsT x ≤ α0,s runs over the bounds and inequality budgets of
P and σs > 0 is its slack at cP : every coordinate of Zt is a coordinate of t or minus a sum of them,
so αsT Zt ≤ k ∥αs ∥1 ∥t∥2 . The repair map is the Euclidean projection onto P , computed blockwise:
on a block with bounds l ≤ x ≤ u and budget β ≤               x ≤ β (with β = β for equality budgets

and β = −∞ otherwise), the projection of w is xi = min{ui , max{li , wi − θ}}, with θ = 0 if this
                                                       P

is continuous, nonincreasing and piecewise linear in θ with breakpoints wi − ui and wi − li ; sorting
them and solving one linear equation gives a rational θ in polynomial time. Projection onto a convex

the feasible objective gap. On fallback draws, let G′ ≥ max{1, sup ∥∇Fγ ∥2 } bound the gradient on

gives physical distance at most 2−q−1 /G′ and objective gap at most 2−q−1 . A value enclosure of
width 2−q−1 then completes the certified interval. These refinements cost B polyd (I + q) and have
expectation polyd (I + q).


                                                  139
D.4     Order constraints
Write x = (xC , xZ ) for continuous and binary coordinates. The slice for a binary assignment b is
Pb = {xC : (xC , b) ∈ X}, an order polytope on IC intersected with the bounds xi = 0 for edges (i, l)
with bl = 0 and xi = 1 for edges (l, i) with bl = 1.

Lemma D.7 (Zero-one vertices). Every vertex of Pb , and of every face of it, is a zero-one vector.

Proof. Let x be a point of Pb with a value t ∈ (0, 1), and let G be the set of coordinates with value
t. Moving all coordinates of G by ±ϵ keeps every edge inside G tight, keeps every edge between G
and other coordinates strict for small ϵ, and keeps the bounds; so x is the midpoint of two feasible
points and not a vertex. A face is a polytope whose vertices are vertices of Pb .

Rounding for theorem 6.17. For fixed U ∈ [0, 1] the scalar map t 7→ h(⌊t/h⌋ + 1{U < frac(t/h)}) is
nondecreasing, fixes the grid values, including 0 and 1, and maps each grid interval onto its endpoints.
Applied to all continuous coordinates of x ∈ Ωj , it therefore preserves every order constraint and
every binary value, keeps every coordinate strictly inside a grid interval within that interval and every
grid coordinate fixed, and so keeps every bag tuple a corner of every candidate cell containing it. For
U uniform, every coordinate is mean preserving with variance at most h2 /4. The segment from x to
Y lies in [0, 1]n , so the full Hessian bound gives EFγ (Y ) ≤ Fγ (x) + L̄2 E ∥Y − x∥2 ≤ Fγ (x) + nc L̄h2 /8.
Every edge lies in a bag and rows violating an assigned edge are removed, so allowed grid points are
feasible. The proof of proposition 5.7 then applies with Ej = nc L̄h2j /8.

   Fix a bag β, write βC = β ∩ IC with cβ = |βC | and βZ = β ∩ IZ , and let O = [n] \ β. For a
binary row b ∈ {0, 1}βZ and v ∈ [0, 1]βC , put

                        Vb (v) = γβTZ b + min F0 (v, b, z) + γO
                                                              T
                                              
                                                                z : (v, b, z) ∈ X ,

with min ∅ = +∞; it depends on γO and γβZ only. A standard order simplex is Sπ = {v : 0 ≤
vπ(1) ≤ · · · ≤ vπ(cβ ) ≤ 1} for a permutation π of βC . Its vertices form a chain of zero-one vectors,
and a face with vertices a0 < · · · < ak in this chain has edge directions dl = al − al−1 , l = 1, . . . , k,
with disjoint nonempty supports.

Lemma D.8 (Endpoint-preserving transport). Let v lie in the relative interior of a face Φ of Sπ ,
with Vb (v) < ∞. There is an affine map w 7→ x(w) on the affine hull of Φ such that, for every
w ∈ Φ, x(w) ∈ X has continuous bag part w and binary bag part b; x(v) attains Vb (v); and along
every edge direction dl of Φ each coordinate of x changes at a rate in [0, 1], binary coordinates at
rate 0. Consequently, whenever v ± θdl ∈ Φ,

                              Vb (v + θdl ) + Vb (v − θdl ) − 2Vb (v) ≤ nc L̄θ2 .

Proof. On the relative interior of Φ, coordinates of βC are partitioned into groups of equal value: a
group at 0, a group at 1 (either may be empty), and k interior groups with values 0 < r1 < · · · <
rk < 1; this partition is the same at every relative-interior point, and on the closed face the groups
stay equal and ordered, 0 ≤ s1 (w) ≤ · · · ≤ sk (w) ≤ 1, where sl (w) is the common value of group l
at w, a linear function of w. Moving along dl changes exactly one group value at unit rate: writing
    P                                                                       P
v = l λl al with positive weights, the value of the lth interior group is i≥l λi , and dl moves weight
from λl−1 to λl .
    Let x⋆ = (v, b, z ⋆ ) attain Vb (v); it exists by compactness. Put r0 = 0, rk+1 = 1, s0 = 0, sk+1 = 1,
and let Tw be the piecewise-affine map of [0, 1] with Tw (rl ) = sl (w), affine between consecutive
knots. Define x(w) by applying Tw to every coordinate of x⋆ . For w ∈ Φ, Tw is nondecreasing with

                                                     140
Tw (0) = 0 and Tw (1) = 1, so x(w) satisfies every order constraint, keeps every binary value, lies
in [0, 1]n , and maps every bag coordinate of group l to sl (w) = wi ; thus x(w) ∈ X with bag parts
(w, b), and x(v) = x⋆ . A coordinate of x⋆ in [rl , rl+1 ] equals (1 − ϑ)rl + ϑrl+1 for a fixed ϑ ∈ [0, 1]
and is mapped to (1 − ϑ)sl (w) + ϑsl+1 (w), which is affine in w; along dl its rate is 1 − ϑ, ϑ, or 0.
Binary coordinates sit at the fixed knots 0 and 1.
     The function φ(w) = γβTZ b + F0 (x(w)) + γO   T x(w) satisfies φ(v) = V (v) and φ ≥ V on Φ. The

   The transported points need not exhaust the fiber over w: at endpoint faces new binary labels
can become feasible, and the projection of the mixed domain onto the continuous bag coordinates
need not be convex. Neither matters, because only the upper support at v ± θdl is used.

Lemma D.10 (Linear-programming exposure). Suppose every global minimizer has binary part
b, let QC be a box containing their continuous parts, c its midpoint, r its largest half-width,
g = ∇C Fγ (c, b) and δ = κ2 r, where κ2 bounds the continuous Hessian row sums on [0, 1]n . For
a proposed equality e (an edge xi = xl with opposite face Pe− = Pb ∩ {xi = 0, xl = 1}, or a bound
xi = 0 or xi = 1 with opposite face Pb ∩ {xi = 1} or Pb ∩ {xi = 0}) with nonempty opposite face,
put ∆e (v) = minPe− v T x − minPb v T x. If ∆e (g) > nc δ, the equality holds at every global minimizer.
For all v, w, |∆e (v) − ∆e (w)| ≤ nc ∥v − w∥∞ . If the opposite face is empty, the equality holds on
all of Pb .

                                                            141
Proof. Let x∗ = (a, b) be a global minimizer and h = ∇C Fγ (a, b). Since a minimizes Fγ (·, b) on
the convex set Pb , hT (x − a) ≥ 0 there, so a lies in the face Ψ = arg minPb hT x, whose vertices are
zero-one by lemma D.7. If an edge equality fails at a, then ai < al , and not all vertices of Ψ have
equal ith and lth coordinates; a vertex v with vi ̸= vl has vi = 0 and vl = 1, so v ∈ Pe− . For a bound,
a vertex of Ψ with the opposite value exists likewise. Let w be a zero-one minimizer of g T x on Pb .
Since hT v ≤ hT w, ∥v − w∥1 ≤ nc , and ∥g − h∥∞ ≤ κ2 ∥c − a∥∞ ≤ δ,

                      ∆e (g) ≤ g T (v − w) = hT (v − w) + (g − h)T (v − w) ≤ nc δ,

a contradiction. For the Lipschitz bound, let x1 be a zero-one minimizer of wT x on Pe− and x2 one
of v T x on Pb ; then ∆e (v) − ∆e (w) ≤ (v − w)T (x1 − x2 ) ≤ nc ∥v − w∥∞ , and symmetrically. If Pe− is
empty, no vertex of Pb violates the equality, so it holds on the convex hull of the vertices.

The closure test. After retention at level j ≥ 1: form the hulls; fail unless every binary hull is a
singleton, which fixes b; apply lemma D.10 to every continuous edge and every continuous bound,
using rational linear programs; merge coordinates joined by certified edge equalities into blocks,
fix blocks with certified bounds or bounds forced by b, intersect the hull intervals of the members,
contract directed cycles, and propagate bounds, replacing each lower bound by the largest lower
bound of its predecessors and each upper bound by the smallest upper bound of its successors; fix
blocks whose interval is a single point and repeat until nothing changes; remove tautological rows.
This yields xC = Dy + x̄C with disjoint zero-one columns, a box [L, U] in y with L < U, and an
acyclic set of remaining order inequalities yu ≤ yu′ . If no block remains, return the point. Otherwise,
with cy the midpoint and ry the largest half-width of the box, test

                         DT ∇2CC Fγ (Dcy + x̄C , b)D − (κ3 ry + g0 )DT D ≻ 0,                      (47)

where κ3 bounds the continuous third-derivative row sums on [0, 1]n .

Lemma D.11 (Order closure). The patch Py = {y ∈ [L, U] : yu ≤ yu′ for the remaining inequalities}
contains the block values of every global minimizer and is full-dimensional. If (47) holds, then
∇2y Fγ (Dy + x̄C , b) ⪰ g0 DT D ⪰ g0 I on Py , and the unique minimizer on Py is the unique global
minimizer. Conversely, if g∗ ≥ g0 , every active proposal at the minimizer with a nonempty opposite
face has exposure gap larger than τ , A = 2+nc L̄/g0 , and hj ≤ min{1/(4A), τ /(4nc κ2 A), g0 /(4κ3 A)},
then the test of level j succeeds.

Proof. Containment. Binary singleton hulls fix b at every minimizer, the certified equalities hold by
lemma D.10, and merging, cycle contraction, bound propagation and singleton fixing are equivalent
reformulations of Pb ∩ QC with those equalities.
    Full dimension. After propagation the bounds are monotone along the remaining inequalities:
Lu ≤ Lu′ and Uu ≤ Uu′ for u → u′ . For an inequality u → u′ , the point that equals U on the blocks
reachable from u′ and L elsewhere is feasible, and u is not reachable from u′ because the remaining
graph is acyclic: an inequality from an unreachable block w to a reachable w′ has Lw ≤ Lw′ ≤ Uw′ ,
and none goes from a reachable to an unreachable block. At this point yu = Lu and yu′ = Uu′ . If
Lu = Uu′ , then Lu ≤ Lu′ ≤ Uu′ and Lu ≤ Uu ≤ Uu′ force singleton intervals, which were removed.
So no inequality and, by the points L and U, no bound is tight on all of Py , and Py has nonempty
interior.
    Certificate. For y, y ′ in the box, ∥D(y − y ′ )∥∞ = ∥y − y ′ ∥∞ and DT (∇2 Fγ (x) − ∇2 Fγ (x′ ))D ⪰
−κ3 ∥x − x′ ∥∞ DT D, so (47) gives the claimed bound, and DT D ⪰ I because every block has at
least one coordinate.


                                                  142
                                        q
                                    h
    Success. Witnesses lie within 2j nc L̄/g0 of the minimizer x∗ = (a, b∗ ), so hulls lie within Ahj
of it, and binary hulls are the singletons b∗ because Ahj ≤ 1/4 and binary cells are singletons at
levels j ≥ 1. By lemma D.10, ∆e (g) ≥ ∆e (h) − nc κ2 r > τ − τ /4 > nc δ for every active proposal with
a finite gap, so all of them are certified. Proposals with an empty opposite face hold on all of Pb .
Hence every point of Py satisfies every constraint that is tight at a, the patch lies in the smallest face
of a in Pb , and the columns of D times directions of Py are directions of that face. Two-sided growth
along them gives DT ∇2 Fγ (x∗ )D ⪰ 2g0 DT D. With ∥Dcy + x̄C − a∥∞ ≤ ry ≤ Ahj , the matrix in
(47) is at least (g0 − 2κ3 Ahj )DT D ⪰ g20 DT D.

    This weighted test measures curvature in the metric of the original coordinates. An unweighted
version that subtracts (nc κ3 ry + g0 )I is also sound, but the two must not be mixed by dropping the
factor DT D from one and the factor nc from the other.

Lemma D.12 (Exposure gaps). Let D = max{1, d−1} and K⪯ = max{1, 2nz (m+2nc )2m+2nc Dnc }.
For τ > 0, the probability that g∗ > 0 and some proposal that is active at the minimizer and has a
nonempty opposite face has exposure gap ∆e (∇C Fγ (x∗ )) ≤ τ is at most K⪯ (τ /σ + 2/M ).

Proof. Fix a binary assignment b, a face Φ of Pb (determined by a subset of its at most m + 2nc
inequalities), and a proposal e that holds on Φ. Write the affine hull of Φ as x = xΦ + Bζ with
B of full column rank. Its stationarity system B T (∇C F0 (xΦ + Bζ, b) + γC ) = 0 has at most Dnc
nonsingular zeros by lemma C.1. If Φ has dimension zero there is one empty stationary tuple,
counted with weight one.
    For an edge e = (i, l), condition on all coefficients except γi , γl , and write γi = t, γl = s − t.
Every direction of Φ has equal ith and lth entries, so B T (ei − el ) = 0 and the system depends on s
but not on t. Fix s and a zero, with point xρ in the relative interior of Φ. Whenever (xρ , b) is a
global minimizer with smallest face Φ, the slice gradient is h(t) = h(0) + t(ei − el ), every vertex w of
Φ minimizes h(t)T x on Pb (the minimizing face contains xρ and hence Φ), and every point of Pe− has
xi = 0, xl = 1, while wi = wl . So ∆e (h(t)) = βρ − t with βρ independent of t, and the event ∆e ≤ τ
confines t to an interval of length τ , since gaps are nonnegative. For each of the at most 2M − 1
values of s, the admissible values of t are spaced by 2σ/(M − 1), so at most τ (M − 1)/(2σ) + 1 of
them lie in the interval of each zero of that fiber. Summing over the fibers and dividing by M 2 , the
probability for one face and edge is at most Dnc (2M − 1)[τ (M − 1)/(2σ) + 1]/M 2 ≤ Dnc (τ /σ + 2/M ).
A tuple-count on the fibers of s is used here because the conditional law of t given s is not uniformly
spread. For a bound xi = 0, vary γi alone: B T ei = 0, and ∆e = βρ + γi ; the upper bound is
symmetric; the probability is at most τ /(2σ) + 1/M .
    If g∗ > 0, the minimizer (a, b) has a smallest face Φ in Pb , its zero is nonsingular because
two-sided growth gives B T ∇2CC Fγ B ⪰ 2g∗ B T B ≻ 0, and the active proposals are those holding
on Φ. The union over at most 2nz · 2m+2nc · (m + 2nc ) choices gives the bound. Edges between a
continuous and a binary coordinate are bounds of the slice and are covered; binary coefficients are
fixed throughout.

Proof of theorem 6.17. Schedule. Let B be the factor of theorem 3.20 for the order polytope with
binary constraints, Ctail the constant of lemma 3.17 for its description, ρ = 1/(4B), g0 = ρσ/(2n),
τ = ρσ/(2K⪯ ), A = 2 + nc L̄/g0 , J ≥ 1 the least level satisfying the condition of lemma D.11, and
M the least power of two with M ≥ max{2, 2J , 4nCtail /ρ, 4K⪯ /ρ}. All widths are at most one, so
lemma 3.17(b) gives P{g∗ < g0 } ≤ ρ, and lemma D.12 gives probability at most K⪯ τ /σ+2K⪯ /M ≤ ρ
for small gaps. Outside these events closure succeeds by level J; it is sound on every draw; otherwise
the fallback runs on the same draw.



                                                   143
    Work. Binary cells are {0, 1} at level 0 and singletons afterwards, so cells have at most 2|β|
children and corners and a grid tuple lies in at most 2|β| cells. Proposition D.9 with M hj ≥ 1 for
j ≤ J, and the argument of theorem 5.2, give (26); the linear programs and matrix tests of a level
cost polyd (I), and the fallback contributes polyd (I) in expectation.
    Evaluation. If no block remains, evaluate the returned rational point and its sampled value exactly.
Otherwise apply lemma C.2 in the block coordinates y, with Z = I and ζ = 1. A rational center
and inner radius come from the linear program max{ϑ : αsT y + ϑ ∥αs ∥1 ≤ α0,s for all rows, ϑ ≤ 12 }
over the rows of Py : its optimal vertex (yc , ϑ∗ ) is rational of polynomial length, and ϑ∗ > 0
by full dimensionality; the ball of radius ϑ∗ about yc lies in Py since αsT u ≤ ∥αs ∥1 ∥u∥2 . Take
R = max{1, dim y}. The repair map clips to [L, U] and then replaces every coordinate by the
largest clipped value over its predecessors, itself included. The result is feasible, because propagated
upper bounds are monotone along the inequalities. If ȳ ∈ Py and ∥z − ȳ∥∞ = ϵ, clipping keeps
every coordinate within ϵ of ȳ, the repaired value is at least the clipped value and hence at
least ȳu − ϵ, and it equals a clipped predecessor value at most ȳw + ϵ ≤ ȳu + ϵ; so the repair is
nonexpansive in ∥·∥∞ , and we take ν = ∥·∥∞ with a monomial bound on DT ∇C Fγ ≤ ∥∇C Fγ ∥1 .
                                                                                      √    1
The curvature hypothesis holds with g = g0 by lemma D.11. Since ∥D∥2 ≤ nc , the objective
tolerance min{2−q , (g0 /(2nc ))2−2q } gives distance 2−q in the original coordinates. On fallback draws,
identify the binary labels by refining to error below 12 , propagate their bounds through the order
graph, clip the continuous approximations to the propagated intervals, and take predecessor maxima;
this keeps the labels and every order constraint and does not increase the ∞-norm error. Choose
each continuous coordinate’s error at most 2−q−1 /(nG′ ), where G′ ≥ max{1, sup[0,1]n ∥∇C Fγ ∥1 }.
Then the physical Euclidean distance is at most 2−q−1 and the feasible objective gap at most 2−q−1 .
Refining the optimal value to width 2−q−1 supplies the certified value interval, in B polyd (I + q) bit
work. Without propagation, a slightly positive approximation of a continuous predecessor could
move a binary zero.


E     Proofs for conditional recourse
This appendix proves the results of section 7. Appendix E.1 lists the external tools. Appendices E.2
to E.4 prove the common machinery, appendix E.5 proves the ambient-noise theorems, appendix E.6
proves the core-only theorem, and appendix E.7 verifies the examples.

E.1    Conventions and tools
Norms are Euclidean unless marked otherwise. For a symmetric matrix the operator norm is at
most the largest absolute row sum. Hence, with K2 , K3 as in (34), every principal submatrix and
every off-diagonal block of ∇2 F0 (x) has operator norm at most K2 , and

          ∇2 F0 (x) − ∇2 F0 (y) ≤ K3 ∥x − y∥∞ ,            D3 F0 (x)[p, p′ , p′′ ] ≤ K3 ∥p∥ p′   p′′      (48)

for x, y ∈ [0, 1]n and vectors p, p′ , p′′ . For the first bound, the (i, j) entry of the difference is at most
  l sup |∂P
          ijl F0 | |xl − yl |, so every row sum is at most K3 ∥x − y∥∞ . For the second, the symmetric
P

matrix ( l ∂ijl F0 p′′l )ij has row sums at most K3 ∥p′′ ∥∞ ≤ K3 ∥p′′ ∥. The same row-sum argument
gives |∂i F0 (x) − ∂i F0 (y)| ≤ K2 ∥x − y∥∞ . Monomial coefficient sums give rational K2 , K3 , G of
polynomial bit length for fixed d.
    We use the following results. Items (T2)–(T5) are proved in section 3 and its appendix; we
restate the forms used.


                                                     144
(T1) Corrected corners and local counts (lemma 3.7 and theorem 3.4). If f has upper coordinate
     curvature at most L on a box and C is a cell with side lengths ηi , then mincorners Fc ≤
     Fc (x) + 18 i Lηi2 for every x ∈ C and every c. For the uniform grid with mi parts of
                P

     width hi per coordinate and independent coefficients with interval concentrations πi , the
     expected number of nodes v with Fc (v) ≤ Fc (v ± hi ei ) + δ for all interior coordinates is at
     most i [2 + (mi − 1) min{1, πi (Lhi + 2δ/hi )}], also conditionally on any random element
            Q

     independent of c on which f may depend.

(T2) Growth tail (theorem 3.16). If Z ⊂ Rp is compact with coordinate widths wi , f : Z → R is
     lower semicontinuous, and c is uniform on [−σ, σ]p , then the point-growth modulus of f + cT y
     satisfies Pr{g∗ < ε} ≤ ε i wi /σ.
                             P


(T3) Finite-law tails (lemma 3.17). Let D ⊂ Rp+m be compact and described by at most s
     polynomial sign conditions of degree at most d (bounded integer coordinates by disjunctions),
     let F have degree at most d, and perturb only the first p coordinates. For the reduced objective
     y 7→ min{F (y, y ′ ) : (y, y ′ ) ∈ D} on the projection of D, with coefficients independent with
     law Uσ,M , Pr{g∗ < ε} ≤ Sε/σ + 2pCtail /M , where S is the sum of the projected widths and
     Ctail is base-computable with log Ctail ≤ polyd (I) when log(s + 1) ≤ poly(I). If m = 0 and
     D = [0, 1]n , the probability that g∗ > 0 and the minimizer has a coordinate at a bound with
     |∂i Fγ | ≤ τ is at most Nact (τ /σ + 1/M ), Nact = n3n max{1, d − 1}n .

(T4) Exact fallback (theorem 3.20). For a fixed-degree polynomial on an explicit compact mixed
     semialgebraic domain with base length I, there are a base-computable B = 2polyd (I) and a fixed
     exponent such that, for all rational linear coefficients of bit length at most b, a deterministic
     algorithm returns, within B polyd (I + b) bit operations, an algebraic output (definition 2.5(d))
     of the lexicographically least global minimizer and of the optimal value: one isolated real root
     and rational polynomial maps for every continuous coordinate and for the value, with integer
     coordinates listed. Its length is at most B polyd (I + b). Enclosing each coordinate to width
     2−q−⌈log2 (n+1)⌉ and the value to width 2−q gives the Euclidean point and value evaluation
     contract in B polyd (I + b + q) work.

(T5) Rare fallback (lemma 3.18(a)). If the fallback costs at most B P (Lmax ) on every draw and
     runs with probability at most 1/B, it contributes at most P (Lmax ) to the expected work. The
     parameters are chosen in the order B, thresholds, stopping depth J, grid size M .

(T6) Fixed-block quantifier elimination [36, Theorem 1.1]. A formula with ω quantifier blocks of
     positive sizes n1 , . . . , nω , ℓ ≥ 1 free variables, s ≥ 1 atoms, and degree at most d0 ≥ 1, with
     arbitrary real coefficients, is equivalent to a quantifier-free formula in disjunctive normal form
     whose numbers Q   of disjuncts and of atoms per disjunct, and whose atom degrees, are at most
                  2cω ℓ b nb

                                                     145
 (T8) Convex values and patch evaluation (lemma C.2, based on weak convex optimization [23,
      Corollary 4.2.7]). For a convex polynomial f of fixed degree on a rational box P with nonempty
      interior relative to its free coordinates, and a rational η ∈ (0, 1], one computes a rational y ∈ P
      and rationals a ≤ minP f ≤ f (y) ≤ U ≤ a + η in time polynomial in the length of the data
      and log(1/η); here the oracle of that lemma is exact rational evaluation of f and ∇f , and the
      repair map is coordinatewise clipping to P , which is nonexpansive.         If ∇2 f ⪰ gI on P with
      rational g > 0, the unique minimizer y ∗ satisfies ∥y − y ∗ ∥ ≤ 2η/g.

 (T9) Nonsingular zeros (lemma C.1). A system of q polynomial equations of degree at most D in q
      complex variables has at most Dq zeros with nonsingular Jacobian.

(T10) Quadratic faces (lemma C.3). For a rational quadratic on a mixed box with nc continuous
      coordinates and NZ integer labels, solving the stationary system of every face whose free
      Hessian block is positive definite yields a rational global minimizer, in 3nc NZ poly(I + b) bit
      operations.

 E.2    Conditional values and the core search
 Proof of lemma 7.2. (a) The term γCT v does not depend on z, so V (v)−γCT v = minz∈Y [F0 (v, z)+γR   T z].
                                                                                         k
      (b) Fix z ∈ Y . Along the segment t 7→ (v + tei , z), which stays in [0, 1] × conv Y , the
 polynomial ϕz (t) = Fγ (v + tei , z) has ϕ′′z = ∂ii F0 ≤ L, so ϕz (t) − Lt2 /2 is concave. The function
 V (v + tei ) − Lt2 /2 = minz∈Y [ϕz (t) − Lt2 /2] is a pointwise minimum of concave functions over an
 index set that does not depend on t, hence concave.
     (c) Let z ∈ Y ′ attain VY ′ (w); it exists because Y ′ is compact. The segment from (w, z) to (v, z)
 lies in [0, 1]k × conv Y , so the mean value theorem and Hölder’s inequality give VY ′ (v) ≤ Fγ (v, z) ≤

      By lemma 7.2(a),(b), the function V̄ = V − γCT v has upper coordinate curvature at most L on
 [0, 1]k and does not depend on γC . Applied to f = V̄ and c = γC , the first part of (T1) gives (30):
 every point v of a level-j cell has a corner c with V (c) ≤ V (v) + Ej .

 Proof of proposition 7.5. Write ℓ(v), u(v) for the oracle’s answers at a queried corner, with ℓ = u = V
 in exact mode. With ηj from (31), every answer at level j satisfies ℓ(v) ≤ V (v) ≤ u(v) ≤ ℓ(v) + ηj ,
 and εj = Ej + ηj . Level 0 generates the cell [0, 1]k , queries its corners, and applies (32); level j ≥ 1
 generates the children of the cells retained at level j − 1. For k = 0 the only cell is the empty core
 point, Ej = 0, and the arguments below apply verbatim with the single corner.
     (a) Let (a, z ∗ ) be a global minimizer; then V (a) = f ∗ . Suppose a cell generated at level j
 contains a; at level 0 this is [0, 1]k . By (30) some corner c has V (c) ≤ f ∗ + Ej , so mincorners ℓ − Ej ≤
 V (c) − Ej ≤ f ∗ . Every upper value is the value of a feasible point, so Uj ≥ f ∗ , and the cell is
 retained. A child of it contains a and is generated at level j + 1. Induction proves (a).
     (b) With c as in (a), Uj ≤ u(c) ≤ V (c) + ηj ≤ f ∗ + εj . The cell that generated the corner cj
 satisfies mincorners ℓ − Ej ≤ ℓ(cj ) ≤ u(cj ) = Uj , so it is retained.
     (c) A retained cell has a corner v with ℓ(v)−Ej ≤ Uj , so V (v) ≤ ℓ(v)+ηj ≤ Uj +Ej +ηj ≤ f ∗ +2εj .
     Count. For k = 0 the count is one. Let k ≥ 1, so that εj = Ej in exact mode and εj = 2Ej in
 certified mode. Fix j ≤ J and h = hj , and let Nj be the number of points v of the deterministic
 grid (hZ)k ∩ [0, 1]k with V (v) ≤ f ∗ + 2εj . Such a point satisfies V (v) ≤ V (v ± hei ) + 2εj for every
 coordinate with 0 < vi < 1, because its grid neighbors are feasible. Apply the second part of (T1)
 to f = V̄ , c = γC , mi = 2j , hi = h, and δ = 2εj , conditionally on γR , which is independent of γC .



                                                    146
The relevant interval length is Lh + 4εj /h, which equals λh with λ = L(1 + k/2) in exact mode and
λ = L(1 + k) in certified mode, and (2) gives πi (λh) ≤ λh/(2σ) + 1/M . Since h−1 = 2j ≤ 2J ≤ M ,
                                      k h                    λh       1 i h    λ ik

                                      i=1

which is Qex or Qap .
    Oracle calls. By (c), each retained cell at level j has a corner counted by Nj , and a grid point
is a corner of at most 2k cells of level j; so at most 2k Nj cells are retained. Level j ≥ 1 generates
at most 2k · 2k Nj−1 cells and queries at most 8k Nj−1 corners; level 0 queries 2k corners. Taking
expectations gives at most 2k + 8k JQ calls through level J.

    The count compares the adaptive search with a deterministic grid fixed before sampling: retained
cells are charged to grid points, and the event that a grid point is near-optimal is stated through the
true function V . The oracle’s answers may depend on all noise coefficients; they enter only through
(a)–(c), which hold for every admissible answer.

E.3    Containment and closure
Proof of lemma 7.6. Cover. If z ∈ Y \ P , some coordinate violates its patch interval. If zi <
max{ℓi , zc,i − r}, then zi < zc,i − r because zi ≥ ℓi ; hence ℓi ≤ zi < zc,i − r, the lower slab Yi− is
formed, and it contains z. The upper case is symmetric.
   Containment. Let v ∈ H and let z minimize Fγ (v, ·) on Y . If z ∈   / P , then z lies in some slab S.
Since c, v ∈ H, ∥v − c∥∞ ≤ w∞ (H). By lemma 7.2(c) applied to S and to Y ,

                  V (v) = Fγ (v, z) ≥ VS (v) ≥ VS (c) − Gw∞ (H) ≥ λout − Gw∞ (H)
                                      > U + Gw∞ (H)
                                      ≥ V (c) + Gw∞ (H) ≥ V (v),

a contradiction. If (a, z ∗ ) is a global minimizer, then z ∗ minimizes Fγ (a, ·) on Y ; when a ∈ H it
follows that z ∗ ∈ P .

Proof of lemma 7.7. (a) Let x∗ be a global minimizer; it lies in Q. If ∂i Fγ > 0 on Q, the original
lower bound ℓi lies in the ith interval Qi , and x∗i > ℓi , then the segment from x∗ to x∗ − (x∗i − ℓi )ei
lies in Q ⊆ X and Fγ strictly decreases along it, which is impossible. The midpoint test is sufficient
because |∂i Fγ (x) − ∂i Fγ (m)| ≤ K2 rQ on Q (appendix E.1). For a quadratic, ∂i Fγ is affine and its
extreme values on Q are attained at vertices chosen coordinatewise. Each fixing is justified with
respect to the same box Q, so all of them hold simultaneously for every global minimizer.
    (b) Let U be the set of unfixed coordinates. For x ∈ Q′ , (48) gives ∇2U U F0 (x) ⪰ ∇2U U F0 (m′ ) −
K3 rQ′ I ⪰ g0 I. Thus Fγ is g0 -strongly convex on the box Q′ and has a unique minimizer there. By
(a), Q′ ⊆ X contains every global minimizer, so minQ′ Fγ = f ∗ , and the unique minimizer on Q′ is
the unique global minimizer.
    (c) As in (b), minQ′ Fγ = f ∗ , and the restriction is a convex quadratic, so every minimizer on Q′
attains f ∗ .

E.4    Certified convex recourse
Proof of lemma 7.12. Fix a query (v, Y ′ , γ, η) and let f = Fγ (v, ·) on Y ′ , after substituting the
coordinates whose interval is a single point; if none remains, return the value. Otherwise f is a


                                                     147
convex polynomial on the box B = Y ′ with explicit rational coefficients of polynomial length, and
monomial bounds give a rational Kf ≥ max{1, maxB ∇2 f max(1, diam B)2 } of polynomial length.
Let δ = min{η/2, η 2 /(8Kf ), 1}.
    A feasible near-minimizer. By (T8), with accuracy δ, one computes a rational y ∈ B with
f (y) ≤ minB f + δ. That lemma uses weak convex optimization over a capped epigraph and repairs
the near-feasibility of its output by clipping; it uses convexity of f only.
    The tangent certificate. Put u = f (y) and ℓ = f (y) + minz∈B ∇f (y)T (z − y), an explicit choice
of one endpoint per coordinate. By convexity f (z) ≥ f (y) + ∇f (y)T (z − y) ≥ ℓ on B, so ℓ ≤ minB f .
Let z̄ attain the linear minimum and ∆ = u − ℓ = −∇f (y)T (z̄ − y) ≥ 0. For t ∈ (0, 1] the
point y + t(z̄ − y) lies in B, and Taylor’s theorem gives minB f ≤ f (y) − t∆ + Kf t2 /2. Since
f (y) ≤ minB f + δ, ∆ ≤ δ/t + Kf t/2. Choose t = min{1, η/(2Kf )}. If η ≤ 2Kf , the two terms are
each at most η/4; otherwise t = 1 and ∆ ≤ η/2 + η/4. In both cases u − ℓ ≤ η. All quantities have
bit length polynomial in the query length and the length of η, so the bit work is polynomial. A
verifier recomputes f (y), ∇f (y), and the endpoint minimum, and needs neither the optimization
trace nor a modulus of strong convexity.

We prove theorems 7.9 and 7.13 together. In the quadratic case F0 = 12 xT Ax + aT x + c0 , the oracle
runs in exact mode, d = 2, and K3 is not used.

that it also bounds q-bit evaluation. Let Nact and Ctail be as in (T3) for [0, 1]n , and choose

In the quadratic case omit the term g0 /(4K3 ). All these numbers are computed from the base
instance, have bit length polynomial in I, and J, log2 M ≤ polyd (I).

The algorithm.      For j = 0, 1, . . . , J:

 (1) Run level j of the core search: exact mode in the quadratic case, certified mode with accuracy
     ηj of (31) otherwise. If k = 0 this is one oracle call with accuracy 4−j ; Uj is its upper value,
     and Hj is the empty core point.

 (2) Form the patch P of radius r about zj and its slabs. For each slab obtain its exact value
     (quadratic case) or a certified lower bound with accuracy ηout = g0 r2 /16; let λout be the least
     of these. If (33) fails with U = Uj and H = Hj , go to the next level.




                                                 148
 (3) Apply the sign tests of lemma 7.7(a) on Q = Hj × P and obtain Q′ . If no coordinate is unfixed,
     return the fixed point and its value. In the quadratic case test whether the Hessian block of
     the unfixed coordinates is positive semidefinite; if so, solve the convex quadratic program on
     Q′ exactly [30] and return its solution. In the polynomial case test lemma 7.7(b) with g0 ; if it
     passes, return (S, zS , Q′ , g0 ). Otherwise go to the next level.

If no level returns, run the fallback on the same draw: (T10) in the quadratic case and (T4)
otherwise.

Correctness on every draw. By proposition 7.5(a),(b), Hj contains the core of every global
minimizer and the corner cj , and Uj ≥ V (cj ). Each certified lower bound is at most the true slab
value. Hence lemma 7.6 shows that every global minimizer lies in Hj × P when step (2) passes, and
lemma 7.7 shows that the outputs of step (3) are correct. The fallback is exact. No step assumes
growth, uniqueness, or any probabilistic event.

Lemma E.1 (Stopping). Suppose Fγ has a unique global minimizer x∗ = (a, z ∗ ) with point growth
at least g0 , and every coordinate of x∗ at a bound has |∂i Fγ (x∗ )| > τ . Then the algorithm returns at
a level j ≤ J.

Proof. Assume no earlier level returned; we show that level J returns. Put h = 2−J and E = EJ .
     Hull and incumbent. Every retained cell has a corner v with V (v)p    ≤ f ∗ + 4E (proposition 7.5(c);
                                    ∗              2
2E in exact mode), and V (v) − f ≥ g0 ∥v − a∥ . Hence ∥v − a∥ ≤ h kL/(2g0 ), and every point of
                                                                                          √
the cell is within h of v in each coordinate. Thus HJ ⊆ a +[−A0 h, A0 h]k , because 1+ y ≤ 2+ y. By
proposition 7.5(b) the incumbent value satisfies εinc = UJ − f ∗ ≤ εJ , which is at most 2E for k ≥ 1
and equals ηJ = 4−J = h2 for k = 0; the empty-core case thus uses the positive allowance of (31),
not an exact incumbent. Growth gives ∥(cJ , zJ ) − x∗ ∥2 ≤ εinc /g0 . Since A0 ≥ max{2, kL/g0 , 8/g0 },
this yields ∥(cJ , zJ ) − x∗ ∥ ≤ A0 h ≤ r/4 and εinc ≤ g0 r2 /16; for k ≥ 1 the last inequality is
kLh2 /4 ≤ kLr2 /(64A20 ) ≤ g0 r2 /16, and for k = 0 it is h2 ≤ r2 /(16A20 ) ≤ g0 r2 /256.
     Containment. A point z of a slab differs from zJ by at least r in one coordinate, hence from z ∗ by
at least 3r/4. Growth gives Fγ (cJ , z)−f ∗ ≥ 9g0 r2 /16, so λout ≥ f ∗ +9g0 r2 /16−ηout = f ∗ +g0 r2 /2 (or

The midpoint test value is at least τ − 54 K2 r − K2 r ≥ τ − 64   9

                                                                  5
the quadratic case the exact test passes because ∂i Fγ ≥ τ − 4 K2 r > 0 on Q. A coordinate strictly

Proof of theorems 7.9 and 7.13. Correctness was shown above. Let B be the event that the hypoth-




                                                   149
esis of lemma E.1 fails. By (T2) and (T3) applied to F0 on [0, 1]n with ambient noise,
                                               ng0 2nCtail     ρ ρ
                                   Pr{g∗ < g0 } ≤  +         ≤ + ,
                                                σ       M      2 2
                      Pr{g∗ ≥ g0 and an active gradient has magnitude at most τ }
                                               τ     1
                                        ≤ Nact     +      ≤ ρ,

so Pr(B) ≤ 2ρ = 1/(2B), and the fallback runs only on B.
    Work. Since M ≥ 2J , proposition 7.5 bounds the expected number of core oracle calls by
2k + 8k JQ. Every query has length polyd (I): core corners are dyadic with at most J bits per
coordinate, sampled coefficients have O(I + log M ) bits, the accuracies Ej and ηout have polynomial
length, and the oracle’s outputs, hence the patch endpoints zj,i ± r, have polynomial length. Each
oracle call therefore costs polyd (I), with an exponent independent of k. Each level adds at most
2nR slab calls and polyd (I) work for the sign and Hessian tests and the final convex solve. The
fallback costs B polyd (I + log M ) and runs with probability at most 1/(2B) ((T5)). Summing, the
expected work is [2k + 8k JQ + (J + 1)nR ] polyd (I) ≤ 8k Q polyd (I), with Q = Qex or Qap . The
global proof record consists of the oracle answers with their checkable certificates (exact values, or
tangent bounds (35)), the retained lists, the slab bounds, and the test data; its expected size obeys
the same bound.
    Outputs. In the quadratic case the returned point is rational of polynomial length on every draw,
by Kozlov et al. [30] on the ordinary branch and (T10) on the fallback branch. In the polynomial case
the descriptor (S, zS , Q′ , g0 ) consists of dyadic hull endpoints, patch endpoints of polynomial length,
and the base number g0 ; (T8), run with η = min{2−q , (g0 /2)2−2q }, evaluates it in polyd (I + q).
The fallback branch returns the algebraic output of (T4), of length B polyd (I + log M ). For its
q-bit evaluation, enclose every coordinate with width 2−q−⌈log2 n⌉ and the value with width 2−q ; the
midpoints of the coordinate enclosures, clipped to [0, 1]n , are feasible and within Euclidean distance
2−q of the represented minimizer. This costs B polyd (I + log M + q), and the branch occurs with
probability at most 1/(2B), which gives the expected evaluation bound.

Proof of corollary 7.10. Given a query (v, i∈R [ℓ′i , u′i ], γ), substitute v and every residual coordinate
                                                 Q

whose interval is a point. For each remaining coordinate write zi = ℓ′i +(u′i −ℓ′i )yi with yi ∈ [0, 1]. The
result is y T Ãy + c̃T y + const with Ãij = 12 Aij (u′i − ℓ′i )(u′j − ℓ′j ), so its interaction graph is a subgraph
of the residual graph, a forest. The algorithm of Del Pia and Khajavirad [17, Theorem 1 and
Lemma 19] returns a rational optimal y of polynomial bit length using O(n2 ) arithmetic operations
on numbers of polynomial length, hence in polynomial bit time with an absolute exponent. Mapping
back and evaluating gives the oracle’s answer. Diagonal entries of ARR of either sign are allowed.

E.6    Core-only noise
Throughout, the hypotheses of theorem 7.15 hold, Y = [0, 1]nR , H = K2 /µ, and HV = K2 (1 + H).
The residual selector is s(v) = arg minz∈Y F0 (v, z), and V0 (v) = F0 (v, s(v)). Core-only noise changes
neither s nor the residual active sets, and Vγ = V0 + γ T v.

Lemma E.2 (Selector, value, and growth lift).

 (a) The selector is unique and ∥s(v) − s(w)∥ ≤ H ∥v − w∥.

 (b) V0 is differentiable on [0, 1]k (one-sided at the boundary), with ∇V0 (v) = ∇v F0 (v, s(v)), and
     ∇V0 is HV -Lipschitz.

 (c) If Vγ (v) − Vγ (a) ≥ gV ∥v − a∥2 on [0, 1]k with gV > 0, then x∗ = (a, s(a)) is the unique
     global minimizer of Fγ and Fγ (x) − Fγ (x∗ ) ≥ gF ∥x − x∗ ∥2 on X, where gF = min{gV /(1 +
     2H 2 ), µ/4}.

Proof. (a) Uniqueness follows from strong convexity. Write ∇z for the residual gradient. The
optimality conditions ∇z F0 (v, s(v))T (y − s(v)) ≥ 0 and ∇z F0 (w, s(w))T (y − s(w)) ≥ 0 for y ∈ Y ,
with y = s(w) and y = s(v) respectively, add to [∇z F0 (w, s(w)) − ∇z F0 (v, s(v))]T (s(w) − s(v)) ≤ 0.
Split the bracket at (w, s(v)). Strong monotonicity bounds the first part below by µ ∥s(w) − s(v)∥2 ,
and the cross block of the Hessian bounds the second part by K2 ∥w − v∥ ∥s(w) − s(v)∥.
   (b) Taylor’s theorem in v at fixed residual points gives

                      V0 (w) ≤ F0 (w, s(v))
                             ≤ V0 (v) + ∇v F0 (v, s(v))T (w − v) + K22 ∥w − v∥2 ,
                      V0 (w) = F0 (w, s(w))
                             ≥ V0 (v) + ∇v F0 (v, s(w))T (w − v) − K22 ∥w − v∥2 .

By (a), ∥∇v F0 (v, s(w)) − ∇v F0 (v, s(v))∥ ≤ K2 H ∥w − v∥. Hence

                  V0 (w) − V0 (v) − ∇v F0 (v, s(v))T (w − v) ≤ K2 12 + H ∥w − v∥2 ,
                                                                          


which proves differentiability. The Lipschitz bound follows by splitting at (w, s(v)) and using (a).
    (c) For x = (v, z) ∈ X, strong convexity in z and the optimality of s(v) on the box give
Fγ (v, z) − Vγ (v) ≥ µ2 ∥z − s(v)∥2 , so Fγ (x) − f ∗ ≥ gV ∥v − a∥2 + µ2 ∥z − s(v)∥2 . By (a), ∥v − a∥2 +
∥z − s(a)∥2 ≤ (1 + 2H 2 ) ∥v − a∥2 + 2 ∥z − s(v)∥2 , and the choice of gF completes the proof.

Lemma E.3 (Core-only tails). Let gV be the point growth modulus of Vγ on [0, 1]k (zero if the
optimal core is not unique), and let the core coefficients be independent with law Uσ,M . There is a
base-computable Ctail with log Ctail ≤ polyd (I) such that

 (a) Pr{gV < t} ≤ kt/σ + 2kCtail /M for every t > 0;

 (b) Pr{gV > 0 and some core coordinate at a bound at x∗ has |∂i Fγ (x∗ )| ≤ τ } ≤ NC (τ /σ + 1/M ),
     where NC = max{1, k3n max(1, d − 1)n }.


                                                  151
Proof. (a) The function V0 is the reduced objective of F0 on D = [0, 1]k+nR with the core as perturbed
coordinates, and gV is its point-growth modulus under the core noise. (T3) with p = k, m = nR ,
s = 2n, and S = k gives (a).
    (b) A face Φ of [0, 1]n fixes some coordinates at bounds and leaves a set FΦ free. Let i ∈ C be fixed
on Φ and condition on all core coefficients except γi . The stationary system ∂j F0 (x) + γj [j ∈ C] = 0,
j ∈ FΦ , on Φ does not involve γi , and its equations have degree at most max(1, d − 1). By (T9) it
has at most max(1, d − 1)n solutions with nonsingular Jacobian; the empty system has the single
vertex. At such a solution x, ∂i Fγ (x) = ∂i F0 (x) + γi lies in [−τ, τ ] with conditional probability
at most τ /σ + 1/M by (2). A union bound over at most 3n faces, k core coordinates, and the
solutions gives the bound for the event that some such solution has a small core gradient. If gV > 0,
lemma E.2(c) gives full growth gF > 0 at x∗ ; on the face Φ∗ whose relative interior contains x∗ ,
two-sided directions give ∇2F F F0 (x∗ ) ⪰ 2gF I, so x∗ is a nonsingular solution of the system of Φ∗ ,
and the event of (b) is contained in the union.

   (a) The selector s is continuous by lemma E.2(a) and semialgebraic: by residual convexity,

compact with dim EK ≤ q − 1.

box. Hence sF , V0 , and the multipliers λi (v) = ςi ∂zi F0 (v, s(v)), i ∈ A, are smooth on B, and λi ≥ 0
there by the optimality conditions of the residual problem.

E.7     Examples
Verification of example 7.3. For each v, the least feasible z is v − 13 ≤ 23 , so V (v) = v − 13 . Since
2j ̸≡ 0 (mod 3), the number 2j /3 has fractional part 13 or 23 , so 13 lies in the interior of a level-j
cell whose endpoints are at distances hj /3 and 2hj /3 from it. Every grid value is at least hj /3, so
Uj − Ej ≥ hj /3 − kLh2j /8 > 0 = f ∗ when hj < 8/(3kL).

z = 0, and also at z = 12 when v = 1), so Vh (v) = v 2 + 15                16 v on the grid, which gives the three

Verification of example 7.19. Write ϱ = z1 − vz2 . The residual Hessian is 2(1, −v)T (1, −v) ⪰ 0 and
∂vv F0 = 2 + 2z22 ≤ 4. Taking z = (0, 0) shows Vγ (v) = (v − 12 )2 + γv = (v − aγ )2 + f ∗ , which gives
the optimal core, its growth constant, and the optimal set {ϱ = 0, v = aγ }. For p = (0, v, 1) one
computes pT ∇2 F0 p = 0 and ∇2 F0 p = (−2ϱ, 0, 0). A positive semidefinite matrix with pT Hp = 0
has Hp = 0, so the Hessian is not positive semidefinite where ϱ =        ̸ 0; every full-dimensional box
contains such points.
    For the strictly convex variant put α = z1 − 12 , β = z2 − 12 , and ϱ = α − vβ. The residual problem
min(α − vβ)2 + β 4 is strictly convex: on a segment either β varies, and β 4 is strictly convex, or β is
constant, and the square is strictly convex in α. Its unique minimizer is α = β = 0, so Vγ and aγ
are unchanged and the optimizer (aγ , 12 , 12 ) is unique and interior. At points with β = 0 and α ̸= 0,

                                                         155
the vector p = (0, v, 1) in the coordinates (v, α, β) satisfies pT Hp = 0 and Hp = (−2α, 0, 0), and the
direction p + αev has (p + αev )T H(p + αev ) = −2α2 < 0. Such points lie arbitrarily close to the
optimizer.

Verification of examples 7.20 and 7.21. For F0 = ϕ(v) + y 2 , ∂y F0 = 2y ≥ 0 with equality only
at y = 0, so the selector is 0 and the multiplier ∂y F0 (v, 0) vanishes for all v. For the coupled
variant, ∂y F0 = 2(y + (v − 12 )2 ) ≥ 0 on y ≥ 0, the selector is 0, the multiplier is 2(v − 12 )2 , and
V0 (v) = (v − 12 )2 + (v − 21 )4 .
    In example 7.21, ∂y Fγ = v + y ≥ 0 on the box, with equality only at (0, 0), so the selector is 0
and Vγ (v) = 14 v 2 + γv. The Hessian has determinant 12 − 1 < 0. The multiplier is λ(v) = v. For
γ > 0, Vγ is increasing on [0, 1], so the optimum is (0, 0) with ∂v Fγ (0, 0) = γ.


F     Proofs for integer recourse
This appendix proves the results of section 8. Appendix F.1 proves the exact box solver, appendix F.2
the native integer theorems and the oracle statements, appendix F.3 the flow results, appendix F.4
the totally unimodular extension, and appendices F.5 and F.6 the strong-noise and low-rank results.
The tools (T1)–(T10) of appendix E.1 are used freely. We also use the following univariate facts
[7]: for integer polynomials of degree at most δ and coefficient bit length at most τ , greatest
common divisors, exact quotients, squarefree parts, modular inverses, resultants, isolation of all real
roots by disjoint rational intervals, the sign of another such polynomial at an isolated root, and
refinement of an isolating interval to width 2−q take poly(δ, τ, q) bit operations with an absolute
exponent. Algebraic degrees are polynomial in δ, and intermediate bit lengths are polynomial in
δ, τ, q. Fast complex-disk isolation and refinement for integer polynomials are also available [32,
arXiv v2, Theorem 5]. Every nonzero root t of an integer polynomial j aj tj with a0 ̸= 0 satisfies
                                                                          P

|t| ≥ |a0 | /(|a0 | + maxj≥1 |aj |), by Cauchy’s bound applied to the reversed polynomial.

F.1    The exact box solver

 (3) Let Kλ = degz χ and p = [z Kλ ]χ ∈ Q[T ]. Skip the form if p is constant, if some degz χi > Kλ ,

     let P = p/G, A = p′ /G, and Bi = bi /G. Multiply P by a positive common denominator of its
     coefficients so that P ∈ Z[T ], and put ri = Bi A−1 mod P . This scaling changes neither the
     roots nor the quotient ideal.

 (4) For every real root θ of P , keep the point with free coordinates ri (θ) and the fixed coordinates
     of the face if it lies in B; the membership test consists of signs of ri − ℓi and ui − ri at θ.

Among all kept points and vertices, select the first one of least value in a fixed order, compared
exactly by lemma F.7. Return its integer polynomial P , the isolating interval of its root θ, the maps
ri of the free coordinates and constant maps for the fixed ones, the value map rf of lemma F.7, and,
as the auxiliary representation, the value polynomial of that lemma with an isolating interval of the
value. A selected vertex is returned with P (T ) = T , θ = 0, and constant maps.

Lemma F.1 (Quotient algebra). Let F be a field containing Q and ζ ∈ F, or let F contain Q(z)
and ζ = z. Under every degree-compatible monomial order, the polynomials gi with z = ζ form a
Gröbner basis of the ideal Iζ ⊆ F[y] they generate, M is a basis of F[y]/Iζ , and the matrices of
multiplication by yi in this basis are Xi (ζ). They commute.

Proof. The term yia has coefficient one and degree a, and every other term of gi has degree at most
d − 1 < a. So the leading monomials are yia whatever ζ is, and they are pairwise coprime. By
Buchberger’s first criterion all S-polynomials reduce to zero, so the gi form a Gröbner basis, and the
monomials not divisible by any yia , namely M, form a basis of the quotient [13]. Reduction by the
gi divides only by the leading coefficient one, so the normal form of a monomial is the specialization
at ζ of the normal form computed over Q[z]. Each recursion step of the algorithm lowers the total
degree by at least a − (d − 1) ≥ 1, so the normal forms of all monomials of degree at most R,
which include yi m for m ∈ M, are computed. Multiplication operators of a commutative algebra
commute.

    Let P be the field of Puiseux series m≥1 Q((ε1/m )); it is algebraically closed [7]. Embed Q(z)
                                           S

in P by z 7→ ε−1 . For s ∈ P let val(s) be its least exponent, and for a vector or a polynomial in T
the least valuation of its entries or coefficients.

Lemma F.2 (Joint eigenvalues). There are points ξ (1) , . . . , ξ (N ) ∈ P r , not necessarily distinct,
such that every ξ (t) is a common zero of the gi (with z = ε−1 ) and det T I − i λi Xi (ε−1 ) =
                                                                                      P             
QN          T (t)               r
  t=1 (T − λ ξ ) for every λ ∈ P .

Proof. Commuting matrices over an algebraically closed field are simultaneously upper triangulariz-
                                                         (t)
able [26]. Let Π triangularize all Xi (ε−1 ), and let ξi be the tth diagonal entry of Π−1 Xi Π. Then
Π−1 Xλ Π is triangular with diagonal entries λT ξ (t) , which gives the determinant. For a polynomial h,
let Xh be the matrix of multiplication by h; the map sending h to the tth diagonal entry of Π−1 Xh Π
is a ring homomorphism, because diagonal entries of sums and products of upper triangular matrices
                                                               (t)
are sums and products of diagonal entries. It sends yi to ξi and every element of the ideal to 0. So
gi (ξ (t) ) = 0.
