Lemma F.6 (Good forms). Among λ(t) = (1, t, . . . , tr−1 ), t = 0, . . . , Qr − 1, at least one form is
good.

Proof. A form fails for t if λ(t)T w = 0 for one of at most N leading poles w, or λ(t)T (v − v ′ ) = 0
                    N
for one of at most 2 pairs of distinct limits. Each condition says that a nonzero polynomial in t
of degree at most r − 1,with coefficient vector w or v − v ′ , vanishes; it has at most r − 1 roots. So
at most (r − 1)(N + N2 ) < Qr integers fail.

Proof of correctness in theorem 8.1. For ε > 0 let x(ε) minimize f +ε i xD
                                                                           P
                                                                               i on B. Along a sequence
εm ↓ 0 choose a convergent subsequence with limit x̂; since the perturbation converges to zero
uniformly on B, x̂ minimizes f . Pass to a further subsequence on which every x(εm ) lies in the
relative interior of one face Φ. If Φ is a vertex, x̂ is that vertex, a candidate. Otherwise the free
part of x(εm ) is an interior minimizer of the deformed face function, hence a real common zero of
(51) with z = 1/εm . By lemma F.5 its limit lies in LΦ ; by lemmas F.4 and F.6 some enumerated
form recovers it exactly from a real root of P , and it lies in B. So x̂ is kept. A bad form that passes
step (3) still yields well-defined maps, because A is invertible modulo P , but its reconstructed points
need not be critical limits; for instance, its leading coefficient can retain the bounded projection
of a branch that escapes to infinity. Step (4) keeps such a point only if it lies in B. Every kept
point is therefore feasible and has value at least minB f , so a kept point of least value is a global
minimizer.

Lemma F.7 (Values and comparisons). For a kept point with root θ of P and coordinates ri (θ)
(fixed coordinates substituted), let rf = f (r(T )) mod P be the remainder of f (r1 (T ), . . . , rk (T )) on
division by P . Then deg rf < deg P and rf (θ) = f (r(θ)), the value of the point. Write rf = h/q0
with h ∈ Z[T ] and q0 ∈ Z \ {0}. The resultant ResT (P (T ), q0 W − h(T )) ∈ Z[W ] is nonzero, has
degree at most deg P , and has the value as a root, which is identified by refining the interval of θ.
Two such values are compared exactly, including equality, by isolating the real roots of the squarefree
part of the product of their two value polynomials and locating both values among them.

Proof. P divides f (r(T )) − rf (T ), and P (θ) = 0, so rf (θ) = f (r(θ)). Up to a nonzero constant
factor, the resultant is ϑ (q0 W − h(ϑ)) over the roots ϑ of P , a nonzero polynomial of degree deg P
                        Q

with the root h(θ)/q0 . Refining the interval of θ encloses h(θ)/q0 by interval evaluation; by the root
separation bound of the squarefree part, polynomially many bits suffice to single out its root. Equal
values are the same root of the squarefree product, so ties are decided exactly.

   The value map rf belongs to the output, so the optimizer and the value are polynomials in
the same root, as definition 2.5(d) requires. The value polynomial and its isolating interval are an
auxiliary representation for comparisons; composing them with the coordinate representation is
never needed.

Lemma F.8 (Cost). The algorithm uses ckd polyd (H) bit operations for a constant cd that depends
only on d. Its output, and the representations of the value, have length ckd polyd (H), and q-bit
refinements cost ckd polyd (H + q).




                                                    159
Proof. Fix a face with r ≥ 1 free coordinates. There are r+R           ≤ 2r+R ≤ 2Dr monomials of
                                                                      
                                                                   r
degree at most R and N = ar ≤ 2Dr . Clear the denominators of ∂i fΦ /D by one integer of Od (H)
bits. Each recursion step multiplies by z and by integer coefficients of Od (H) bits; a normal form
is a sum over reduction paths of at most R steps, of which there are at most (s + 1)R , where
s = polyd (r) bounds the number of monomials of a derivative. So every normal form has z-degree
at most R and coefficients of Od (R(H + log(s + 1))) bits; memoization computes all of them
without enumerating paths, in 2Od (r) polyd (H) operations. The forms have O(r2 log(N + 1))-bit
entries. Each determinant has order N , degree at most N in T and u and at most RN in z, and
is interpolated from (RN + 1)(N + 1)2 rational determinants of polynomial length; so χ and the
χi have poly(N, H, r)-bit coefficients. The Qr = O(rN 2 ) forms, the univariate steps (3)–(4) on
polynomials of degree at most N , the at most N real roots per form, and the value and comparison
steps of lemma F.7 each cost poly(N, H, r); the value map is obtained from at most H monomials
of degree at most d, each a product of at most d maps reduced modulo P . Summing over at most
3k faces gives ckd polyd (H) with cd depending on D only.
    Refinement. Refine the interval of θ and evaluate the ri and rf by interval arithmetic with
derivative bounds on the interval; polynomially many additional bits give every coordinate within
2−q−⌈log2 (k+1)⌉ , hence a point within Euclidean distance 2−q , and the value within 2−q . Clipping a
rational approximation to B does not increase its distance to the exact point, which lies in B. With
a rational bound Gf ≥ max{1, supB ∥∇f ∥1 } from monomial sums, coordinates within 2−q /(2Gf )
give a feasible rational point whose value exceeds the optimum by at most 2−q−1 ; enclosing the
value with width 2−q−1 gives a lower bound, and so a certified gap 2−q . The value gap alone would
not bound the distance to x∗ ; the two refinements are separate.

Lemma F.9 (An explicit constant). Implement the algorithm with schoolbook integer arithmetic,
fraction-free Bareiss determinants, tensor interpolation on consecutive integer nodes, subresultant
greatest common divisors and Bézout coefficients, and squarefree Sturm sequences for isolation, sign
determination, and refinement. Then its bit work for dimension k, input length H, and q refinement
bits is at most Cd 210000Dk (H + q + 1)10000 for a constant Cd depending only on d. In particular
cd = 210000D is a valid constant in theorem 8.1 and, with a larger Cd , in corollary 8.2.
Proof. Put U = 2Dk (H + q + d + 2). The ledger is deliberately loose. On a face with r ≤ k
free coordinates the number of memoized monomials and N are at most 2Dk ; normal forms have
z-degree Od (k) and coefficient bit length Od ((H + k + 1)2 ); there are O(rN 2 ) forms with Od (k 2 )-bit
coefficients; determinants have order N and degrees N , N , Od (kN ), so their interpolation grids
have Od (U 4 ) nodes, sampled values have bit length Od (U 5 ), and interpolated coefficients Od (U 8 ).
Subresultant and Sylvester bounds keep greatest common divisors, inverses, and the ri within degree
and bit length Od (U 12 ); composition with f and the value resultant stay within Od (U 18 ); Cauchy
and squarefree separation bounds, derivative bounds for evaluation, and the q extra bits require
precision Od (U 25 ). Every primitive call therefore has input length at most Cd U 50 , and the number
of primitive calls, over at most 3k ≤ 2Dk faces, forms, coordinates, nodes, roots, and comparisons, is
at most Cd U 10 .
    For a primitive call let L be the combined binary length of its dense arithmetic data, including
rational endpoints and interpolation nodes, and let qreq be the largest requested refinement precision.
Put ℓ = 2 + L + qreq . Thus precision enters numerically, rather than only through the length of its
binary encoding. The preceding bounds give ℓ ≤ Cd U 50 . We next justify a universal primitive-work
bound A(ℓ + 2)40 , which is smaller than the exponent-100 bound used below. The fixed factor A is
absorbed into the global prefactor Cd .
    Schoolbook multiplication and long division on B-bit integers cost O(B 2 ). Euclidean gcd uses
at most O(B) divisions, so rational arithmetic with reduction costs at most O(B 3 ). After clearing

                                                  160
denominators, input polynomial degrees are at most ℓ and heights at most Kℓ2 , for a fixed absolute
constant K. Hadamard’s inequality bounds Sylvester minors by Kℓ3 bits. The subresultant identities
show that each nonzero primitive Euclidean remainder is proportional to a subresultant; skipping
defective degrees gives the same bound. Therefore primitive gcd and signed Sturm data have
heights at most Kℓ4 . Ordinary rational long division, followed by division by positive contents,
computes these sequences in at most Kℓ3 coefficient operations, with intermediate lengths at most
Kℓ6 . This gives at most Kℓ21 bit work, including exact quotients and squarefree parts. The classical
subresultant identities and Sturm correctness are used here [7]; the numerical work estimate follows
from this operation count.
    Bézout coefficients and modular inverses can be obtained from a consistent Sylvester linear
system with at most 2ℓ + O(1) unknowns. Exact pivoting and fraction-free determinants bound the
intermediate lengths by Kℓ8 . At most Kℓ2 determinants, each requiring O(ℓ3 ) exact operations, give
a bound below Kℓ30 . Bareiss determinants, resultants, and consecutive-node Lagrange interpolation
satisfy the same loose bound: intermediate entries are minors, and products of node differences
have polynomial height. Tensor interpolation has at most three variables and is a fixed number of
successive univariate interpolations.
    For isolation, let p be a primitive squarefree polynomial of degree n ≤ ℓ and height τ ′ ≤ Kℓ4 .
Degrees zero and one are handled directly. For n ≥ 2, every root has absolute value at most
        ′
R = 2τ +2 . The discriminant is a nonzero integer. If ζ, η are two closest roots, its product formula
gives
                                  1 ≤ |an |2n−2 |ζ − η|2 (2R)n(n−1)−2 ,
where an is the leading coefficient. Hence a sufficiently large fixed integer K gives a separation
bound 2−S with S = K(n + 1)2 (τ ′ + 1) ≤ Kℓ6 . Bisect [−R, R], subdividing only intervals that
contain a real root by exact Sturm counts. At any depth at most n open intervals contain roots;
roots at endpoints or midpoints are recorded once as point intervals. One-sided variations, obtained
from the first nonzero derivative at a zero endpoint, handle these cases. After K(τ ′ + S + qreq + 1)
levels, distinct roots are separated and the isolators have the requested width. Thus at most Kℓ7
variation evaluations suffice, at endpoints of at most Kℓ6 bits. Homogenized Horner evaluation and
endpoint derivative tests use at most Kℓ3 integer operations per evaluation, with operands of at
most Kℓ7 bits. Their total cost is below Kℓ30 , including rational normalization. This also bounds
refinement of a given isolator.
     For a sign test at a stored root, gcd membership first detects a zero value. Otherwise apply
the same separation and isolation argument to the squarefree part of the product of the two input
polynomials. The stored root is then separated from all other roots, and the sign is evaluated
at a rational point in its interval. The product has degree at most 2ℓ and height at most Kℓ2
before squarefree extraction, so the same ledger applies with a larger fixed K. Comparing two
represented values uses their two value polynomials in exactly this way, including equality. All the
listed primitives therefore fit A(ℓ + 2)40 and, in particular, A(ℓ + 2)100 . The program constants are
fixed independently of dimension, degree and precision. The total work is at most

                 Cd′ U 10 (Cd U 50 + 2)100 ≤ Cd′′ U 5010 ≤ Cd′′′ 210000Dk (H + q + 1)10000 .



   The constant is an explicit proved bound used to define noise laws and fallback budgets before
sampling. A smaller constant with a proved bound of the same form may replace it.

Proof of corollary 8.2. Enumerate the integer labels, substitute each, and apply theorem 8.1; sub-
stitution keeps the degree and gives coefficients of length polyd (H). Keep the current best value

and compare each new value with it by lemma F.7. Each comparison involves two univariate
polynomials of degree at most (D − 1)k , so the total is ckd i Ni polyd (H). The explicit budget above
                                                            Q

includes these comparisons; only its global prefactor is enlarged. Only the winner’s representation is
retained; isolating its roots afresh in their own polynomials removes any extra precision used in
comparisons.

Lemma F.10 (Competing labels). Let H be a core box, c ∈ H, and z ∈ Y with Fγ (c, z) = V (c). The
sets Yi− = {z ′ ∈ Y : zi′ ≤ zi − 1} and Yi+ = {z ′ ∈ Y : zi′ ≥ zi + 1} cover Y \ {z}. If (38) holds with
Vother (c) the least exact value over the nonempty ones (+∞ if none), then z is the unique minimizer
of Fγ (v, ·) on Y for every v ∈ H. If moreover H contains the core of every global minimizer, every
global minimizer lies in H × {z} and minv∈[0,1]k Fγ (v, z) = f ∗ .
Proof. If Y = {z}, the conclusions are immediate. Otherwise, a label z ′ ̸= z differs in some
coordinate, and by integrality zi′ ≤ zi − 1 or zi′ ≥ zi + 1. For v ∈ H, lemma 7.2(c), applied to Y \ {z}
and to {z}, gives

                             VY \{z} (v) ≥ Vother (c) − Gw∞ (H)
                                        > V (c) + Gw∞ (H)
                                        = Fγ (c, z) + Gw∞ (H) ≥ Fγ (v, z).

A global minimizer (a, z ∗ ) with a ∈ H therefore has z ∗ = z. The slice [0, 1]k × {z} lies in X and
contains (a, z), so its minimum is f ∗ .

    Algorithm and correctness. At levels j = 0, . . . , J run the core search in exact mode with
the integer oracle, and test (38) at (cj , zj ) with at most 2nR further oracle calls on tightened
bounds. When it passes, apply theorem 8.1 to Fγ (·, zj ) on [0, 1]k and return. Otherwise, after

lemma F.10, every return is correct.
    Probability. By lemma F.11, the fallback runs only if g∗ < g0 , which by (T3) has probability at
most Sg0 /σ + 2nCtail /M ≤ ρ = 1/(4B).
    Work and output. By proposition 7.5 the expected number of core calls is at most 2k + 8k JQex ;
each call, including the label calls, has polynomial query length and costs polyd (I). The core slice
has coefficients of length polyd (I + log M ) = polyd (I), so the completion costs ckd polyd (I) and
returns a representation of that length. The fallback contributes polyd (I) in expectation by (T5),
and returns one label with its re-isolated core representation, of the same length bound. The proof
record contains the oracle answers, retained lists, label values, and the solver’s computation; its
expected size obeys the work bound. For quadratic F0 replace theorem 8.1 by (T10) on the core
box (3k poly(I), rational output) and take B = max{2, NZ 3k }.

Proof of proposition 8.6. Write Az = b as Az ≥ b, −Az ≥ −b; with the bound rows, the constraint
matrix remains totally unimodular, and tightening bounds keeps it so. At a rational core point v
and rational linear coefficients, each t 7→ fi (v, t) + γi t is a convex polynomial on [ℓi , ui ]; extending
it beyond each endpoint by the tangent line there gives a convex function on R that agrees on
the interval and has rational values of polynomial length at rational points. The proximity-scaling
algorithm of Hochbaum and Shanthikumar [24, Algorithm 4.2 and Theorem 4.3] solves separable


                                                    163
convex integer minimization over totally unimodular systems exactly, using a number of linear
programs logarithmic in the numerical bounds, each with an optimal extreme point computed
exactly in polynomial bit time [23], and function evaluations at prescribed points. All data have
polynomial length, so the oracle runs in polynomial bit time, and the exact value is the evaluation
at the returned integer point. Infeasibility is detected by the linear program of the first stage.

Proof of lemma 8.7. Sufficiency. Let z ′ ∈ Y and ∆ = z ′ − z. It is a circulation; route ∆a > 0
units on the forward residual copy of a and −∆a units on the backward copy when ∆a < 0; both
copies are available because z ′ satisfies the bounds. By discrete convexity, fa (za′ ) − fa (za ) is at
least the residual arc cost times the number of units. Summing, F (z ′ ) − F (z) is at least the cost of
this nonnegative residual circulation, which equals its reduced cost because potential terms cancel
around a circulation, and so is nonnegative.
    Necessity. A simple residual cycle using at most one copy of each original arc admits one unit of
feasible augmentation. Its objective change is exactly the sum of its unit marginal costs, which is
nonnegative by optimality. This includes two-cycles on distinct arcs and one-arc loops. The only
simple cycle using both residual copies of an original arc is their two-cycle, whose cost is nonnegative
by convexity. Thus no simple residual cycle has negative cost. Hence shortest-path distances π from
an added source joined to every node by zero-cost arcs are finite and satisfy ce + πp − πp′ ≥ 0 on
every residual arc from p to p′ . A shortest path uses at most s arcs. When unit marginal costs are
rational of bit length at most Hm , its length, and hence each potential, has bit length polynomial in
s and Hm .

F.3    Flows under core-only noise
Proof of corollary 8.8. The feasible residual set is fixed, so lemma 7.2(b) and lemma 3.7 apply to
the conditional value V . Every core point belongs to a grid cell of side h = 1/m, and a corner of
that cell has conditional value at most V (v) + kLh2 /8. Thus minGm ℓ − kLh2 /8 ≤ min V = f ∗ . If v0
minimizes the lower answers on Gm , then the least upper answer satisfies Fγ (v̂, ẑ) ≤ u(v0 ) ≤ ℓ(v0 )+η;
subtracting the displayed lower bound proves the interval and gap claims. Feasibility comes from
the returned completion. The choice of m makes kLh2 /8 at most ε. For integer flows the exact
oracle of proposition 8.6 has η = 0; approximate calls have the separately specified allowance η. No
residual variable is rounded.

    We treat networks and totally unimodular systems together. Let Y = {z ∈ ZnR : Az = b, ℓ ≤
z ≤ u} with A totally unimodular; a network is the case of a node–arc incidence matrix. The
objective is Fγ (v, z) = ϕ(v) + i fi (v, zi ) + γ T v with fi (v, ·) convex on [ℓi , ui ]. A multiplier map
                                 P

is a polynomial vector ψ(w) ∈ RnR such that ψ(w)T z is constant on Y for every w; for a network
ψa (w) = πp′ (w) − πp (w) for an arc a = (p, p′ ), and for a totally unimodular system ψ(w) = AT λ(w).
The adjusted costs are gi (w, t) = fi (w, t) − ψi (w)t, and

                   Fγ (w, z) − Fγ (w, z ′ ) =       gi (w, zi ) − gi (w, zi′ )   (z, z ′ ∈ Y ).

Definition F.12 (Charts). For a network, a chart is determined by a feasible flow z, a spanning
arborescence T rooted at an added source in the residual network of z augmented by zero-cost
source arcs, an arc a, and an integer t with t, t + 1 ∈ [ℓa , ua ]. Its potentials π T (v) are the sums
of the residual unit marginals of z along the tree paths, and its chart polynomial is m(v) =
fa (v, t + 1) − fa (v, t) + πpT (v) − πpT′ (v). The family C of all charts has at most NZ (2nR + s)s R1
members, R1 = a (ua − ℓa ).
                  P



                                                       164
    A unit difference fa (v, t + 1) − fa (v, t) has core degree at most d − 1, so chart polynomials and
potentials have core degree at most d − 1, adjusted costs have total degree at most d, and all chart
coefficients have bit length at most a base bound H0 = polyd (I): labels have base length, and tree
paths have at most s arcs. A tree chosen by the algorithm is a member of C, because the family
contains every structurally valid arborescence of every label. The residual reduced costs of a flow
under the potentials of such a tree are, up to sign, chart polynomials.

Proof of theorem 8.9. Algorithm. Run the core search in exact mode with the convex flow oracle.
At level j, at the incumbent corner cj with flow zj , compute shortest-path distances in the residual
network at cj , choose a spanning arborescence of the subgraph of tight arcs (zero reduced cost) by
graph search, and form its potentials π(v). For every arc of the residual network of zj decide exactly
whether its reduced cost is nonnegative on Hj , by minimizing it with theorem 8.1 and comparing
the algebraic minimum with zero; the source arcs serve only to build the tree and are not tested. If
all pass, lemma 8.7 shows that zj is optimal at every point of Hj , which contains every optimal
core; so the slice [0, 1]k × {zj } contains a global minimizer, and theorem 8.1 solves it. After level J
use label enumeration as in theorem 8.4. Every return is correct, with or without the interiority
premise.
    Exceptional set. Let S ⊆ [0, 1]k be the union of the zero sets of the chart polynomials that are not
identically zero, and E = w [−∇Fw ](S) over feasible labels w, where Fw (v) = ϕ(v) + a fa (v, wa ).
                             S                                                             P

Both are compact of dimension at most k − 1. For one chart polynomial m and one label w, the
set {β : ∃x ∈ [0, 1]k , m(x) = 0, β + ∇Fw (x) = 0} is defined by a formula with one block of k
variables, k free variables, O(k) atoms, and degree at most max{d, 2}. By (T6) it is a Boolean
                                                                                               2
combination of at most Ξd (k) atoms of degree at most Ξd (k), with Ξd (k) = (k + 1)Od (k ) ; as in
the proof of proposition 7.16(b), the product of its nonconstant atoms is a nonzero polynomial of
degree at most Ξd (k)3 vanishing on it. So E lies in the zero set of a nonzero polynomial of degree at
most Dint = max{1, NZ |C| Ξd (k)3 }, and log Dint ≤ polyd (I). Nothing of this is computed by the
algorithm.
    Base choices. Let B be the label-enumeration budget of theorem 8.4, H2 ≥ 1 a monomial bound
on ∇2 Fw over the core box for all labels in the bounding box, Ctube = C(k, Dint ) with C from
proposition 7.16, and Cgrowth the constant of (T3) for the reduced objective V0 (core perturbed, flows
as the eliminated block). Choose ρ = 1/(4B), g0 = ρσ/(2k), A0 = 2+kL/g0 , δ = ρσ/(4Ctube H2 ), the
least J with 2−J ≤ δ/(2kA0 ), and the least power of two M ≥ max{2, 2J , 4kCgrowth /ρ, 4kCtube /ρ}.
    Probability. Let a be an optimal core and w an optimal flow at a. Then Fw + γ T v ≥ Vγ
with equality at a, so a minimizes this smooth function on the box; by the interiority premise
γ = −∇Fw (a), and dist(γ, E) ≤ H2 dist(a, S). By (T3) and the grid tube bound in the proof of
proposition 7.16(c), the probability
                                   √    that the projected growth is below g0 or that dist(γ, E) ≤ H2 δ
is at most ρ + Ctube (H2 δ/σ + k/M ) ≤ 23 ρ < 1/(2B).
    Stopping. Outside this event the optimal core a is unique, as in lemma F.11 every point of HJ is
within Euclidean distance kA0 hJ ≤ δ/2 of a, and dist(a, S) > δ; so HJ ∩ S = ∅. Each reduced cost
of the computed tree is, up to sign, a chart polynomial. If it is identically zero it passes. Otherwise
it has no zero on the connected box HJ , so it has constant sign there; it is nonnegative and, since
cJ ∈/ S, nonzero at cJ ; hence it is positive on HJ . The test passes by level J.
    Work. Each level makes at most 2nR exact box minimizations, of cost ckd polyd (I) in total over
the J levels after absorbing J and nR , plus shortest paths and the core search. With one completion
and the expected fallback, the expected work is [8k Qex + ckd ] polyd (I). Quadratic objectives use
(T10) for the box minimizations and the completion.

Proof of theorem 8.11. For a network take ψa (w) = πp′ (w) − πp (w) from the tree potentials. At c
they certify optimality (lemma 8.7), which says exactly that each ga (c, ·) is minimized at za0 , and

so fa (c, ·) is affine there and ∂tt fa (c, t) = 0; since ∂tt fa (c + hςi ei , t) ≥ 0 for small h ≥ 0, the inward
derivative ςi ∂vi fa (c, ·) is convex on that interval. With linear interpolation on two-point intervals
and substitution on singletons, and tangent extensions, βi is the optimum of a separable convex
flow problem on the tightened bounds (proposition 8.6). The polynomial identities in (a) need

Lemma F.13 gives the conclusions.

Lemma F.14 (Value margins away from zeros). Let p ̸= 0 be a rational polynomial of degree at
most d in q ≤ k variables with coefficients of bit length at most H0 , let δ > 0 be rational, let Zp be
its zero set in [0, 1]q , and let K = {x ∈ [0, 1]q : dist(x, Zp ) ≥ δ} (the whole cube if Zp = ∅). If K ̸= ∅,
                                                                                                2
then minK |p| ≥ 2−(B0 +1) with B0 ≤ Ξ′d (k) polyd (H0 + ⟨δ⟩ + 1) and Ξ′d (k) = (k + 1)Od (k ) .
Proof. For q = 0 the value is a nonzero rational with denominator at most 2H0 . Let q ≥ 1. By
compactness m = minK |p| > 0. The formula

           ∃x ∀y : x ∈ [0, 1]q ∧ ¬(y ∈ [0, 1]q ∧ p(y) = 0) ∨ ∥x − y∥2 ≥ δ 2 ∧ 0 < p(x)2 < t

has truth set (m2 , ∞): a minimizer witnesses every t > m2 , and a witness lies in K, so no t ≤ m2
is witnessed. It has two blocks of q variables, one free variable, O(q) atoms, and degree at most
2 max{d, 1}. Clearing denominators gives integer coefficients of bit length at most q+d

By (T6), an equivalent quantifier-free formula has integer coefficients of bit length at most B0 of the
stated form. The boundary point m2 is a root of a nonconstant atom polynomial, since otherwise
all atom signs would be constant near m2 . Remove a power of t from it; the remaining integer

Lemma F.16 (Affine margins). Let p(x) = p0 + π T x be a nonzero affine polynomial on [0, 1]q whose
coefficients have numerators and denominators of bit length at most H0 , and let Zp be its zero set
in the cube. If Zp ̸= ∅, then |p(x)| ≥ 2−H0 dist(x, Zp ); otherwise |p| ≥ 2−(q+1)H0 on the cube.

Proof. Let πmin ≥ 2−H0 be the least nonzero |πi |. If Zp ̸= ∅ and p(x) > 0, some vertex has p ≤ 0.
Move from x toward it one coordinate with πi =     ̸ 0 at a time; p decreases at rate at least πmin
per unit length and reaches zero after an ℓ1 path of length at most p(x)/πmin , which bounds the
Euclidean distance to Zp . Negative values are symmetric. If Zp = ∅, p has one sign on the cube and
its least absolute value is attained at a vertex, where it is a nonzero rational whose denominator
divides a product of at most q + 1 denominators.

Proof of corollary 8.13. All unit marginals ψa (t + 1) − ψa (t) + (W T v)a , all potentials, and all chart
polynomials are affine in the core with base-bounded coefficients. Replace µ0 in the proof of
theorem 8.12 by 2−(k+1)H0 δ/2, valid by lemma F.16 at distance δ/2; it has polynomial length,
so J, log2 M ≤ polyd (I). The identities in (a) are coefficient comparisons, the minima in (b) are
endpoint choices, and the inward derivative ςi (∂i ϕ(c) + γi + (W z)i ) is linear in z, so βi is a linear-cost
flow problem on the tightened bounds. The tube degree bound still uses (T6) for the possibly
nonlinear ϕ, but only its logarithm enters. The per-level work is polynomial and is absorbed by
8k Qex ; the completion minimizes ϕ plus a linear function, at cost ckd polyd (I); quadratic ϕ gives
rational output by (T10).

F.4    Totally unimodular systems
Substitute fixed coordinates, check consistency, and delete dependent rows; the remaining A is
totally unimodular with full row rank m, and every native interval has positive length.



                                                    168
Lemma F.17 (A compact dual certificate). Let z 0 be an integer optimum at a rational core point
c. There is λ ∈ Rm with

 fi (c, zi0 ) − fi (c, zi0 − 1) ≤ (AT λ)i (zi0 > ℓi ),     (AT λ)i ≤ fi (c, zi0 + 1) − fi (c, zi0 ) (zi0 < ui ). (54)

Every solution certifies optimality of z 0 . With a rational bound V̄ ≥ 1 on the absolute values of all
unit marginals over the core cube, a solution exists in [−R, R]m , R = mV̄ + 1, and a vertex of (54)
intersected with this box is computed in polynomial time; its active basis C, a nonsingular submatrix
of AT with unit rows appended, has an inverse with entries in {0, ±1}.

Proof. Let fˆi be the piecewise-linear interpolation of fi (c, ·) at the integers of [ℓi , ui ]; it is convex.
On each integer unit cell the function i fˆi is affine and the cell intersected with {Az = b} is a
                                              P

polytope with a totally unimodular matrix and integral right-hand side, so its vertices are integral.
Hence the minimum of i fˆi over the real polytope equals the integer minimum, and z 0 is a real
                              P

optimum. Linear programming duality for the epigraph formulation gives λ such that z 0 minimizes
P ˆ               T
  i [fi (zi ) − (A λ)i zi ] over the box; for convex piecewise-linear functions with integer breakpoints
this is (54). Conversely, (54) and discrete convexity make each fi (c, t) − (AT λ)i t minimal at zi0 , and
λT Az = λT b on Y .
     Every column has an inequality, and a line in the solution set would be orthogonal to all
columns, which full row rank excludes; so the nonempty polyhedron has a vertex, determined by m
independent active rows forming a nonsingular signed submatrix of AT , whose inverse has entries in
{0, ±1} by Cramer’s rule. Its coordinates are at most mV̄ in absolute value, so the box contains it.
A vertex of the bounded polyhedron is found by exact linear programming, and its active rows are
extracted from the original inequalities and box rows; appending unit rows to AT preserves total
unimodularity.

Proof of theorem 8.14 and corollary 8.15. At a core point the algorithm uses the totally unimodular
oracle of proposition 8.6, computes λ and its basis C by lemma F.17, and keeps the basis symbolically:
λ(v) = C−1 h(v), where h(v) consists of unit marginals and the constants ±R. This is a multiplier
map of core degree at most d − 1 with base-bounded coefficients, and the adjusted costs at c are
minimized at z 0 . The chart family consists of the polynomials fi (v, t + 1) − fi (v, t) − (AT λ(v))i over
all labels, ordered bases of at most 2nR + 2m candidate rows, coordinates, and adjacent pairs; it
has at most NZ (2nR + 2m)m R1 members, so all logarithms remain polynomial. Only the original
adjusted marginals are tested on the hull; the artificial box rows serve to select a bounded vertex at c.
Lemmas 8.10 and F.13 hold for A, and the proofs of theorem 8.12 and corollary 8.13 apply verbatim
with these charts. For corollary 8.15, the slack system Cz + s = b, 0 ≤ si ≤ bi − minℓ≤z≤u Ci z,
has the totally unimodular matrix [C I] and projects bijectively onto Y ; slacks have zero cost and
coupling, so the objective, its core derivatives, and L are unchanged, and the added bounds have
polynomial length.

F.5    Strong fields
Proof of theorem 8.16. Enclosures. For a monomial derivative, the exact range of each univariate
power on its interval is computed from the endpoints, adding 0 for an even positive power on an
interval containing 0; the product of these intervals, scaled by the coefficient, and the sum over
monomials enclose ∂i F0 on the hull. All endpoints have polynomial length, and each qi is computed
by counting the grid indices of the closed interval [−∂ i , −∂ i ].
    Pinning. If γi > −∂ i , then ∂i Fγ > 0 on the whole continuous hull; for any feasible x with
xi > ℓi , decreasing xi to ℓi , continuously or between integer labels, strictly decreases Fγ , and the


                                                         169
product domain keeps the point feasible. So every global minimizer has xi = ℓi ; the case γi < −∂ i
is symmetric. The bad event is γi ∈ [−∂ i , −∂ i ], which leaves coordinate i unpinned by this test.
These events depend on one coefficient each and are independent with probabilities qi .
    Separation. After substituting the pinned values, each remaining monomial has pairwise adjacent
                                                                                      P
bad variables, which lie in one component of the induced subgraph. So Fγ = cγ + C fC,γ on a
product of component domains, and choosing a minimizer of each summand minimizes the sum.
Each component is solved by corollary 8.2, or for quadratics by (T10), in A(C) polyd (I + log2 M )
              Q
with A(C) = i∈C ai ; the separated data have length polyd (I + log2 M ).
    Expectation. A connected set of size t containing a vertex v has a rooted ordered spanning
tree with root v; there are at most 4t−1 shapes and at most ∆t−1 + choices of neighbors, so at most
(4∆+ )t−1 such sets. A connected set S is a bad component only if all its vertices are bad, with
                     Q
probability at most i∈S qi . Hence
                                                                                                  P

                   X               X       Y                 X              X

                   C            S connected i∈S               v             t≥1

    Refinement. Put q = q + ⌈log2 (n + 1)⌉. For each component separately, use theorem 8.1 to
                                 ′                                                        ′
obtain point error at most 2−q relative to its stored optimizer, value width at most 2−q , and a
                                                        ′
feasible rational point with certified gap at most 2−q . Clip continuous approximations to their
boxes and keep integer labels exact. Combining the point approximations with pinned coordinates
                                √     ′
gives Euclidean error at most n 2−q ≤ 2−q ; adding value intervals and certified gaps gives width
                        ′
and gap at most n2−q ≤ 2−q . The gap witnesses and the point approximations may be refined
separately; no distance conclusion is inferred from an objective gap. This adds only O(log(n + 1))
bits. If all coordinates are pinned, the rational point and value are returned directly. The proof
record (enclosures, pinning comparisons, partition, component computations) is checked within the
same bound; independence and the noise condition enter only the expectation.

F.6    The low-rank lattice theorem
Proof of theorem 8.17. Expansion and scalar recourse. Work with the expanded coefficients of all
listed pieces. Expanding a shifted or factored piece of degree at most the fixed d∗ takes polynomial
bit work and preserves polynomial coefficient length. Let Qden be the denominator product specified
in section 8.6; all expanded coefficients have denominator dividing it. For a rational a ∈ Rk put
W (a) = α2 ∥a∥2 +minx∈X [G(x)−αaT T x]. The inner problem separates into ψj (t) = gj (t)−α(T T a)j t
on {Lj , . . . , Uj }. Convexity gives 2ψj (t + 1) ≤ ψj (t) + ψj (t + 2), so its forward differences are
nondecreasing even when consecutive arguments lie in different pieces. For each difference, locate the
piece at each argument by exact comparisons with the rational knots and evaluate both polynomials.
At a knot either adjacent piece gives the same value by continuity.
     Binary search for the first t with ψj (t + 1) − ψj (t) ≥ 0 returns a minimizer; if none exists
choose Uj . A singleton interval is evaluated directly. The neighboring signs at the returned point,
when the neighbors exist, certify optimality by summing the nondecreasing differences to any other
integer. Ties, constant pieces, affine pieces, and changes of derivative at knots are allowed. There
are O(1 + log(Uj − Lj + 1)) comparisons per coordinate, without enumerating its labels. This
returns W (a) and an attaining integer witness x(a). If k = 0, these scalar searches solve the original
separable objective directly; henceforth assume k ≥ 1.
     Square completion. Since α2 ∥a∥2 − αaT T x = α2 ∥a − T x∥2 − α2 ∥T x∥2 , W (a) = minx [F (x) +
α              2             α     2

                                                             170
curvature α. With Zξ (a) = W (a) + ξ T a and Cξ = ∥ξ∥2 /(2α),
                                           h        α          ξ 2i

and every witness satisfies Fξ (x(a)) ≤ Zξ (a) + Cξ .
    Search and count. Run the corrected-corner search of theorem 3.9 on A with f = W , c = ξ,
curvature α in every coordinate, and the isotropic balanced meshes of definition 3.5 (hj = s2−j ),
through the predetermined level J = log2 M , with exact evaluations of Zξ . Since miJ ≤ 2J = M ,
corollary 3.6 with the coordinatewise laws Uσi ,M gives expected level counts at most Hrat , and the
expected number of evaluations is at most 2k + 8k Hrat J. The comparison in corollary 3.6 applies
only to coordinates with interior grid nodes; coordinates not yet subdivided contribute their two
endpoints. Zero, dependent, or constant rows of T cause no exception: each auxiliary width is
positive because σi > 0.
    Exact termination. By theorem 3.9(iii), the incumbent value satisfies UJ ≤ minA Zξ + EJ , and
its witness has 0 ≤ Fξ (xinc ) − Fξ∗ ≤ UJ + Cξ − (minA Zξ + Cξ ) ≤ EJ , and

                                 αX 2      kαs2     1          1
                          EJ =       hiJ ≤      ≤       <            .
                                 8 i       8M 2   8D0 M   D0 (M − 1)

Here D0 = 2Q3den , and the choice M ≥ max{2, kαs2 D0 } gives the second inequality. Every
                                                           1
base denominator divides Qden . For integer x, gj (xj ) ∈ Qden Z, α2 ∥T x∥2 ∈ 2Q13 Z, and, writing
                                                                                     den
                                                                                  1
ξi = σi (2Ki − (M − 1))/(M − 1) with the sampled index Ki , ξ T T x ∈ Q2                Z. So every value
                                                                             den (M −1)
                   1
Fξ (x) lies in D0 (M −1) Z. The optimum lies in this lattice because X is finite. The nonnegative
incumbent gap is strictly smaller than its spacing, so it is zero and xinc is optimal; its value is
computed exactly. One common grid size for all rows is what makes the original values share the
single denominator factor M − 1; the auxiliary values Zξ and Cξ can involve (M − 1)2 , and no
lattice property of them is used. An integer unary monomial is an integer, whatever its degree, and
a rational knot only selects a piece; neither contributes another sampling denominator.
    Bit lengths and work. The sum of the expanded denominator lengths bounds log Qden , so
log D0 = O(I). Row extrema are obtained by rational endpoint sums. The widths, s, and kαs2 D0
therefore have polynomial length. Comparing rationals computes the least adequate power of two
in polynomial work; it does not enumerate its support. Thus J = log2 M ≤ polyd∗ (I), sampling
uses exactly kJ fair bits, and each ξi has polynomial length.
    Every feasible integer has input-bounded bit length. Raising a B-bit integer to a power at
most d∗ uses O(d∗ B) bits; rational Horner evaluation on a listed piece has polynomial numerator
and denominator length. Piece lookup compares rational knots with binary integers by integer
products, and both lookup and the number of scalar comparisons are polynomial in I. This also
covers negative labels and knots within a unit integer step. Continuity and convexity can be checked
with rational endpoint evaluations and univariate sign determination on each piece; the input length
counts the complete list.
    At level j ≤ J, each queried auxiliary coordinate is its base endpoint plus an integer index times
wi /mij , with polynomial length in I + j. Consequently α(T T a)j , all scalar differences, W (a), Zξ (a),
Ej , and each original witness value have uniformly polynomial length. Each query combines only
polynomially many rationals; denominators do not accumulate from unrelated calls because an
incumbent is selected by comparison and each new query is constructed from the base data and its
mesh indices. Cell addresses have O(kJ) bits. Every query and every cell therefore costs polyd∗ (I)

                                                   171
with an exponent independent of k, even on a draw visiting many cells. Multiplying this cost by
2k + 8k Hrat J proves the expected bound. The final integer vector has input-bounded length, and
the same fixed-degree evaluation bound gives a polynomial-length rational optimal value on every
draw.

This appendix proves theorems 9.4 and 9.10 and proposition 9.11.

G.2     Proof of theorem 9.10
All three parts, including the core-only-noise and deterministic consequences, also appear in the
unpublished companion manuscript [20]; they are reproduced here so that the proof is self-contained.
Both use the same two tools.
Lemma G.1 (A sign test). Let Φ be a polynomial on a box Z with ∇2 Φ ⪰ κI on Z for some
κ ∈ (0, 1], let z 0 be its unique minimizer on Z, and let ζ be an affine function with ∥∇ζ∥ ≤ 1. Put
ε = κ/4 and, on Z × [0, 1],
                                     H(z, θ) = Φ(z) + θ2 + ε θ ζ(z).
Then ∇2 H ⪰ 43 κI, H has a unique minimizer (z H , θH ), and θH > 0 if and only if ζ(z 0 ) < 0.
Proof. The Hessian of Φ(z) + θ2 is at least κI, and the bilinear term has Hessian of operator norm
ε ∥∇ζ∥ ≤ κ/4. This gives the modulus and uniqueness on the compact convex box. On the face
θ = 0, H equals Φ, whose unique minimizer is z 0 . At (z 0 , 0) the z-gradient of H is ∇Φ(z 0 ), which
satisfies the first-order conditions of Φ on Z, and ∂θ H = εζ(z 0 ). If ζ(z 0 ) ≥ 0, the point (z 0 , 0)
satisfies the first-order conditions of the convex function H on its box, so it is the minimizer and
θH = 0. If ζ(z 0 ) < 0, increasing θ decreases H, so (z 0 , 0) is not the minimizer; no other point of the
face θ = 0 is either, since a minimizer on that face minimizes Φ. Hence θH > 0.

Lemma G.4 (Integral radical sums). If positive integers a1 , . . . , an satisfy i ai ∈ Z, then every
ai is a perfect square.
                    √          √                                                √
Proof. Let F = Q( a1 , . . . , an ) and Tr = TrF /Q . If ai is not a square, Q( ai ) has degree two
                   √                                                     √
and the trace of ai from it to Q is zero, so transitivity gives Tr( ai ) = 0. A rational z has
                                             P √
Tr(z) = [F : Q]z. Applying the trace to i ai = B ′ ∈ Z shows that the sum over the square
                                                                √
radicands alone equals B ′ , so the sum of the positive numbers ai over the nonsquares is zero, and
there are no nonsquares.

    The field F is used only in the proof; the reduction tests squareness by integer square roots.
If every ai is a square, decide the instance by integer arithmetic. If B ≥ 2KR, the answer is yes,

                                                        175
because s0 ≤ 2. Otherwise b = B/(KR) ∈ [0, 2), and s0 ̸= b by lemma G.4. Take two copies of Ftree
with root variables s± , apply lemma G.1 with κ = 18 to the first copy with ζ+ = b − s+ and to the
                                                                 3           H > 0 exactly when s0 > b
second with ζ− = s− − b, and apply lemma G.2 with µ0 = 32           . Then θ+

                                  P √                   P √
minimizer has y-coordinate 1 if i ai > B and 0 if i ai < B.
     For the structure, take the two decompositions of lemma G.3, add the bags {s± , θ± } at their
roots and the bags {θ± , y} attached to them, and join the two new bags; this is a tree decomposition
of bag size three that covers every term, so the interaction graph has treewidth at most two. The
diagonal Hessian entries are at most 92 for leaf variables, 52 for internal variables, 2 + 2η for θ± , and
4η for y, hence at most 5. All coordinate intervals have width at most two. The affine maps onto
[0, 1] preserve degree and scopes and multiply diagonal Hessian entries by at most four, giving the
bound 20. Every term has degree at most four, at most three variables, and coefficients bounded
by an absolute constant after the substitution, and every variable lies in at most four terms, so
every nonconstant monomial of the expanded polynomial has a coefficient bounded by an absolute
constant. The constant term need not be bounded, and deleting it does not change the minimizer.
The construction takes polynomial time.

Part (b): PosSLP. A straight-line program is a sequence of integers A1 , . . . , As in which each
Aj is 0, 1, or Al ◦ Al′ with l, l′ < j and ◦ ∈ {+, −, ×}; its output is A = As . The integers can
have exponentially many bits. The construction never computes them; it writes only bounded local
formulas.

   Take two copies of G with outputs t± , apply lemma G.1 with κ = 1, ζ+ = −t+ , and ζ− = t− ,
and apply lemma G.2 with µ0 = 34 . Since t∗ ̸= 0, θ+  H > 0 exactly when A > 0 and θ H > 0 exactly
                                                                                      −
when A ≤ 0. The resulting quartic Φ is convex on its rational box, and its unique minimizer has
y-coordinate 1 if A > 0 and 0 if A ≤ 0. Every residual xi − pi (x) has at most three monomials and
every weight has O(N ) bits, so Φ is an explicit rational quartic with O(N ) monomials, computed in
time polynomial in the size of the program.

Part (c). For Fγ , the core and residual terms are separate. The core term (v − 21 )2 + γv equals
(v − vγ )2 plus a constant, so the core optimizer is vγ = (1 − γ)/2 ∈ [ 14 , 34 ], the projected growth is
one, and ∂vv Fγ = 2. The residual objective Φ does not depend on γ, so the unique optimizer is
(vγ , x̂) on every draw. Given an instance of either problem, construct Φ, compute the law, draw γ,
run the algorithm, and compute a point within distance 1/4 of (vγ , x̂). Its y-coordinate is within
1/4 of x̂y , so comparing it with 12 decides the instance. The answer is correct on every draw, and
the expected running time is polynomial. For the deterministic statement apply the algorithm to Φ
directly. Finally, Square Root Sum is decidable in polynomial time with a PosSLP oracle [2], which
gives the consequences for Square Root Sum also through part (b).

G.3     Proof of proposition 9.11
The atoms of U1,M are −1 + 2j/(M − 1), 0 ≤ j < M . Such an atom is at least 12 exactly when
j ≥ 3(M −1)/4. For a power of two M ≥ 4, 3M/4 is an integer and 3M/4−1 < 3(M −1)/4 ≤ 3M/4,
so the condition is j ≥ 3M/4, which holds for M/4 indices. Each coordinate therefore has probability
1                                    1
4 , and by independence Pr(E) = 16 . The second derivatives of Fγ in the core are ∂vi vi = 1, so L = 1
is valid.
     Part (a). Minimizing v1 z1 + v2 z2 over the two labels gives min{v1 , v2 }, so Vγ has the stated
form. On E each γi > 0, and vi ≥ vi2 on [0, 1], so γ T v ≥ mini γi ∥v∥2 ; with min{v1 , v2 } ≥ 0 this gives
Vγ (v) ≥ ( 12 + mini γi ) ∥v∥2 ≥ ∥v∥2 . Since Vγ (0) = 0, the origin is the unique optimal core, and both
labels have cost zero there.
     Part (b). At (a, 0) with a > 0 the label (1, 0) costs a and (0, 1) costs 0; at (0, b) with b > 0 the
opposite holds. The noise term is common to both labels and cancels from these comparisons. So

                                                    177
each label is strictly worse than the other at some point of the box, and no valid certificate can
assert that one label is optimal throughout.
    Part (c). The cell [0, h]2 has the corner 0 with value Vγ (0) = min Vγ , and the incumbent Uj is a
feasible value, hence at least min Vγ . So the lower bound of the cell, at most Vγ (0) − h2 /4, does not
exceed Uj , and the cell is retained whenever its parent is. At level 0 the whole core box is retained,
and induction gives the claim at every level.
    The chain. With m − 1 further stages of two zero-cost parallel arcs, each feasible flow chooses
one arc per stage, so there are 2m flows. The conditional value is unchanged, so parts (a)–(c) hold,
and on E every flow is optimal at the origin. In every box [0, a] × [0, b] the optimal flows at (a, 0)
and at (0, b) use different first-stage arcs, so no single flow is certified on the retained hull at any
level. A strategy that enumerates all flows when this certificate fails does at least 2m work on E,
and its expected work is at least 2m /16. The network has 2m unit-capacity arcs and m + 1 nodes,
so its encoding has length O(m log m).
